Ultracold lithium atoms reveal a hidden struggle between quantum magnetic forces
Ultracold lithium atoms reveal a hidden struggle between quantum magnetic forces
What happens when three different kinds of particles interact, and each pair behaves differently?

Physicists have now recreated this situation in the laboratory, building a three-component quantum system that displays competing magnetic correlations.

The achievement could help investigate some of physics’ hardest problems, including high-temperature superconductivity and the interactions governing particles inside atomic nuclei.

Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Cognitive Wellness Journal
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

How researchers built a three-component quantum system
The traditional Hubbard model describes interacting electrons using two possible spin states: spin-up and spin-down. Despite its relatively simple mathematical structure, it remains notoriously difficult to solve.

Adding a third component could reveal new quantum behaviors, but three-body collisions can cause atoms to disappear from the system, making experiments difficult to sustain.

A team of international researchers overcame this challenge using ultracold lithium-6 atoms. The researchers placed them in an optical lattice, an artificial crystal created with laser light that provides regularly spaced positions for atoms.

The atoms can tunnel between neighboring sites, effectively moving from one position to another. Instead of using two, the team employed three different internal quantum states of lithium-6 to represent distinct fermionic components.

Related video: Quantum discovery could lead to far faster electronics (WooGlobe)

By adjusting the magnetic field, they could independently tune the interactions between each pair, giving them control over three different interaction strengths.

Watching competing quantum behaviors
To observe these interactions, the researchers developed a flavor-resolved quantum gas microscope that could distinguish the three components and locate individual atoms. This allowed them to examine how the particles occupied lattice sites and interacted.

One important target was a Mott insulating state, in which strong interactions suppress multiple occupancy of lattice sites and restrict particle movement. The researchers identified this behavior by measuring fluctuations in atomic occupancy and the system’s response to changes in density.

“It was particularly important to us to observe a so-called Mott state, in which the interactions suppress the double occupation of sites,” Peter Schauss, one of the study authors and a physics professor at the University of Virginia, said.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - 750 MHz HDAWG - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - 750 MHz HDAWG - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The experiment revealed competing quantum behaviors. Depending on interaction strengths, some pairs of components displayed ferromagnetic correlations, while others showed antiferromagnetic correlations.

The researchers also measured triply occupied sites, where all three components shared a location. These measurements agreed with numerical calculations performed by collaborators at Rice University.

A three-flavor system could bring quark physics into the lab
The findings could help physicists investigate questions that conventional calculations have struggled to answer. Kaden Hazzard, a Rice University associate professor and co-author, explained:

“Although the Hubbard model is important and simple to write down, it isn’t easy to solve. Theorists have been banging our heads on the two-component Hubbard model for decades, yet there are still major open questions. Adding a third tunable component lets us see how the two-component model reacts to it, potentially helping us answer these major questions,”

This Popular Drink Is Now Linked to Better Mental Clarity for 10 Out Of 11 Seniors!
This Popular Drink Is Now Linked to Better Mental Clarity for 10 Out Of 11 Seniors!
Brain Fog Magazine
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

He also highlighted the relationship between experiments and theoretical research:

“The experiments are a wonderful testing ground for theoretical ideas. Sohail and I were able to compare sophisticated numerical calculations to the experiments, which are already pushed to the boundaries of our capabilities. These comparisons will feed back into helping us refine our theoretical tools. It’s an exciting and fast-developing time.”

Three-body collisions still cause atom losses, while heating makes it difficult to reach the low temperatures needed to explore more delicate quantum phases. Nevertheless, the system proved surprisingly stable\

“It turned out that the experimental realization is surprisingly stable,” Schauss said. “Usually, one fears that three-body collisions could quickly destroy the system. Despite this potential danger, we were able to successfully carry out the measurements and observe a Mott insulator,” Schauss said:

The researchers plan to refine the interactions, investigate a possible paired Mott state and explore triangular and kagome lattices. Ultimately, the system could help simulate aspects of quantum chromodynamics, which describes interactions between quarks inside protons and neutrons.

Its three components resemble the three color charges of quarks, while the platform could also shed light on interactions behind high-temperature superconductivity.

The study is published in the journal Science.

Scientists built a virtual version of Earth where life never existed, and there’s a good reason why
Story by Sarah Jones • 11h • 3 min read


Credit: Wikimedia Commons | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: Wikimedia Commons | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
Scientists at the University of Washington have created a virtual version of Earth with one big difference: there’s no life on it. The simulation follows 5 billion years of planetary evolution, helping researchers figure out which atmospheric signals might point to living organisms on distant worlds.

Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Cognitive Wellness Journal
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Astronomers have already discovered more than 6,000 exoplanets outside our solar system. Some are rocky worlds that could have the right conditions for liquid water, one of the main ingredients scientists look for when searching for life. But finding a planet where life could survive is very different from finding one where life actually exists.

And that’s where things get tricky, a planet’s atmosphere can contain gases associated with living organisms, but those same gases can also come from geological activity or other natural processes. To tell the difference, researchers first need to understand what a planet might look like if life never appeared.

What If Earth Had No Life?
In a study published on September 18 in The Planetary Science Journal, researchers described a computer model that follows Earth’s evolution over 5 billion years. It starts with the planet’s formation around 4.5 billion years ago and continues roughly 500 million years into the future. The simulation tracks how chemicals move between Earth’s core, mantle, crust, surface and atmosphere, showing how these different systems interact and shape the planet’s environment over time.

Related video: Watch what scientists found beyond our solar system - it wasn't supposed to exist (The Space Race)

The researchers tested their model against 19 known planetary properties, including heat flow through the mantle, and found that it could reproduce them. The project builds on earlier work from the laboratory of Rory Barnes, an astronomy professor at the University of Washington. Those previous models already included Earth’s interior and the effects of radiation from its star, but left out the surface and atmosphere.


A diagram of the main processes in the lifeless Earth model. Credit: The Planetary Science Journal
A diagram of the main processes in the lifeless Earth model. Credit: The Planetary Science Journal
© Daily Galaxy US
Samantha Gilbert-Janizek, who led the new study while completing her doctorate, added those missing pieces to create a more complete picture of how a rocky planet evolves. By bringing the planet’s interior, surface and atmosphere together, the researchers developed a model that can also be adapted to study other rocky worlds.

A Planet That Looks Alive May Not Be
On Earth, living organisms continually release these gases into the atmosphere. Since they react with each other and are gradually destroyed, finding both together can suggest that something is constantly replacing them. For astronomers studying another planet, that combination could be a sign worth investigating.

Aplanet’s interior can release gases, while radiation from its star can change atmospheric chemistry. So a world with interesting atmospheric signals isn’t necessarily home to living organisms.

“This model can help us see how habitable but non-living planets might mimic living planets,” Gilbert-Janizek said in the University of Washington announcement.

The new simulation gives astronomers a way to test that possibility. Researchers can enter information about a rocky exoplanet and see whether the model produces an atmosphere similar to the one they’ve observed, without adding life to the equation. If the results don’t match, that planet becomes especially interesting. As Gilbert-Janizek explained, the goal is to better determine the probability that atmospheric observations indicate life on the surface.

Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information

Which Planets Are Worth Exploring?
According to the University of Washington team, the simulation can be adjusted to study different rocky exoplanets, making it useful for interpreting atmospheric observations from distant worlds. One project that could benefit is NASA’s proposed Habitable Worlds Observatory, a future space telescope intended to study potentially habitable planets.

The model’s ability to reproduce Earth’s properties gives researchers a starting point for exploring how other planets might behave. Barnes said the achievement follows more than a decade of work by multiple scientists. For the first time in this research effort, the model simultaneously reproduces properties of Earth’s interior, surface and atmosphere.

“After more than a decade of work by many scientists, Dr. Gilbert-Janizek has finally completed a model of the Earth that simultaneously reproduces properties of Earth’s core, mantle, surface and atmosphere. That calibration gives us confidence that future simulations of exoplanets will be accurate,” he explained.


A diagram showing how carbon, water, and geological processes interact in Earth’s lifeless simulation. Credit: The Planetary Science Journal
A diagram showing how carbon, water, and geological processes interact in Earth’s lifeless simulation. Credit: The Planetary Science Journal

The virtual Earth gives scientists a new way to search for extraterrestrial life by showing what a planet’s atmosphere could look like without living organisms.

Examining meme comparing Hegseth's speech backdrop to Hitler's eagle imagery
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 1mo • 6 min read


Illustration features side-by-side image of U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth standing in front of an eagle symbol bearing 
Illustration features side-by-side image of U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth standing in front of an eagle symbol bearing

The claim that an image of U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth standing in front of an eagle emblem with his arms raised closely resembles a real image of Nazi leader Adolf Hitler is unfounded. 
The image of Hitler has strong indications of artificial intelligence generation. The image of Hegseth also has signs pointing to AI editing, although real video footage from an Oct. 3 speech authentically shows that the changes were minor and that Hegseth did raise his arms in front of a stylized version of the Department of Defense's eagle seal. 
The eagle emblem in the image of Hitler matches the "Reichsadler" symbol Hitler's Nazi regime used — but it's worth noting that the emblem was a highly stylized version of an eagle symbol used in Germany both before and after the Nazi Party came to power. The DOD has since 1949 used a seal featuring an eagle holding arrows above an olive branch; the version on display during Hegseth's Oct. 3 speech omitted the olive branch and had a more stylized design. 
We've reached out to the DOD to ask about the reason for the redesign and whether any similarities to the Reichsadler were intentional. The DOD denied any similarities existed. 
On Oct. 3, U.S. Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth delivered a speech at the U.S. Air Force Academy. Images from the speech of Hegseth with his arms spread wide in front of the Department of Defense's eagle seal went viral, drawing comparisons to Nazi leader Adolf Hitler and an official Nazi symbol. 

Snopes readers shared the images with us, asking to confirm whether such comparisons were accurate. One Facebook meme showed Hegseth in front of a simplified version of the DOD's eagle seal without its traditional olive branch. Next to him, in a similar pose with his arms outstretched is Hitler in front of the "Reichsadler," the eagle emblem of Nazi Germany. The accompanying caption stated: 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

On Saturday, Hegseth traveled to the U.S. Air Force Academy in Colorado Springs to address the cadets.

As he walked on stage, the Department of War seal was visible behind him and on the podium in front of him.

Before he began, he raised both arms out to the side and told the cadets to take their seats.

That was all it took.

Within hours, critics were posting side-by-side images comparing the staging of the speech to old photos of Adolf Hitler speaking in front of an eagle emblem.

Quantum interference keeps two atoms bound despite enough energy to break apart
Story by Christine Pauli • 8h • 4 min read


Emergence of a Friedrich–Wintgen BIC. Credit: Nature Physics (2026). DOI: 10.1038/s41567-026-03519-6
Emergence of a Friedrich–Wintgen BIC. Credit: Nature Physics (2026). DOI: 10.1038/s41567-026-03519-6
How can something remain intact when it should naturally break apart? This question has been addressed by researchers at RPTU. Their work has led to the experimental observation of an unusual quantum state in which two atoms remain bound together even though they possess enough energy to separate. The results have been published in the journal Nature Physics.


Researchers led by professor Artur Widera (left) and Alexander Guthmann have provided the first experimental evidence of an extraordinary quantum state. Credit: Lena Hartmann / RPTU
Researchers led by professor Artur Widera (left) and Alexander Guthmann have provided the first experimental evidence of an extraordinary quantum state. Credit: Lena Hartmann / RPTU
Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

"What is remarkable is that the molecular state could decay from an energetic point of view, yet quantum interference suppresses this decay," explains professor Artur Widera, head of the Individual Quantum Systems group at RPTU. "The wave nature of matter allows a state to remain bound even though we would normally expect it to decay."

Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Cognitive Wellness Journal
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In simple terms, particles such as atoms do not behave solely like tiny objects. They also possess wavelike properties. When such waves overlap, they can either reinforce or cancel one another, a phenomenon physicists refer to as interference. As the new study demonstrates, it is precisely this effect that prevents two atoms from separating.


Rapidly oscillating magnetic fields enable researchers to precisely control interactions between atoms in an ultracold gas, making unusual quantum states experimentally accessible. Credit: Alexander Guthmann / RPTU
Rapidly oscillating magnetic fields enable researchers to precisely control interactions between atoms in an ultracold gas, making unusual quantum states experimentally accessible. Credit: Alexander Guthmann / RPTU
Bound despite being able to decay
Under ordinary circumstances, energy is required to separate the atoms that make up a molecule. Below a certain energy threshold, the atoms remain bound together. Above that threshold, they can dissociate.

In the quantum world, however, the rules are not always so straightforward. The RPTU team has experimentally demonstrated a so-called bound state in the continuum. Physicists use this term to describe a state whose energy would ordinarily be sufficient for the constituent atoms to separate. Yet despite this possibility, the atoms remain bound.

Related video: This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works (TED)
View on Watch
View on Watch

TED
This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works
Current Time 
0:01
/
Duration 
5:46
0
The phenomenon has long attracted considerable interest in physics. As early as 1985, theorists predicted that such states could emerge under specific conditions. Related effects have since been observed in optical and acoustic systems. However, the new study now provides the first experimental demonstration that the underlying mechanism can be deliberately realized in collisions of ultracold atoms.

A principle also used in noise-canceling headphones
The underlying principle is similar to that employed in active noise-canceling headphones. These devices generate sound waves that selectively cancel unwanted noise.

A comparable process occurs in the RPTU experiment, albeit at the level of individual atoms. A molecule can, in principle, decay into separate atoms through different pathways. Each decay pathway is associated with its own quantum wave. When these quantum waves interfere in just the right way, they can cancel one another out.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Much like two equally strong forces acting in opposite directions, this interference effect blocks the decay process. As a result, the atoms remain bound together even though they possess sufficient energy to separate.

Building on earlier research
The experimental breakthrough was made possible by a method the team had previously introduced in Science Advances in 2025. In that work, the researchers demonstrated how rapidly oscillating magnetic fields can be used to precisely control interactions between atoms in an ultracold gas.

For the present study, the team cooled lithium atoms to less than one millionth of a degree above absolute zero. Under such extreme conditions, the quantum behavior of matter can be observed and manipulated with exceptional precision.

The researchers then brought two molecular resonances into close proximity using carefully tuned magnetic fields. These resonances correspond to states in which atoms interact particularly strongly. This approach enabled the team to create exactly the conditions required to suppress molecular decay.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

To verify the existence of the state, the researchers examined, among other observables, how many atoms were lost from the gas during the experiment. Without the protective interference effect, significantly more atoms would have disappeared from the system. In addition, they monitored the dynamics of the atomic cloud and used radio-frequency spectroscopy to probe the molecular states directly. Together, these measurements provided the characteristic experimental signature of the unusual quantum state.

"Our previous work provided us with a tool that allows us to control how atoms interact," says first author Alexander Guthmann. "Here, we used that control to make two possible decay pathways of a molecule cancel each other out. It is a striking example of how quantum physics can transform an expected loss mechanism into a protective one."

A foundation for future applications
The study not only confirms a decades-old theoretical prediction. It also establishes a new approach for controlling how quantum systems interact with their environment.

The findings are highly relevant in the context of emerging quantum technologies. Quantum systems are regarded as one of the key enabling technologies of the future, with potential applications ranging from highly sensitive sensors for medicine and navigation to secure quantum communication and quantum computers capable of solving certain classes of complex problems far more efficiently than conventional computers.

More information: Alexander Guthmann et al., Bound states in the continuum through quantum interference in ultracold atomic collisions, Nature Physics (2026). DOI: 10.1038/s41567-026-03519-6

Changing-look black hole brightens four years running: Its unusual spectrum could test how spinning black holes ignite
Story by Joseph Blake • 2d • 14 min read


An illustration of a star being devoured by a supermassive black hole
An illustration of a star being devoured by a supermassive black hole
© esa.int
Deep inside a distant galaxy, a supermassive black hole that was silent in every archival spectrum has been waking up — and it has not stopped. New photometric measurements in The Astronomer's Telegram confirm that AT2022aamy, one of the most closely watched "changing-look" black holes discovered in the past decade, has brightened again: reaching g = 17.05, r = 16.17, and i = 15.61 magnitudes as of this morning, compared to a g-band reading of 17.21 recorded about 381 days ago by the Zwicky Transient Facility. The object has been brightening continuously since it was first catalogued in 2022 — a four-year arc of ignition playing out on a timescale humans can actually watch.

Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Cognitive Wellness Journal
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

What makes this particular black hole scientifically extraordinary is not just that it is brightening. It is how it is brightening — and what the shape of its light reveals about the machine running underneath.

What Is a "Changing-Look" Black Hole, and Why Does It Matter to You?
For most of the history of observational astronomy, astronomers treated active galactic nuclei — the blazingly luminous cores of galaxies powered by gas falling into supermassive black holes — as essentially stable objects. A nucleus either showed broad emission lines (gas moving at thousands of miles per second around the black hole, labeled "Type 1") or it did not (labeled "Type 2"). The prevailing explanation was geometric: Type 2 nuclei were simply Type 1s seen edge-on, with the inner regions hidden behind a doughnut-shaped curtain of dust. The black hole had not changed; the view had.

Changing-look AGN — CLAGNs, in the literature — broke that model. These are galactic nuclei whose broad emission lines appear or disappear on timescales of months to years — timescales so short that no motion of a dust torus could explain them. The changing is real. The black hole itself is switching states.

Related video: Gray hole (Q-star) could mimic a black hole, letting some light escape (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Gray hole (Q-star) could mimic a black hole, letting some light escape
Holes are everywhere.
Current Time 
0:01
/
Duration 
10:27
0
Astronomers now recognize two distinct flavors of changing-look event. In the first, a column of gas and dust drifts across the line of sight, temporarily screening the inner region — variable obscuration. In the second, more fundamental category, the accretion flow itself changes: the black hole eats more (or less), the disk heats up (or cools down), and the ultraviolet and X-ray radiation that "lights up" the surrounding gas cloud either floods the region or vanishes. AT2022aamy belongs to this second, rarer, more revealing category — a turn-on event, in which broad emission lines absent from every earlier spectrum have emerged with real strength over the past four years.

This matters beyond academic astronomy. Supermassive black holes — ranging from a million to several billion times the mass of the Sun — sit at the center of nearly every large galaxy, including our own Milky Way. How they transition between dormant and active states shapes the galaxies around them: AGN feedback drives outflows that heat gas, suppress star formation, and regulate the structure of the universe at large. Understanding what flips the switch is not a minor footnote. It is a central question in our understanding of how the cosmos evolves.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - 750 MHz HDAWG - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - 750 MHz HDAWG - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The Double-Peaked Smoking Gun
What sets AT2022aamy apart from other brightening black holes is the specific shape of its newly emerged emission lines.

When the Transient Name Server first flagged this object, the spectrum showed something unusual: double-peaked broad H-alpha and H-beta emission lines — a profile with two distinct humps rather than a single broad mound. That shape is not generic. It carries a precise physical meaning.

In a simplified picture of an accretion disk, gas on one side of the disk is moving toward us (blueshifted) while gas on the opposite side is receding (redshifted). When the emission comes directly from the outer disk itself — rather than from a diffuse cloud of gas far removed from the disk plane — both Doppler-shifted populations produce light simultaneously, and the combined profile shows the characteristic twin-peaked structure. Studies of such profiles in SDSS-selected AGN have confirmed they are consistent with accretion disks viewed at inclinations below roughly 50 degrees, with emissivity patterns consistent with photoionized disk atmospheres.

Hi3D | AI 3D Model Generator
Hi3D | AI 3D Model Generator

call to action icon

Crucially, the double-peaked structure also carries information about the black hole's current feeding state. Research into strongly variable AGN has shown that broad-line profiles tend to show double-peak morphology in the low-Eddington-ratio regime — when the black hole is feeding well below its theoretical maximum rate — and transition toward a single-peaked profile as the feeding rate rises. Seeing a double-peaked profile emerge during the early stages of a turn-on event tells astronomers that the emission is coming from the outer accretion disk itself, not from a spherical halo of gas, and that the disk is at an inclination where both approaching and receding sides are visible.

Here is where the story gets genuinely exciting — and where this observation may point toward something much larger.

A theoretical paper published earlier this year proposed that at least some changing-look events are driven not by a gradual increase in the accretion rate, but by a process called disk tearing: when a tilted accretion disk orbits a rapidly spinning black hole, the black hole's rotation distorts spacetime around it (a phenomenon called Lense-Thirring precession, or frame dragging), torquing the inner and outer disk at different rates until the disk literally tears apart into discrete, independently precessing rings. These rings collide, cancel angular momentum, and dump material onto the black hole in sudden surges — producing order-of-magnitude swings in luminosity on the same months-to-years timescales seen in changing-look events.

The disk-tearing model makes a specific, testable prediction: as the independent rings precess at different rates, the BLR illumination pattern rotates, and the red and blue peaks of a double-peaked emission line should show evolving asymmetries — the red peak brightening relative to the blue, or vice versa, over weeks to months as the geometry shifts.

AT2022aamy, with its double-peaked Balmer profile emerging during an active brightening phase, is now a live candidate for exactly this test. If spectroscopic follow-up over the coming months reveals a time-varying red-to-blue asymmetry in the double-peaked profile, it would be the first real-time observational fingerprint of disk tearing as a CLAGN driver — and potentially the most direct evidence yet that the Lense-Thirring effect is shaping how supermassive black holes turn on.

The double-peaked profile is not merely a marker of disk geometry. It is specifically the shape a disk-tearing scenario predicts will evolve asymmetrically, making it a potential discriminator between models in a way that total brightness alone cannot achieve.

China's World-Class New Observatory Is Watching
The new measurements that prompted today's alert were carried out by researchers Bing Lyu, Ruixiang Hu, and Bei You of Wuhan University, using an instrument called MATCH — the Multi-mode Autonomous Terminal for Compatible Hybrid-optics — mounted on the Wuhan University 1-meter optical telescope.

MATCH is equipped with a large-format QHY411 CMOS camera: a 14,304 × 10,748 pixel array with 3.76-micrometer pixels, actively cooled to –30°C (–22°F). That deep cooling delivers readout noise of just 1.0 to 3.0 electrons per pixel and a dark current of only 0.0011 electrons per pixel per second — noise characteristics that make precise differential photometry of faint nuclear transients possible even against the bright background glow of a host galaxy.

That host-galaxy background is exactly what makes this measurement technically demanding. To isolate the light coming specifically from the black hole rather than from the surrounding galaxy's stars, the team employed image-subtraction photometry: subtracting a reference image of the galaxy's starlight from the new frame, leaving behind only the variable nuclear emission. The two columns in today's photometry table — differential magnitude and image-subtraction magnitude — represent two independent ways of quantifying the same brightening:

Band	MJD	Differential Magnitude	Image-Subtraction Magnitude
g	61350.629	17.048 ± 0.026	16.855 ± 0.077
r	61350.630	16.171 ± 0.015	16.211 ± 0.045
i	61350.631	15.612 ± 0.027	15.697 ± 0.035
MJD = Modified Julian Date, an astronomical timekeeping convention. MJD 61350 corresponds to November 7, 2026.

The g-band value of 17.05 is notably brighter than the ZTF reference measurement of 17.21 ± 0.02 mag from approximately 381 days earlier — a brightening of roughly 0.16 magnitudes in g-band. The r- and i-band readings (16.17 and 15.61, respectively) indicate an increasingly red spectral slope consistent with growing thermal emission from hot accreting gas.

The telescope's home — the Lenghu Observatory on Saishiteng Mountain in Qinghai Province, China — is one of the most consequential emerging astronomical sites in the world. Perched at 4,200 to 4,500 meters (13,800 to 14,800 feet) above sea level on the Tibetan Plateau, Lenghu was assessed in a three-year study in Nature as being on par with the world's premier observing sites, including Cerro Paranal in Chile and Mauna Kea in Hawaii. Its median seeing of approximately 0.75 arcseconds (a measure of atmospheric blurring), its approximately 70% fraction of photometric nights, and its precipitable water vapor below 2 millimeters on more than half of all nights place it among the best in the world for high-precision optical astronomy.

Critically, Lenghu sits at a longitude — roughly 93°E — that fills a gap in the global network of elite observatories. When Mauna Kea goes dark at dawn and European sites have not yet reached night, Lenghu can keep watching. For a slowly evolving transient like AT2022aamy, that continuous longitudinal coverage is not incidental. It is the difference between catching a spectral evolution over days and missing it.

Why This Brightening Is Happening: Three Competing Theories
The physical mechanism behind turn-on CLAGN events remains one of the most contested problems in observational high-energy astrophysics. Three frameworks are currently competing in the literature.

The accretion-rate model is the most intuitive. Sudden increases in the mass flow reaching the central black hole push the inner accretion disk inward, dramatically increasing the production of ionizing ultraviolet and X-ray photons — the specific radiation that excites the BLR into emitting bright broad lines. Research linking X-ray luminosity and broad-line flux in CLAGNs supports a picture where the system occupies a critical transition zone near a bolometric Eddington ratio of roughly 1%, where even modest changes in mass flow produce large changes in BLR emission. Work on ZTF18abuamgo — a comparable changing-look AGN studied this year in MNRAS — directly confirmed this picture: the Type 1.5 to Type 1.2 transition over approximately four years was driven by a rise in Eddington ratio from 0.032 to 0.08, with no significant change in obscuration. Broad-line region electron temperatures around 11,800–11,900 K were measured at peak — a data point now within reach for AT2022aamy through its rising Balmer emission.

The accretion-mode transition model goes one step deeper. When the accretion rate rises above a critical threshold — typically near 1% of the Eddington rate — the inner disk can transition from a radiatively inefficient, geometrically thick "advection-dominated accretion flow" (ADAF) to a geometrically thin, optically thick disk. In the ADAF state, most of the energy released by infalling gas is swallowed by the black hole rather than radiated away; the BLR sees almost no ionizing photons and stays dark. The transition to a thin disk floods the system with UV and soft X-ray radiation, switching the BLR on. Work from the Shanghai Astronomical Observatory has proposed that size variability of the inner ADAF disk naturally produces the observed changing-look transitions, with the ADAF contracting as the accretion rate climbs.

The disk-tearing model offers the most radical explanation — and, with AT2022aamy's double-peaked profile in hand, the most testable. As described above, a misaligned disk around a rapidly spinning black hole can be torn by Lense-Thirring precession into independently precessing rings, producing sudden luminosity surges on timescales far shorter than the standard viscous timescale. Unlike the first two models, disk tearing predicts not just a brightening but a specific spectral asymmetry in double-peaked broad lines — a time-varying imbalance between the red and blue peaks as the geometry of the torn disk evolves.

For AT2022aamy specifically, the absence of significant X-ray obscuration — if confirmed by follow-up X-ray observations — combined with the infrared continuum tracking the optical brightening, would point firmly toward intrinsic accretion changes rather than a dust screen lifting. That is consistent with both the accretion-rate and accretion-mode models. But only disk tearing predicts the evolving asymmetry. Only spectroscopy can tell them apart.

The Case for Spectroscopic Follow-Up — Now
The Wuhan University team closed their telegram with a direct appeal: "Further spectroscopic observations will continue and are encouraged." For anyone in the time-domain astronomy community, that phrasing has a specific meaning: the source is at a juncture where data taken in the next few weeks will shape how the story develops over the next few years.

What spectroscopy in the coming weeks could reveal:

Line profile evolution: Whether the double-peaked H-alpha and H-beta profiles are sharpening or blurring — a direct probe of disk geometry and illumination changes over time, and the critical measurement for the disk-tearing asymmetry test.
Reverberation lag: Whether the emission-line flux is tracking the continuum rise or lagging behind it. The lag, measurable via reverberation mapping, encodes the physical radius of the BLR: light from the central engine must travel outward to the BLR before the lines respond, so the delay in days corresponds directly to the BLR's size in light-days.
Higher-ionization species: Whether lines like He II λ4686 or [Fe VII] are beginning to emerge — a signal that the central engine has become hot enough to photoionize more exotic gas, constraining the spectral energy distribution of the driving radiation.
BLR temperature: Applying the Boltzmann plot method to the visible Balmer series, as done for ZTF18abuamgo this year, can yield the BLR's electron temperature — connecting this object to the growing empirical sample of well-characterized CLAGNs.
Facilities suited for this follow-up include the Kast Spectrograph at Lick Observatory, ESO's VLT/X-Shooter, the Gemini Multi-Object Spectrograph, and the LAMOST survey telescope — the latter already deployed for systematic CLAGN monitoring in the northern sky. Even small robotic telescopes with low-resolution spectrographs can contribute to tracking evolving line profiles over time.

A Black Hole in a Wider Context
AT2022aamy joins an increasingly well-monitored registry of CLAGNs being tracked by both targeted follow-up and all-sky survey platforms.

The Zwicky Transient Facility (ZTF), operating from the 48-inch Samuel Oschin Schmidt Telescope at Palomar Observatory, California, has become the backbone of the community's ability to catch nuclear transients in the act. Its 576-megapixel camera covers 47 square degrees of sky per exposure — roughly 90 times the area of the full Moon — and scans the entire northern sky every two to three days to limiting magnitudes of approximately g~20.8. That dense time-domain baseline makes it the natural reference against which targeted follow-up measurements, like today's from Lenghu, are compared.

Statistical surveys have begun to characterize CLAGNs as a population. The Sloan Digital Sky Survey V (SDSS-V) CLAGN sample — one of the largest assembled to date — found that roughly 67% of CLAGNs dim while approximately 33% brighten, with a clear preference for low Eddington ratios in the transitioning objects. If changing-look transitions occur in 10% to 20% of AGN over cosmic time, as some estimates suggest, then this is not a curiosity. It is a standard chapter in the life of a supermassive black hole — one that most of them go through, that shapes their host galaxies, and that plays out on timescales short enough for astronomers to watch from start to finish.

AT2022aamy's object designation follows the IAU transient naming convention: the year prefix indicates it was first catalogued in 2022, meaning this brightening arc has been evolving for roughly four years before today's latest measurement. If the trend holds, it may become one of the best-studied slow-rise turn-on CLAGNs in the literature — a long, well-sampled light curve spanning the full arc of AGN ignition.

Frequently Asked Questions
What is a changing-look AGN?
A changing-look active galactic nucleus is a galactic core powered by a supermassive black hole whose spectral appearance changes dramatically over timescales of months to years — specifically, one in which the broad emission lines from fast-moving gas around the black hole appear or disappear. This contradicts the classic model that said AGN types were fixed by viewing angle, and suggests instead that the black hole's accretion flow itself can switch states on observable timescales.

What does the double-peaked emission line profile tell us?
A double-peaked broad emission line profile means the light is coming from two distinct populations of gas moving in opposite directions around the black hole — the approaching and receding sides of the accretion disk — producing a twin-humped spectral shape rather than a single broad peak. This is a marker of emission arising directly from the outer accretion disk, at a relatively low Eddington ratio (the black hole is feeding below its theoretical maximum rate). It is also the specific spectral shape that disk-tearing models predict will evolve asymmetrically over time, making it a discriminating test for competing theories.

What is MJD?
MJD stands for Modified Julian Date, a continuous count of days used by astronomers to avoid ambiguities in calendar dates across time zones. MJD 61350 corresponds to November 7, 2026. The photometry table reports measurements taken at MJD 61350.629–61350.631, meaning within about 15 minutes of each other on the morning of November 7.

Where is the Lenghu Observatory?
Lenghu Observatory is located on Saishiteng Mountain in Qinghai Province, China — on the Tibetan Plateau at an elevation of approximately 4,200 to 4,500 meters (13,800 to 14,800 feet) above sea level. A three-year site characterization published in Nature found its atmospheric conditions comparable to the world's premier observatories. Its location in the Eastern Hemisphere fills a longitudinal gap in global time-domain observing coverage.

What is the Zwicky Transient Facility?
ZTF is a wide-field sky survey operating from the 48-inch Samuel Oschin Schmidt Telescope at Palomar Observatory in California. Its camera covers 47 square degrees per exposure and scans the entire northern sky every two to three days. It is the primary survey instrument detecting and tracking transient events like AT2022aamy, and its measurements provide the baseline against which follow-up observations are compared.

Can amateur astronomers observe AT2022aamy?
AT2022aamy is currently around magnitude 15.6 to 17.1 depending on the band — well within reach of a 10-inch or larger telescope under dark skies with a sensitive camera. However, the scientifically valuable measurements at this stage are spectroscopic (measuring the shape and asymmetry of emission lines), which require a telescope-and-spectrograph combination beyond most amateur setups. Photometric brightness monitoring — tracking how bright the nucleus is over time — can be contributed by well-equipped amateurs with access to calibrated photometry workflows.

What Comes Next
The broader theoretical prize on offer is understanding how a supermassive black hole transitions between its "off" and "on" states on human-observable timescales, and whether the double-peaked disk-emission signature encoded in AT2022aamy's spectrum can help discriminate between three models — disk instability, accretion-mode transition, and disk tearing — that are actively contending for primacy in the literature.

The disk-tearing model's prediction of evolving red-to-blue asymmetry in the double-peaked profile is the most specific, the most falsifiable, and the most consequential. If that asymmetry is detected in spectroscopic follow-up over the next weeks and months, it would not merely confirm one model over another. It would provide the first direct, real-time observational evidence that Lense-Thirring frame dragging — the warping of spacetime by a spinning black hole, a prediction of general relativity — is shaping the structure of an accretion disk in a specific, measurable, astronomical-scale way.

As the MATCH team continues its photometric monitoring from the pristine, high-altitude skies of the Qinghai-Tibetan Plateau, the global AGN community has been put on notice. A black hole that has been waking up for four years is now entering what may be its most informative phase. The science of watching it happen in real time is only just beginning.

Source: ATel #18145 photometry alert

(NewsNation) — A judge has set a June 2028 trial date for Tyler Robinson, the man accused of assassinating conservative political figure Charlie Kirk. 

Judge Tony Graf announced his decision during a hearing Monday, with the trial set to begin June 21, 2028.

“This trial setting and case management order’s intent is to ensure that the constitutional rights of Mr. Robinson and Mrs. Kirk are upheld at every junction," Graf said.

The court has reserved 105 court days for trial and plans to send questionnaires to 3,600 prospective jurors in April 2028 because of extensive publicity surrounding the case.

Robinson’s attorneys sought a 2029 trial date, while Kirk’s widow, Erika Kirk, urged the court to begin the trial in 2027.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Robinson’s attorneys argued they needed time to review tens of thousands of files prosecutors turned over and conduct extensive investigations into Robinson’s life for mitigating factors that could influence sentencing.

Erika Kirk’s attorneys called the defense timeline “absurd” and “grossly out of line” with comparable cases. 

Graf said he rejected Robinson’s request for 2029 saying his attorneys had not shown why they needed another full year to prepare. He acknowledged the large amount of evidence and the challenges of preparing a death penalty case but said the defense had already begun much of its work.

A new twist on Schrödinger’s cat could finally unite quantum mechanics and Einstein's relativity
Story by Joseph Shavit • 15h • 6 min read

A theoretical study applies spontaneous wavefunction collapse to a simplified model of cosmic geometry.
Its modified dynamics can narrow a superposition toward one definite geometry without an outside observer.
A study in the Journal of High Energy Physics provides a possible mechanism, not experimental proof or a complete theory of quantum gravity.
Quantum mechanics allows a system’s state to combine several possibilities. Yet the universe we observe has a definite large-scale geometry. Explaining how those descriptions fit together remains a difficult question, especially when the system under discussion includes everything, leaving no outside observer to make a measurement.

Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Memory Experts Have Now Tied This 1 Boxed Cereal to 89% of Forgetfulness Cases
Cognitive Wellness Journal
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

A study published in the Journal of High Energy Physics explores one possible answer. Matteo Carlesso and José Luis Gaona-Reyes, affiliated with the University of Trieste, worked with Lucía Menéndez-Pidal and Mir Faizal to examine spontaneous collapse models in quantum cosmology.

Their proposal changes the mathematical evolution of the quantum state so that it can narrow toward a definite cosmic geometry. It belongs to a research tradition that asks whether quantum mechanics needs an additional physical process to explain definite outcomes. Laboratory cat-like states make the underlying questions especially tangible.

The work does not show that quantum mechanics fails for large objects, or that gravity and quantum theory have finally been unified. Instead, it demonstrates what a particular modification can accomplish within a simplified model. Experiments involving quantum objects remain essential for deciding which proposed changes, if any, nature actually requires.


An artistic reimagining of Schrödinger’s cat pairs antique laboratory instruments with ghosted imagery suggesting quantum superposition, reflecting questions about how definite outcomes emerge from quantum possibilities. (CREDIT: The Brighter Side of News)
An artistic reimagining of Schrödinger’s cat pairs antique laboratory instruments with ghosted imagery suggesting quantum superposition, reflecting questions about how definite outcomes emerge from quantum possibilities. (CREDIT: The Brighter Side of News)
The gap between superposition and a definite outcome
A superposition is more than uncertainty about an object’s hidden condition. Different components of a quantum state can interfere, producing effects that an ordinary statistical mixture cannot. Experiments routinely exploit this behavior. The unresolved issue is how to connect quantum evolution with the definite outcomes recorded in individual measurements.

Related video: Gravity might not work the way we think (New Scientist)

Schrödinger’s cat dramatizes that issue by linking a microscopic event to a macroscopic consequence. It does not establish that a real animal remains visibly both alive and dead until a person looks. The thought experiment exposes questions about applying quantum rules consistently to the detector, the cat and their surroundings.

Decoherence explains how interactions distribute quantum information into a system’s environment, suppressing accessible interference. Preserving molecular entanglement therefore requires careful control of those interactions. However, explaining lost interference and explaining one actual outcome are distinct tasks, whose interpretation remains debated.

Cosmology adds another complication: there is no environment outside the entire universe. Parts of the universe can still decohere through interactions with other parts. The authors seek an additional mechanism that selects a definite global geometry. Broader proposals involving quantum networks illustrate other ways researchers investigate the relationship between quantum behavior, time and gravity.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Building a simplified quantum universe
The researchers began with a highly symmetric universe: spatially flat, homogeneous and isotropic. In plain terms, the model has no preferred location or direction at large scales. Matter takes the form of an idealized fluid. These assumptions make the mathematics manageable while leaving out much of the real universe’s complexity.


Diagram compares two theoretical descriptions of the universe’s quantum state, showing how conventional quantum mechanics preserves a superposition of possible states, while a modified Wheeler-DeWitt equation introduces a mechanism that could collapse the universe’s wavefunction into a single definite state. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
Diagram compares two theoretical descriptions of the universe’s quantum state, showing how conventional quantum mechanics preserves a superposition of possible states, while a modified Wheeler-DeWitt equation introduces a mechanism that could collapse the universe’s wavefunction into a single definite state. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
They then considered a quantum state containing different possible geometries. Here, geometry concerns the universe’s spacetime structure and expansion, rather than planets appearing in multiple places. The question is whether added collapse dynamics can turn that spread of possibilities into a state associated with one definite geometry.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Their starting equation is a form of the Wheeler–DeWitt equation, used in approaches to quantum cosmology. To describe evolution, they choose an internal physical variable as a clock. Research on relational time explores why defining time from within a system is an important issue in its own right.

That choice allows the equation to take a Schrödinger-like form, with a state evolving relative to the selected variable. The model therefore assumes a usable clock rather than deriving time from nothing. Related questions about quantum time remain separate from demonstrating that a collapse process can operate once a clock has been chosen.

Spontaneous collapse changes the dynamics
Spontaneous collapse models add random, nonlinear terms to ordinary quantum evolution. These terms make collapse part of the physical dynamics, rather than something triggered by a special measuring device. In this paper, the added process acts continuously and randomly. It is not a sequence of collapses occurring at regular intervals.

The team chose a collapse operator proportional to the Hamiltonian, the operator governing the system’s dynamics. This is a modeling choice, not an experimentally established law. Its role is to specify which property the random process makes increasingly definite and how strongly the state responds to the added noise.


Graph shows how the average value and uncertainty of a quantum variable evolve across 1,000 simulations of a random noise field, illustrating the effects of stochastic fluctuations on a theoretical model of wavefunction collapse. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
Graph shows how the average value and uncertainty of a quantum variable evolve across 1,000 simulations of a random noise field, illustrating the effects of stochastic fluctuations on a theoretical model of wavefunction collapse. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
Within the model, uncertainty in the selected quantity decreases as evolution proceeds. Individual simulated histories head toward different outcomes, depending on the noise realization. The authors also show that narrowing can be slow. The result concerns an approach toward localization, rather than an instantaneous cosmic event established by observation.

“Our model describes a quantum universe that eventually collapses, becoming effectively classical,” Carlesso explained. Here, collapse means localization of a quantum state, not a universe shrinking under gravity. Other work on collapse dynamics and levitated particles explores different theoretical or experimental aspects of these foundational questions.

A definite constant is not a predicted value
The researchers also applied their framework to parametrised unimodular gravity, an alternative formulation of gravitational dynamics. In that description, the quantity selected by collapse can be interpreted as the cosmological constant. This constant is associated with a familiar description of dark energy and the universe’s accelerated expansion.

The proposal offers a way for one definite value to emerge from a quantum spread of possible values. That is narrower than predicting the particular small value inferred from observations. The calculation does not establish that nature selects the measured value, or resolve every aspect of the cosmological constant problem.

Measurements of dark energy and cosmic expansion address the universe we actually observe. The collapse study instead asks how a definite parameter could arise in a quantum description. Connecting those tasks requires more than showing that a parameter’s uncertainty can decrease mathematically.


Graphs compare two mathematical solutions describing the evolution of a quantum variable, showing how an approximate model predicts a rapid reduction in uncertainty, while the exact solution indicates that the quantum state becomes localized over a much longer period. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
Graphs compare two mathematical solutions describing the evolution of a quantum variable, showing how an approximate model predicts a rapid reduction in uncertainty, while the exact solution indicates that the quantum state becomes localized over a much longer period. (CREDIT: Matteo Carlesso et al, Journal of High Energy Physics)
The paper also acknowledges that its framework does not necessarily solve quantum gravity’s problem of time. Its conclusions depend on having a suitable clock variable. Extending the approach to other models may broaden its usefulness, but would not itself establish that spontaneous collapse occurs in the real universe.

Practical implications for testing quantum theory
The immediate value is a possible connection between collapse dynamics and cosmology. The model can describe a transition without an external observer. It does not predict a distinctive late-universe signal that separates its outcome from an already classical universe. Researchers also cannot rerun cosmic history to compare many outcomes directly.

Repeatable laboratory experiments offer a different route. Collapse models can produce small departures from standard quantum predictions, depending on their details. Researchers look for effects involving interference, motion, heating or radiation. Work with atom interference and quantum sensors illustrates the broader effort to make delicate quantum behavior measurable.

The 2024 paper did not conduct those tests or establish a new experimental detection. Testing a specific collapse prescription requires identifying its predictions and separating them from ordinary noise. Existing experimental constraints on other collapse models cannot automatically be treated as confirmation or rejection of this cosmological proposal.

For now, spontaneous collapse remains a hypothesis. The study shows how it could help a simplified quantum universe acquire a definite geometry. Its lasting contribution is a concrete framework that researchers can examine, extend and challenge, while keeping the distinction between mathematical possibility and demonstrated physics clear.

Dig deeper into quantum collapse and classicality
These resources cover experimental superposition tests, collapse-model constraints and the role of decoherence, listed newest first.

Optical experiment demonstrates the strong Mpemba effect in a single qubit
Story by Ingrid Fadelli • 16h • 5 min read


An artistic impression of the quantum strong Mpemba effect. Two luminous paths approach a shared equilibrium state: the winding blue path evokes slow relaxation associated with quantum coherence, while the direct golden path represents faster relaxation when the initial coherence is removed. The transparent sphere suggests the state space of a single quantum bit, and the optical motifs reflect the photonic platform used to demonstrate the effect. Credit: Xiang Zhan and Peng Xue, Southeast University.
An artistic impression of the quantum strong Mpemba effect. Two luminous paths approach a shared equilibrium state: the winding blue path evokes slow relaxation associated with quantum coherence, while the direct golden path represents faster relaxation when the initial coherence is removed. The transparent sphere suggests the state space of a single quantum bit, and the optical motifs reflect the photonic platform used to demonstrate the effect. Credit: Xiang Zhan and Peng Xue, Southeast University.
Typically, after a physical system is disturbed, it can gradually return toward a state of thermal equilibrium by exchanging heat with its surroundings. Thermal equilibrium is a state in which there is no net transfer of heat between the system and its surroundings.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

One may assume that the further a disturbed system is from equilibrium, the longer it will take for it to settle. Under some conditions, however, the opposite occurs, and systems that start farther from equilibrium approach it faster than those that start closer. This counterintuitive phenomenon is known as the Mpemba effect.

In some cases, the Mpemba effect occurs because different components of a system's initial disturbance fade at different rates, and if a system's starting state does not contain the slowest-decaying components, it can approach equilibrium faster. This is the main idea behind the so-called strong Mpemba effect.

Researchers at Southeast University recently ran an optical experiment that demonstrated this effect in a single qubit, a unit of quantum information that can be in a superposition of two states. Their observations, outlined in a paper published in Physical Review Letters, offer new insight into how the strong Mpemba effect could shape a system's journey back to equilibrium.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Easy Measurement Automation - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Easy Measurement Automation - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"Among the various mechanisms proposed for the Mpemba effect, the 'strong' Mpemba effect offers the conceptually most transparent one: relaxation is exponentially accelerated when the initial state has no overlap with the slowest decay mode," Peng Xue, senior author of the paper, told Phys.org. "However, an obvious problem immediately arises. This mechanism is commonly believed to require a unique slowest decay mode."

Simulating thermal relaxation with light
A theoretical model introduced by researchers at Trinity College Dublin and Ludwig-Maximilians University Munich in 2024 suggests that in the dynamics described by the Davies map, several modes can share the slowest decay rate, and all these modes involve the loss of quantum coherence.

The Davies map is a mathematical rule describing how a quantum system changes over time when interacting weakly with its environment at a fixed temperature.

In more technical terms, the theory suggests that for the so-called Davies map, the slowest degenerate modes are purely coherent. This means that if a qubit starts without coherence between its energy levels, it could skip these slow processes.

"Based on this theory, removing coherence from the initial state could restore the effect," said Xue. "Inspired by this, we aimed to demonstrate the mechanism experimentally in the minimal quantum system—a single qubit—and to identify the precise role of quantum coherence."

In their experiment, Xue and their colleagues simulated a qubit approaching a state of thermal equilibrium using a single photon (i.e., a particle of light). They encoded the qubit in the photon's polarization, variations in how its light field oscillates, while the different paths the photon could take served as the ancillas. In this context, the ancillas are additional components used to simulate the qubit's interactions with its environment.

"The Davies map was decomposed into a purely dissipative step plus a simple rotation, built from standard linear optics," explained Xue. "The temperature and evolution time are encoded directly into wave-plate angles, so scanning the relaxation only requires rotating wave plates, with no circuit reconfiguration."

The team prepared their qubit in two distinct starting states. In one of these states, an energy eigenstate, the qubit had a definite energy and no quantum coherence between its energy levels, which allowed it to bypass the slowest stages of its return to thermal equilibrium.

In the other state, the qubit was in a superposition of two different energy states and started its relaxation with coherence. As a result, the slow processes still influenced its journey toward equilibrium.

"We measured these states' evolution using a technique called quantum state tomography and tracked their nonequilibrium free energies," said Xue. "Finally, we verified that the simulator faithfully implements the Davies map via the semigroup property."

A route to test how quantum systems reach equilibrium
The researchers found that the qubit starting in the energy eigenstate approached thermal equilibrium faster than the one starting in a superposition of two states, even though the energy eigenstate initially had higher nonequilibrium free energy and was therefore farther from equilibrium. This finding indicates that they successfully observed the quantum strong Mpemba effect in a one-qubit system.

"The nonequilibrium free energies of the two initial states clearly reverse their ordering: the state starting farther from equilibrium relaxes faster," said Xue. "We also achieved an elegant optical design in which the evolution time does not exist physically. Instead, it is encoded entirely into the setting angles of optical elements."

As part of their experiments, the researchers also assessed how accurately their optical setup reproduced the expected system dynamics. To do this, they tested whether applying the same simulated evolution twice in succession yielded the same result as applying it once for twice the simulated duration.

"Although there is no real time in the setup, checking that applying the 'time-t' channel twice reproduces the 'time-2t' channel certifies that the simulated dynamics faithfully realize the Davies map," said Xue. "Moreover, we showed the two roles of coherence here. Removing coherence accelerates relaxation, while coherence also carries nonequilibrium free energy beyond classical populations."

This recent study could contribute to the understanding of the strong Mpemba effect. In the future, it could potentially also inform the development of new quantum thermal machines that leverage the Mpemba effect, faster cooling techniques, advanced methods to prepare quantum states or controllable photonic platforms to study open-system quantum thermodynamics.

"We now plan to extend the study from a single qubit to high-dimensional systems (e.g., lattice modes and quantum walks) and interacting many-body open systems, and to explore mixed initial states," added Xue. "We will also use our modular scheme, which can realize arbitrary trace-preserving quantum channels in linear optics, to study quantum thermodynamics and Mpemba-assisted quantum thermal machines."

Written for you by our author Ingrid Fadelli, edited by Sadie Harley, and fact-checked and reviewed by Robert Egan—this article is the result of careful human work. We rely on readers like you to keep independent science journalism alive. If this reporting matters to you, please consider a donation (especially monthly).

More information: Xiang Zhan et al., Genuine Quantum Strong Mpemba Effect by Eliminating Coherence, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/z9t9-mwgv.

Court records prove Amy Acton accurately described abusive childhood
Story by Jack Izzo • 2w • 7 min read


Dr. Amy Acton, a middle-aged white woman with long, wavy, blonde hair, wearing a white lace blouse and a black blazer, smiling as she is being fitted with a microphone prior to a rally. . Jon Cherry, accessed via Getty Images
Dr. Amy Acton, a middle-aged white woman with long, wavy, blonde hair, wearing a white lace blouse and a black blazer, smiling as she is being fitted with a microphone prior to a rally. . Jon Cherry, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

Ohio gubernatorial candidate Amy Acton's birth mother, Donna Arthur, remained married to Acton's stepfather, Cledith Arthur, until 2004, despite him being charged with raping Acton when she was 12.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

Context:

Cledith Arthur was never tried on the rape charge because he and Donna Arthur fled the state, and Cledith Arthur never returned to face the charges before his death in 2004. Without a conviction, Snopes cannot definitively confirm he raped Acton.

 
In October, less than a month before the U.S. 2026 midterm elections, Ohio's Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy released an advertisement featuring Donna Arthur, the mother of his opponent Dr. Amy Acton. In the advertisement, Arthur claimed that Acton "lies about her past." 

In response, Acton's campaign released a statement from her brother, Philip Stearns, noting that the two had been estranged from their mother for decades. Her stepfather, Cledith Arthur, raped Acton when she was 12, and both she and her brother were removed from Donna and Cledith Arthur's custody after his arrest, according to the statement. Stearns' statement further claimed that Donna Arthur "stayed married to Amy's rapist until he died" in 2004. 



Acton, the former state health department director, has been open when talking about her difficult childhood for years. A 2019 piece about Acton for Youngstown, Ohio's local newspaper The Vindicator described her as "abused, abandoned, neglected," and claimed she lived in "18 or so homes in her first 12 years," including a winter spent living in a tent at a Youngstown campground. That article also mentioned the sexual abuse she suffered at the hands of her stepfather.

Donna Arthur disputed the "living in a tent" claim directly in the pro-Ramaswamy advertisement, but did not mention the abuse allegations. (PolitiFact found she did claim Acton lied about being raped in a 2020 interview with Operation Rescue, an anti-abortion group.) 

Ramaswamy has stood by the ad, and President Donald Trump and Vice President JD Vance have said they support running it. Vance told Republicans to "stop whining" about the ad at a rally.

Hidden world discovered in James Webb telescope image after scientists remove stellar glare
Story by Joanna Thompson • 3d • 2 min read


James Webb Space Telescope observations of the star 51 Eridani seen before (left) and after (right) the team's new model was used to clean up starlight.
James Webb Space Telescope observations of the star 51 Eridani seen before (left) and after (right) the team's new model was used to clean up starlight.
© Rodrigo Ferrer-Chavez.
When it comes to spotting planets orbiting distant stars, the biggest hurdle is often separating the signal from the noise — specifically, trying to distinguish a planet from the stellar glare around it. That's true even for the most sophisticated telescopes, including the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST).

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Now, astronomers have developed a new way to spot planets that were formerly drowned out in a sea of light. Using a combination of modeling and machine learning techniques, they picked out a previously hidden giant exoplanet in data from JWST. The results were published Nov. 4 in the journal Nature.

"Planets are a million times fainter than their stars," study first author Rodrigo Ferrer-Chavez, an astronomer at Northwestern University, said in a statement. "So, to image exoplanets, we need to remove that glare."

Telescopes like JWST use instruments called coronagraphs to block out some of this excess light, similar to the way the moon and a pinhole camera operate during a total solar eclipse. But even the best coronagraphs have limitations — for example, they can't block all the glare, and the telescopes themselves move.

Even the most stable telescopes vibrate almost imperceptibly as they collect light, so the images a telescope produces contain ever-so-slight mismatches compared with what the instrument actually sees. These slight shifts can be enough to obscure even very large exoplanets.

Related video: Possible 'hints' of life found on planet 124 light-years away in James Webb Space Telescope data (Dailymotion)
To address this, Ferrer-Chavez and his team developed a "digital twin" of JWST that virtually recreates how light travels through the telescope's detectors, minus any movement-based imperfections. To fine-tune this digital replica, the researchers used computational models based in real-world physics alongside machine learning techniques similar to those used to train AI. But the authors stressed that this is not an AI model.

"Because we already know the physics, we don't need AI to understand what's happening," study co-author Jason Wang, also an astrophysicist at Northwestern, said in a statement.

The researchers named their approach Physics-based Learning for Imaging Planets, or FLIP.

Using FLIP, the team analyzed data from three stars, including 51 Eridani, which is located around 97 light-years from Earth and was already suspected to have a giant exoplanet orbiting it. Although the planet, called 51 Eridani b, had been detected by ground-based telescopes, it was drowned out by light in previous JWST observations. With FLIP, however, 51 Eridani b popped out from the dataset.

The $24 Tank Replacing Entire Bra Drawers Across America
The $24 Tank Replacing Entire Bra Drawers Across America
Sofuna
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The scientists hope this approach will help astronomers pick out even more hard-to-spot exoplanets, especially ones that look like the planets in our own backyard. In the nearly 35-year history of exoplanet hunting, the vast majority of planets we've found have been radically different from those in our own solar system, partially because larger and more reflective worlds are easier to spot with current telescopes.

"It's not that [solar-system-like planets] don't exist," Ferrer-Chavez said. "It's that we can't see them."

Billionaire tax measure on California ballot defeated
Story by Ann Saphir and Peter Henderson • 34m • 2 min read


FILE PHOTO: US Senator Bernie Sanders (I-VT) attends a rally in support of Proposition 40 and in opposition to Propositions 41 and 42, related to a proposal to tax billionaires, ahead of the midterm elections in Los Angeles, California, US, October 5, 2026. REUTERS/Mike Blake/File Photo
FILE PHOTO: US Senator Bernie Sanders (I-VT) attends a rally in support of Proposition 40 and in opposition to Propositions 41 and 42, related to a proposal to tax billionaires, ahead of the midterm elections in Los Angeles, California, US, October 5, 2026. REUTERS/Mike Blake/File Photo
© Thomson Reuters
By Ann Saphir and Peter Henderson

SAN FRANSISCO, Nov 9 (Reuters) - A billionaire tax that US Senator Bernie Sanders and other progressives championed as an antidote to economic inequality and a potential model for the rest of the country was defeated in California, US media projected on Monday.

Proponents had estimated Prop 40 would raise $100 billion in revenue, most of which would have been earmarked for healthcare programs to offset federal funding cuts designed to pay for tax breaks enacted last year under President Donald Trump's "One Big Beautiful Bill." Those tax breaks, largely benefiting wealthy individuals and corporate interests, passed with Republican-only support in the US Senate.

The failure of the billionaire tax marks a blow to the left wing of the Democratic Party, even as Democrats won decisive majorities in both houses of Congress in a sweeping midterm election rebuke of Trump and his policies. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Supporters of Prop 40, which targeted stocks, bonds and business assets rather than earned income, reasoned it stood a healthy chance of enactment in a state where voter rolls favor Democrats over Republicans by a nearly two-to-one margin.

But California Governor Gavin Newsom, a likely candidate for the Democratic Party presidential nomination in 2028, came out against the measure, arguing that Prop 40 would backfire in the long run by creating an unwelcome climate for upwardly mobile high-tech entrepreneurs.
