2.9K Followers
Scientists have found strange objects in deep space unlike anything seen before and they could help solve a huge mystery in space science
Story by Jamie Middleton • 1d • 3 min read

Astronomers combing through archival data from NASA’s Chandra X-ray Observatory have identified a new class of cosmic objects that behave unlike anything catalogued before – and which could help resolve two long-standing mysteries in astrophysics.

The objects were uncovered in a trove of data collected by Chandra between 1999 and 2017, suggesting more may yet be found.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

‘Hypersoft X-ray sources’ emit unusually low-energy X-rays and intense levels of ultraviolet radiation.

They were identified in six galaxies, including the Andromeda and Pinwheel galaxies.

In total, 84 were discovered by comparing images taken at different X-ray energies; the new objects appeared only in those taken at the lowest energies.

More amazing Chandra observations
Chandra's stellar explosion timelapse
Milky Way's black hole in x-ray
Death Star black hole

Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Chicago/S.C. Mackey et al.; Radio: NRF/SARAO/MeerKAT; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk
Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Chicago/S.C. Mackey et al.; Radio: NRF/SARAO/MeerKAT; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk

What these objects could be – and why they're so weird
"We’ve never encountered a group of objects that act like this," says lead researcher Mustafa Muhibullah of the University of Alabama.

"Of course, the next step was to try to figure out what these things are."

Researchers believe they may be binary systems in which a compact object – potentially a white dwarf, neutron star or black hole – is pulling material from a companion star.

As this material falls towards the compact object, it heats up and produces radiation.


An artist's impression of a white dwarf star. Credit: 7activestudio / Getty Images
An artist's impression of a white dwarf star. Credit: 7activestudio / Getty Images
Their unusual behaviour could help solve two puzzles. The first is Type Ia supernovae, which are used to measure the Universe’s expansion.

The scenarios that trigger them remain uncertain; these new hypersoft X-ray sources could be a candidate.

"If we could find a way to spot these Type Ia supernova explosions before they go off, that would be really important," says co-author Jimmy Irwin.

The second puzzle is the source of radiation that strips electrons from diffuse gas in and around galaxies, a process that helps shape star formation.


Researchers found 84 hypersoft X-ray sources in spiral galaxy M101 (seen here), Messier 31 and four elliptical galaxies. Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk
Researchers found 84 hypersoft X-ray sources in spiral galaxy M101 (seen here), Messier 31 and four elliptical galaxies. Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk
The new objects’ intense ultraviolet output may provide part of that missing radiation.

There are good reasons these objects remained hidden for so long.

Low-energy X-rays are difficult to detect, while ultraviolet radiation is readily absorbed by hydrogen and helium gas between the stars.

"By combing through the Chandra archive, we were able to eliminate what used to be a blind spot for telescopes," says co-author Rosanne Di Stefano.

The team now hope to identify more hypersoft X-ray sources and determine what powers them.


M101 with circles showing seven of the newly-discovered objects. Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk
M101 with circles showing seven of the newly-discovered objects. Credit: X-ray: NASA/CXC/Univ. of Alabama/M. Muhibullah et al.; Optical: NASA/ESA/STScI; Image Processing: NASA/CXC/SAO/N. Wolk
Why this discovery is so exciting
Comment by astronomer Chris Lintott

Dian Triani in Harvard will be looking at these new discoveries with some excitement, having led work only last year that pointed out that many galaxies show signs of harbouring excited gas, despite the absence of a nice, bright active galactic nucleus to do the job.

Your mortgage is due for a review.
Your mortgage is due for a review.
Rocket Mortgage
Rocket Mortgage
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Objects like these seem to provide enough ‘hard’ or high-energy radiation to have an impact on their surroundings. 

More than that, they’re a useful reminder that, even in a time with new telescopes arriving rapidly, there is scientific gold hidden in the archives.

With astronomers getting better at sifting through the data, who knows what might turn up next?

Unwrapping claim about popular candy brands using lab-grown chocolate
Story by Joey Esposito • 1w • 7 min read


A pile of delicious looking chocolate bars. . Natasha Breen/REDA/Universal Images Group via Getty Images
A pile of delicious looking chocolate bars. . Natasha Breen/REDA/Universal Images Group via Getty Images

A rumor circulated in October that some popular chocolate brands have switched to using lab-grown chocolate, also referred to as cell-based chocolate or cultured chocolate.
The rumor stems from information published by a variety of unreliable outlets that cite no sources, such as investment firms giving advice on investing in lab-grown cocoa, holistic food blogs or alternative lifestyle blogs. 
Snopes bases its ratings on direct and verifiable primary evidence. In the absence of such evidence in this instance, we've left this claim unrated. Most of the companies named in examples of the claim have denied using lab-grown cocoa in current products. One has admitted to investing in the research of lab-grown cocoa but not actively using it in products. Another has a product with a sunflower-seed-based cocoa alternative that is different from cultured chocolate. One of the companies has not responded as of this writing. 
Ahead of the candy-fueled Halloween season in October, a rumor spread that some popular chocolate brands have switched to using lab-grown chocolate, also referred to as cell-based chocolate or cultured chocolate.

Cell-based chocolate has been billed as a possible sustainability solution for harvesting cocoa, an industry faced with supply, environmental and labor issues. 

Essentially, the goal of cell-based chocolate is to create an environmentally and ethically sustainable alternative to farming cocoa beans, the fermented seed of the cacao tree that is the basis of making chocolate. 

Critics of such technology claim some of the leading chocolate brands are "quietly pulling off a massive corporate bait-and-switch ... to flood retail shelves with lab-grown chocolate alternatives." One popular social media video asserts that specific brands are "spending billions of dollars into biotech startups that are making the switch from real cocoa to lab-grown," including:

He differs very significantly from the general population: Scientists tested Hitler's DNA and uncovered a rare condition
Story by Kenneth Glad • 1d • 2 min read


“He differs very significantly from the general population”: Scientists tested Hitler’s DNA and uncovered a rare condition
“He differs very significantly from the general population”: Scientists tested Hitler’s DNA and uncovered a rare condition
When Adolf Hitler ended his life in his Berlin bunker in April 1945, his blood soaked into the sofa fabric. An American soldier later sliced away a scrap of that bloodstained upholstery, which ended up preserved inside an American museum. Decades later, modern tools finally made it possible to extract and study that ancient sample.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Professor Turi King from the University of Bath led the investigation, matching the sample against a known living relative of Hitler. That test confirmed the blood was almost certainly his. From there, King and her team mapped the dictator’s genetic code to uncover several unexpected medical markers.

They also addressed an old historical rumor, writes science site Forskning. The analysis turned up no evidence to support the long-standing claim that Hitler had Jewish ancestry.

Rare hormone disorder
Beyond that debunked myth, the genetic map showed a mutation in the PROK2 gene. That specific variation points toward Kallmann syndrome, a rare condition that disrupts hormone production and drops testosterone levels.

The condition often delays puberty and can impair genital development. In some men, it leads to an undescended testicle, a condition noted in a 1923 medical record from Hitler’s time in prison.

Related video: What rare condition made Hitler differ so significantly from the general population, according to new DNA tests? (Dagens.com US)

‘Natural Ozempic’ discovery could slash appetite by half

“Where are they?”: Inside the mission to send a robotic ambassador to the stars

Still, experts stress that the record alone does not prove the diagnosis, nor does it confirm historical speculation about a micropenis. Instead, the biological marker offers fresh context for historians who study his difficult relationships with women.

High psychiatric scores
Researchers in Denmark then tested his DNA against a modern population database. Professor Ditte Demontis from Aarhus University compared Hitler’s profile with genetic data from 37,000 Danish individuals to calculate polygenic risk scores.

Hitler scored within the top one percent for autism, schizophrenia, and bipolar disorder. Not a single person in the 37,000 comparison group scored that high across all three conditions at once.

Even so, Demontis stressed caution. In modern populations, people with those high genetic scores have only a five percent chance of developing those illnesses. Genetic risk never excuses or explains his atrocities.

Is Illinois about to shrink? 45 counties have now approved measures to secede the state — all because of Chicago. Here’s what happens next
Story by Rudro Chakrabarti • 1d • 5 min read


45 Illinois counties eye an exit
45 Illinois counties eye an exit
© Getty Images, Shutterstock
Nearly half of Illinois’ 102 counties have now voted to break away from the state, and the organizers behind the push say the problem is Chicago.

Twelve more downstate counties approved nonbinding secession referendums Nov. 3, bringing the total to 45 since 2020, FOX 32 Chicago reported. Approval ranged from 57% in McDonough County to 90% in one Adams County township, Illinois Separation director Loret Newlin told The Center Square.

The newest additions are Coles, Ford, Gallatin, Hamilton, Henderson, Macoupin, McDonough, Monroe, Piatt, Saline, Warren and Washington counties. Illinois Radio Network counts 44 counties plus 11 Adams County townships — the difference is Ford County, whose question wasn’t part of the organized campaign.

Indiana has spent nearly two years courting these counties with lower taxes, and for some Illinoisans that pitch holds up. For others, especially retirees, it backfires.

Jeff Bezos backs a platform that lets anyone invest in rental homes for as little as $100 — 6 ways to build wealth like a landlord without actually being one
A single line on your car insurance policy could be inflating your premium by up to 30% — here's what to change
Why downstate blames Chicago
Supporters say state lawmakers cater to Chicago and Cook County and overlook the rest of Illinois. “The state legislature tends to not take us into consideration when they’re making their laws,” Newlin told FOX 32. She also said downstate has no voice in how tax dollars are spent.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

But a study from Southern Illinois University’s Paul Simon Public Policy Institute found the money flows the other way. In 2016, southern Illinois got back $2.88 in state spending for every $1 it paid in state taxes, and downstate as a whole got back $1.70. Cook County got back 98 cents and the collar suburbs 60 cents.

The authors credit the state universities, prisons and parks located downstate, and their 2025 update says state spending still transfers some wealth from Cook County and the suburbs to downstate. New Illinois, a separation group, has called the study flawed.

A little bit more than magic: The secret to quantum computing may lie in negativity
Story by Science X staff • 2mo • 4 min read


Credit: University of Cambridge, further adapted with ChatGPT
Credit: University of Cambridge, further adapted with ChatGPT
Quantum computers hold great promise for applications from drug discovery to cybersecurity. Yet figuring out what would give quantum computers their edge over everyday "classical" computers is a subtle problem. A new theoretical study led by researchers at the Cavendish Laboratory shows that quantum computers are harder to make powerful than previously assumed while offering the clearest picture yet of what actually makes them work.

Empower your Customer Support - Generate More Sales - 40% Boost in Conversion Rates
Empower your Customer Support - Generate More Sales - 40% Boost in Conversion Rates
Tidio - Customer Service
Tidio - Customer Service
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

The research, published in Physical Review Letters, helps identify the precise quantum states that are genuinely useful for quantum computation. It also increases the set of quantum calculations known to be easy for classical computers, meaning quantum devices face a higher bar to demonstrate an advantage.

Not all magic states help
At the heart of quantum computing is a kind of "magic." Quantum computers typically work with quantum bits, or qubits: particles such as electrons or atoms that can exist in two states at once. To run algorithms that outperform any classical computer, those qubits must be prepared in special starting configurations known as "magic states." These states act as the computational fuel: Without them, a quantum computer is no better than a conventional machine.

Related video: This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works (TED)
View on Watch
View on Watch

TED
This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works
Current Time 
0:01
/
Duration 
5:46
0
But the new research expands scientists' understanding that not all magic states are equal. Many states that appear "magic" and were previously assumed to be useful turn out to offer no quantum advantage.

By identifying this class of useless magic states, the team redraws the boundary between calculations that need a quantum computer and those that can still be handled classically.

"We're showing that magic is necessary but not sufficient to unlock quantum computers' full power," said Dr. David Arvidsson-Shukur, from the Hitachi Laboratory at the Cavendish Laboratory. "If a quantum state is not magic, you can't get a quantum advantage. But having magic alone doesn't guarantee you have one either. The picture is more nuanced and much more interesting than that."

Dirac's framework sharpens the test
To identify which quantum states have what the team classifies as "useful" magic and which have the "useless" kind, the researchers turned to a mathematical framework developed in Cambridge in 1945 by Paul Dirac, the physicist behind the relativistic quantum equation that predicted antimatter. Working at St John's College, Dirac independently established a distribution similar to one introduced a decade earlier by MIT's John Kirkwood and extended the idea by building the mathematical framework in which to use it. This became known as the Kirkwood–Dirac distribution.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The framework involves a concept that sounds paradoxical: negative probabilities, which the team used to frame the task of quantum computation. And just as Dirac once argued that a negative solution to an equation should be taken seriously (a theory that eventually led to the discovery of antimatter), the team shows that the appearance of negative values in the Kirkwood–Dirac distribution is a meaningful signal.

When that distribution stays entirely positive (or zero) throughout a computation for a given input quantum state, a classical computer can simulate the quantum computation with ease. When it takes negative values, classical simulation becomes exponentially harder and a genuine quantum advantage may exist.

"We're essentially bringing a new ingredient to the magic mix: the Kirkwood–Dirac negativity," said J.J. Thio, lead author of the study and a doctoral student in Professor Crispin Barnes' group at the Cavendish Laboratory and St John's College, where Dirac worked. "The idea builds on probabilities—for example, the odds of getting heads when flipping a coin—but with a twist: They may be negative. And those negative 'probabilities' are needed for the quantum computer to outperform its classical counterpart as well."

Mathematical Foundations Of Trustworthy AI: Theory, Algorithms, And Engineering Principles
Mathematical Foundations Of Trustworthy AI: Theory, Algorithms, And Engineering Principles
Amazon.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"That distribution provides a stricter, more precise framework for mapping which quantum states can be efficiently simulated by classical computers."

A wider classical frontier
To demonstrate their theory, Thio worked with fellow student Rishi Goel to complete a classical simulation program that runs on a standard laptop, performing computations previously thought to require a quantum computer. The result is a direct demonstration that the classical frontier is larger than previously thought.

These findings matter because billions of pounds are being invested in quantum computing by governments and private companies worldwide. Yet there is still genuine scientific uncertainty about when quantum computers will outperform classical machines on tasks of practical value.

By pushing the boundary of what classical computers can do, the Cambridge team is helping establish a clearer, more rigorous threshold for what would constitute a true quantum advantage. "Without this kind of foundational work, we will never know whether a quantum device has achieved something genuinely beyond classical reach," said Arvidsson-Shukur.

The team hopes the results will guide both the design of quantum software and the production of magic states, which remains one of the major engineering challenges in building large-scale quantum computers.

"The better we can identify these useful magic states, the better we can produce and apply them," said Dr. Nicole Yunger Halpern, a fellow of the Joint Center for Quantum Information and Computer Science (Maryland, U.S.). "Some of us have conjectured for years that Kirkwood–Dirac negativity can assist with this goal, and I'm delighted that the group has finally answered affirmatively."

This new work brings the field a step closer to a complete understanding of what gives quantum computers their potential, a question that, despite decades of research, remains open.

More information: Jonathan J. Thio et al, Kirkwood-Dirac Nonpositivity Is a Necessary Resource for Quantum Computing, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/x819-898d

Provided by University of Cambridge

This story was originally published on Phys.org.

How symmetry shapes our universe in physics
In this video, we explore the concept of symmetry, regarded as the root of physics. We delve into the significance of symmetry and its connection to group theory, particularly Noether's Theorem, which links symmetries to conservation laws. The discussion includes U(1) symmetry, the Dirac equation transformation, and how Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) arises from U(1) symmetry. It also clarifies the relationships between U(1), SU(2), and SU(3) symmetries, along with their associated conservation laws and fundamental forces, such as electromagnetic, weak, and strong forces. #gaugesymmetry #grouptheory #noetherstheorem

Arvin Ash

Follow
1mo1 month ago, 9/30/2026, 3:00 PM
The simplest explanation of quantum mechanics in the universe!
Chapters: 0:00 Why do we need Quantum Mechanics? 2:23 What's "weird" about QM? 4:07 What is the Measurement Problem? 6:29 Uncertainty principle explained 8:35 Why don't we see quantum behavior in the macroscopic world? 9:31 Entanglement explained 10:25 What do atoms actually look like? Summary: Classical mechanics predicts electrons should radiate energy and spiral into atomic nuclei. Quantum mechanics resolves this by showing electrons occupy quantized states determined by Planck’s constant. de Broglie proposed matter has wave-like properties; Schrödinger formulated an equation describing those waves, and Max Born interpreted the wave function probabilistically—quantum objects have only probabilities for where they will be found until measured. Quantum objects can produce interference (as in the double-slit experiment) yet are observed as localized particles. Measurement—an interaction typically involving irreversible energy exchange—appears to produce wavefunction "collapse," but how or why collapse occurs is not explained by the theory (the measurement problem). The Schrödinger equation describes the wave’s evolution, which is not itself directly observed. The Uncertainty Principle states that position and momentum cannot be known simultaneously with arbitrary precision; this is a fundamental property, not merely a measurement limitation. All objects have wave-like behavior, but macroscopic wavelengths are effectively negligible (for example, a tennis ball at 10 m/s has a wavelength on the order of 10^(-33) m), so quantum effects aren’t noticeable at everyday scales. Wave-like, nonlocal behavior also produces entanglement—strong correlations between particles. Although historically called "spooky action at a distance," entanglement does not allow faster-than-light communication. Electrons in atoms are better pictured as probability clouds rather than classical circular orbits. Many familiar quantities (energy, momentum, charge, mass—and potentially space and time) appear in discrete quantum units. #quantummechanics #quantumphysics

A gentle squeeze rearranged the magnetic domains in a new class of magnets called altermagnets, Oak Ridge neutron experiments show
Story by Alexander Clark • 9h • 4 min read


A gentle squeeze rearranged the magnetic domains in a new class of magnets called altermagnets, Oak Ridge neutron experiments show
A gentle squeeze rearranged the magnetic domains in a new class of magnets called altermagnets, Oak Ridge neutron experiments show
© Image Credit: JanDerChemiker - CC BY 3.0/Wiki Commons
Pressing a tiny crystal between two titanium plates was enough to shift the magnetic makeup of manganese telluride and iron sulfide, two materials at the center of research on altermagnets. Neutron diffraction at Oak Ridge National Laboratory’s Spallation Neutron Source showed that the strain changed how the crystal’s magnetic domains were populated. The experiments are described in two papers, one in Physical Review X and one in Advanced Materials.

TIDIO Livechat Plugin - Drag-and-Drop Chat Builder - Confirmed by 1961+ Reviews
TIDIO Livechat Plugin - Drag-and-Drop Chat Builder - Confirmed by 1961+ Reviews
Tidio AI Customer Support
Tidio AI Customer Support
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Tech news worth reading: Get Techpresso, the daily email with the most interesting tech news and insights, read by 400,000+ professionals from Google, Apple and OpenAI.

Get Techpresso →

We may earn a commission if you sign up.

Pengcheng Dai of Rice University, a former Oak Ridge neutron scientist and senior author on both, said that controlling the domains gives researchers a direct handle on how electrons move through the material. Feng Ye, an Oak Ridge neutron scientist and co-author on both papers, described the technique as a small mechanical squeeze between two plates that can expose entirely new magnetic behavior.

Altermagnets and the domain problem
An altermagnet is a magnetic state in which neighboring atoms carry opposite spins, so the material has zero net magnetization, yet its electronic bands are split by spin in a way that resembles a ferromagnet. That mix draws interest for spintronics, which uses an electron’s spin as well as its charge to store and process information. ScienceDaily’s version of the story mentions possible links to wireless communication and quantum computing.

Related video: This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works (TED)
View on Watch
View on Watch

TED
This everyday object forced physicists to rethink how matter works
Current Time 
0:02
/
Duration 
5:46
0
The obstacle is domains. A crystal divides into regions with particular magnetic arrangements, and if different domains point different ways their electrical effects can cancel. The Oak Ridge team argues that mechanical strain could be a simple way to favor some domains over others and steer electron flow without changing the chemistry. The ORNL news article by Sumner S. Brown Gibbs appeared on Oct. 27, and ScienceDaily republished the story on Nov. 4.

Dai is a physicist at Rice University who formerly worked as a neutron scientist at Oak Ridge, and the collaboration spans a university and a national laboratory, with Ye on the Oak Ridge side. The Oak Ridge article also names ORNL researchers Douglas L. Abernathy and Matthew B. Stone, and the author lists carry many more names, among them Emilia Morosan, Qimiao Si, Rafael M. Fernandes and Jiun-Haw Chu.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Uniaxial strain, titanium plates and neutron beams
Each experiment compressed a small crystal in one direction, a setup called uniaxial strain. The team used neutron diffraction to look inside, because neutrons carry a magnetic moment and scatter from the arrangement of magnetic moments in a crystal. The Spallation Neutron Source is an accelerator-based facility at Oak Ridge that produces intense pulsed neutron beams as a Department of Energy user facility.

The researchers also measured the anomalous Hall effect, a sideways voltage that appears without an external magnetic field. Applied strain altered that electrical signal, which tied the change in domain populations to a change in how the material conducts. Ye said neutron scattering plays a critical role in revealing the underlying change in magnetism.

The two papers split the materials between them. The Physical Review X paper carries the name “Strain-Tunable Anomalous Hall Effect in Hexagonal MnTe,” and the Advanced Materials paper carries the name “Uniaxial Strain Tuned Magnetism of the Altermagnet Candidate H-FeS.” Iron sulfide is therefore described as a candidate, while manganese telluride is treated as an established example.

Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The force involved is described only as gentle. The ScienceDaily report says the crystals were gently compressed, and the Oak Ridge article calls the effect a small mechanical squeeze The wording points to a modest mechanical input compared with changing a material’s chemistry. The Spallation Neutron Source is a Department of Energy Office of Science user facility, open to outside research groups.

Iron sulfide, Berry curvature and an unsettled explanation
A Rice University release on EurekAlert dated Aug. 13 adds detail on the iron sulfide result. Compressing a hexagonal FeS crystal weakened both its tiny net magnetic moment and its anomalous Hall effect, while the larger magnetic order stayed essentially the same. Neutron scattering showed that pressure shifts which magnetic orientations are most common, though the basic structure holds.

Dai said the two effects shrink together when the crystal is squeezed in one direction, which points to a tight link between them. First author Weiliang Yao added a caution: the team is not saying the electronic explanation is wrong, and the Berry curvature account of the electrical signal was not ruled out. That leaves the mechanism partly open. The data show that strain and the electrical signal move in step, but the physics that ties them together has more than one candidate. On the practical side, Dai said that for any application, control is essential,.

None of the sources provide numbers for the pressure applied, the temperatures used or the size of the Hall signal, so the scale of the effect cannot be judged from the summaries. Those figures sit in the Physical Review X and Advanced Materials papers, whose journal pages could not be loaded for this article. What the Oak Ridge work establishes is the principle: mechanical strain can reorganize the magnetic domains in these materials, and neutrons can watch it happen.

Ramanujan’s genius π formulas from a century ago might help explain the deepest secrets of the universe
Story by Tibi Puiu • 10mo • 6 min read


Credit: Space-Tech, X.
Credit: Space-Tech, X.
© ZME Science
In 1914, Srinivasa Ramanujan arrived at Cambridge with a notebook filled with 17 extraordinary infinite series for 1/π. They were strikingly efficient, producing accurate digits of the world’s most famous irrational number much faster than any technique of the time. For over a century, mathematicians viewed these formulas as a pinnacle of number theory, but they lacked a “physical” explanation for why they worked so well.

TIDIO Livechat Plugin - Generate More Sales - Drag-and-Drop Chat Builder
TIDIO Livechat Plugin - Generate More Sales - Drag-and-Drop Chat Builder
Tidio - Customer Service
Tidio - Customer Service
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Now, researchers at the Indian Institute of Science (IISc) have uncovered a hidden bridge between Ramanujan’s “recipes” for pi and the cutting-edge physics used to describe black holes and turbulent fluids. The study suggests that Ramanujan was intuitively using the same mathematical engine that governs how matter behaves at the edge of a total transformation.

Srinivasa Ramanujan’s life reads like fiction. Born into poverty in southern India and largely cut off from formal education, he taught himself mathematics by obsessively working through whatever books he could find, then pushing far beyond them. His notebooks filled with equations that seemed to arrive whole, without derivation, as if tuned in from some distant frequency.

In his early twenties, he began mailing these results to British mathematicians, most of whom ignored them. One didn’t. British Mathematician G.H. Hardy recognized that the strange, unproved formulas on the page could not be mere accidents. They were too original, too coherent. It was Hardy who brought Ramanujan to Cambridge, where the young mathematician produced a torrent of results before illness forced him back to India, where he died in 1920 at just 32. Ramanujan’s story was turned into the 2015 film The Man Who Knew Infinity.

Seventeen Formulas That Should Not Have Worked

Ramanujan portrait and π infinite series. Credit: Kyuriosity.
Ramanujan portrait and π infinite series. Credit: Kyuriosity.
© ZME Science
In 1914, Srinivasa Ramanujan published 17 formulas for calculating 1/π. Each looked almost magical.

Add just a few terms, and π snaps into focus with uncanny speed. Compared with older methods — such as the long, laborious series dating back to Archimedes — Ramanujan’s expressions converged almost explosively.

Related video: Why the mysterious number 137 defines the fundamental laws of physics (Thomas Mulligan)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Thomas Mulligan
Why the mysterious number 137 defines the fundamental laws of physics
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:57
0
Today, they still power the fastest π calculations on Earth. “Scientists have computed pi up to 200 trillion digits using an algorithm called the Chudnovsky algorithm,” says Aninda Sinha, a physicist at IISc and senior author of the new study. “These algorithms are actually based on Ramanujan’s work.”

Yet Ramanujan offered little explanation for why his formulas worked so well.

For more than a century, mathematicians treated them as isolated marvels. Sinha and his collaborator Faizan Bhat wanted to know whether that mystery pointed somewhere deeper.

“We wanted to see whether the starting point of his formulae fit naturally into some physics,” Sinha says. “In other words, is there a physical world where Ramanujan’s mathematics appears on its own?”

The Physics of Things on the Brink
Their search led to a class of theories with a long name and strange implications: logarithmic conformal field theories.

Physicists use conformal field theories to describe systems at critical points, such as the moments when matter teeters between phases and ordinary notions of scale break down.

Gen AI and LLMs for Dummies - Download the Ebook - Gen AI Training and Tuning
Gen AI and LLMs for Dummies - Download the Ebook - Gen AI Training and Tuning
snowflake.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Water provides a classic example. When water is heated to exactly 374°C under 221 atmospheres of pressure, at this precise moment, the distinction between liquid and vapor vanishes into a “superfluid” state. Zoom in or out, and the system looks statistically the same.

“At the critical point, you cannot actually say which is liquid and which is vapor,” Sinha told The Hindu. “That is the point where CFTs [Conformal field theories] enter: they are used to explain what happens in this kind of critical phenomena.”

Logarithmic versions of these theories describe even stranger systems: percolation (how fluids spread through porous materials), dense polymers, certain quantum Hall states, and the onset of turbulence. They also appear in theoretical descriptions of black holes.

What Bhat and Sinha found is that the mathematical backbone of Ramanujan’s π formulas — the same structure that makes them converge so quickly — also appears inside the equations that define these physical theories.

Pi Hiding in Plain Sight

A bust of Srinivasa Ramanujan in the garden of the Birla Industrial & Technological Museum, Kolkata. Credit: AshLin (CC BY-SA)
A bust of Srinivasa Ramanujan in the garden of the Birla Industrial & Technological Museum, Kolkata. Credit: AshLin (CC BY-SA)
© ZME Science
At the heart of Ramanujan’s work with π series lies a mathematical identity known as the Legendre relation. On its own, it looks abstract and apparently has nothing to do with physics.

But when the IISc team rewrote it using the language of conformal field theory, something unexpected happened. The symbols began to line up with physical quantities: correlation functions, scaling dimensions, and operators that encode how systems fluctuate at criticality.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Easy Measurement Automation
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In their analysis, Ramanujan’s mysterious parameters map directly onto the properties of twist operators — mathematical objects that track how systems behave when boundaries or symmetries are disrupted.

The result is a new computational shortcut.

By borrowing Ramanujan’s strategy — compressing complex behavior into compact expressions — the researchers constructed faster ways to calculate key quantities in logarithmic conformal field theories. In some cases, calculations that normally require summing many contributions collapse to just one.

“Remarkably,” the authors write, the entire answer can emerge from what physicists call the “identity operator” alone.

That kind of simplification hints at a universal property of these theories, something fundamental hiding beneath the equations.

Black Holes, Turbulence, and a Rubber Band
The connection extends even further.

In the study’s appendix, the authors show that the same mathematical structure appears in models of black holes described using holography — a framework where gravity in higher dimensions maps onto quantum physics in lower ones.

In this setting, Ramanujan’s formulas correspond to how disturbances propagate between a black hole’s horizon and the edge of spacetime. The same equations also describe how polymers stretch, how fluids turn turbulent, and how clusters form in percolating materials.

Bhat sees a familiar pattern.

“[In] any piece of beautiful mathematics, you almost always find that there is a physical system which actually mirrors the mathematics,” he says. “Ramanujan’s motivation might have been very mathematical, but without his knowledge, he was also studying black holes, turbulence, percolation, all sorts of things.”

Sinha offers a metaphor drawn from string theory. A string, he explains, behaves like a rubber band: stretch it different ways, and it reveals different properties. Pi, hidden inside those equations, shows up through infinitely many mathematical perspectives.

When Pure Math Waits for Physics

Ramanujan (Center) as a Fellow of Trinity College, Cambridge. Credit: Public Domain.
Ramanujan (Center) as a Fellow of Trinity College, Cambridge. Credit: Public Domain.
© ZME Science
This is not the first time mathematics has anticipated physics by decades.

Riemannian geometry began as a 19th-century abstraction, but Einstein later revealed that spacetime itself obeys its rules. Fourier transforms emerged from studies of heat flow and now underpin digital images, music, and data compression.

Ramanujan’s work fits this pattern uncannily well.

Working largely in isolation in early 20th-century India, with little exposure to modern physics, he stumbled onto structures that now sit at the center of quantum field theory and cosmology.

“We were simply fascinated by the way a genius working in early 20th century India, with almost no contact with modern physics, anticipated structures that are now central to our understanding of the universe,” Sinha says.

Beyond Pi
The researchers are careful not to oversell their results. This work does not solve open problems in number theory or unlock a theory of everything.

But it opens a door.

The same approach could reveal fast-converging formulas for other irrational numbers. It could streamline calculations in theories that model turbulence and critical behavior.

The IISc researchers are already looking toward the next horizon. The same mathematical structure they identified in Ramanujan’s pi series has reappeared in their models of an expanding universe.

It turns out that when we calculate the circumference of a circle, we might be using the same rules that govern the very fabric of the cosmos.

The new findings appeared in the Physical Review Letters.

This story originally appeared on ZME Science. Want to get smarter every day? Subscribe to our newsletter and stay ahead with the latest science news.

Trump said he'd become Israeli prime minister if people ever get tired of me in the US
Story by Laerke Christensen • 5d • 3 min read


U.S. President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a campaign rally at the Rock Hill Sports and Event Center on October 29, 2026, in Rock Hill, South Carolina. . Andrew Harnik, accessed via Getty Images
U.S. President Donald Trump delivers remarks during a campaign rally at the Rock Hill Sports and Event Center on October 29, 2026, in Rock Hill, South Carolina. . Andrew Harnik, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

U.S. President Donald Trump said on Election Day 2026, “If people ever get tired of me in the U.S., I’ll move to Israel, become prime minister.”

Rating:

Correct Attribution (About this rating?)

As polls opened for the 2026 U.S. midterm elections, a claim (archived) circulated online that U.S. President Donald Trump said, "If people ever get tired of me in the U.S., I'll move to Israel, become prime minister."

X user @MarioNafwal shared the alleged quote and wrote, "Well, I think they're already tired of you, Mr. President, but Israelis are tired of Bibi, too."



The claim also circulated on Facebook (archived).

Social media users correctly attributed the quote to Trump, who said it during a phone interview with Fox News (time code 22:20) on Tuesday. 

According to a clip from Fox News' coverage that the network posted online, Trump said the following while speaking about cutting U.S. financial aid to Israel (emphasis ours):

And let me tell you something, if they do need it, if for some reason they do need it or they need help — So, I'm the number one, I was so voted, I'm at 99% approval rating in Israel, can you believe it? In fact, if people ever get tired of me in the U.S., I'll move to Israel, become prime minister. But I'm at 90 — think of it — I'm at 99%, I have the highest approval rating of anybody in history.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

We contacted the White House to ask which approval polling Trump was referring to during his interview and await a reply.

Polling from the Jewish People Policy Institute, an Israel-based think tank that aims to shape strategy and policy on issues affecting Jewish people, published on Nov. 2 found that 73% of Israelis find Trump to be "a better-than-average U.S. president for Israel's interests." That polling also found that 54% of Jewish Israelis said Trump was "one of the best presidents in U.S. history in the Israeli context" and an additional 25% described him as "above average" on the same metric, according to the JPPI.

1.6M Followers
Mystery of hundreds of little red dots in the sky could be finally solved by scientists
Story by Josh Milton • 3h • 4 min read


Getty/Metro
Getty/Metro
Since 2023, scientists have discovered about 300 so-called little red dots looking down at us.

These dots, sometimes called rubies, have been spotted by the James Webb Space Telescope, sometimes only taking up one pixel in photos.

We have no idea what they are for sure and what makes these specks odd is that they’re really, really old. Almost as old as time itself.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Experiment & System Control - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Now researchers from Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore think they have the answer – a never-before-seen type of black hole, according to their paper in Physical Review Letters.

‘They are very compact, red objects, seen as they were within roughly the first billion years after the Big Bang,’ the study’s co-author, Loris Del Grosso, tells Metro.

‘At first they were puzzling because they did not fit either of the obvious explanations.’

Red dots could be cosmic hungry-hungry hippos

Little red dots have appeared in deep-sky surveys (Picture: ASA/ESA/CSA/STScI/Dale Kocevski/Colby College)
Little red dots have appeared in deep-sky surveys (Picture: ASA/ESA/CSA/STScI/Dale Kocevski/Colby College)
Black holes are gargantuan pits of eternally dark nothingness, so dense that their gravity prevents anything, even light, from escaping.

Most are made from the rotting remains of a dead star, drifting through space and sitting at the heart of large galaxies, like our own.

A primordial black hole, however, would have existed back when the universe was a dark, star-less pit.

Related video: We can't look beyond the Big Bang - The cosmic mystery that could hide an entire parent universe beyond our reach (Origins Explained)

Origins Explained
We can't look beyond the Big Bang - The cosmic mystery that could hide an entire parent universe beyond our reach
actually showing us the inside of a black hole? ArmedVideo Player is loading.
‘Primordial ones could have almost any mass, from far lighter than the sun to thousands of times heavier,’ Del Grosso, a postdoctoral researcher, says.

‘They would also already exist when the first galaxies were forming, giving them a head start to grow. That head start is why they could explain how such massive black holes appeared so early.’

Researchers suspected these hungry-hungry relics formed from collapsed parts of the young universe, their tint being clouds of dust sucked inside.

Follow Metro on WhatsApp to be the first to get all the latest news

Follow us to receive the latest news updates from Metro (Picture: Getty Images)
Follow us to receive the latest news updates from Metro (Picture: Getty Images)
© Provided by Metro
Metro’s on Whatsapp! Join our community for breaking news and juicy stories.


QSO1 could contain a primordial black hole, which has never been confirmed to exist (Picture: NIRCam Image)
QSO1 could contain a primordial black hole, which has never been confirmed to exist (Picture: NIRCam Image)
To test this, the team zoomed in on Abell 2744-QSO1, a red dot that twinkled when the universe was just 700million years old.

QSO1 sits in Abell 2744, or Pandora’s Cluster, a giant group of galaxies four billion light-years from Earth.

This dot seems to be made of the first elements that formed during the beginning of the universe: hydrogen and helium.

Download Your Free Guide - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Download Your Free Guide - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

It also sports a swirling, super-hot disc of cosmic crumbs, energy, and gas, hinting it’s more unspoiled and older than other little red dots.

The team modelled how a dot like QS01 could form from a primordial black hole.

There were three explanations for QSO1, says study co-author Valerio De Luca, a postdoctoral researcher at Johns Hopkins.


A black hole is an object so compact that nothing can escape its gravitational pull (Picture: Getty Images)
A black hole is an object so compact that nothing can escape its gravitational pull (Picture: Getty Images)
First, rather simply, was it born that way?

‘This is ruled out, because it would have left a detectable imprint on the cosmic microwave background, the afterglow of the Big Bang,’ De Luca says.

Their second hunch was that QSO1 was made of smaller primordial black holes, though the messiness of the universe makes it unlikely.

Chrome - Official Download
Chrome - Official Download
Google Chrome
Google Chrome
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

This left option three: lighter primordial “seeds” that sprouted by swallowing bright dust and gas, allowing a young black hole to reach large mass within just a few hundred million years.

‘This one works,’ says De Luca.

‘It can reproduce the properties of well-studied dots such as QSO1, whose light is magnified by the gravity of a galaxy cluster in front of it.’


They could be black hole stars, covered in shrouds of brightly burning hydrogen gas (Picture: JWST Feed)
They could be black hole stars, covered in shrouds of brightly burning hydrogen gas (Picture: JWST Feed)
The team now intend to look at other little red dots to see if any more fit the primordial black hole bill.

Still, Del Grosso is open to other explanations for these red-tinted astronomical objects.

They could be chunky black holes that formed in the ‘ordinary’ way, such as a galactic gas cloud that collapsed, like a crumpled-up sheet of paper.

‘The black hole may be wrapped in an extremely dense cocoon of gas that absorbs its light and re-emits it, so the whole thing glows like the surface of a giant, cool star,’ Del Grosso says.

If that’s the case, the dots would be an entirely new phenomenon: ‘black hole stars’.

Other scientists have suggested the dots are short-lived giant stars, or infant galaxies making stars at mind-boggling speeds.

Or, Del Grosso adds, all of the above. ‘These ideas are not necessarily rivals either,’ she adds.

‘A primordial black hole could be the seed at the centre of a black hole star.’

As scientists continue to point telescopes into the cosmos, Del Grosso hopes they can even discover how much they weigh or how fast they grow.

‘That will help us tell these explanations apart,’ she adds.

10 rumors we've investigated involving Pope Leo XIV
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 3w
We revisited our past coverage of the pope amid news that a tumor was removed from his right lung.
© Rocco Spaziani, accessed via Getty Images
In October, the Vatican announced that Pope Leo XIV had been diagnosed with lung adenocarcinoma, a common form of lung cancer, and had surgery to remove the lower lobe of his right lung. Days after undergoing surgery, Leo returned to the Vatican and said he would gradually return to his regular duties. His surgeon also said the pontiff would be allowed to go on his tour of Latin America in November.

Leo became leader of the Roman Catholic Church in May 2025. As the first pope from the United States and an anti-war advocate, Leo has featured in multiple rumors about his relationship with U.S. President Donald Trump — many of which have not been true.

For example, Snopes debunked a false claim that Leo said Trump was "destroying democracy" to stay out of prison. We also reported on an AI-generated false claim that Leo delivered a "fiery sermon" criticizing the U.S. president. 

For further reading, we compiled 18 rumors about Leo that we covered from 2025 to early 2026. Below is a collection of our most recent coverage of the pope.

LHC collaboration presents first result in the hunt for quirks
Story by Sam Jarman • 17h • 4 min read


(a) Preselected data (blue) and three representative signal models, each containing 300 events (green, red, purple). (b) The same distribution for data after requiring QLC in all eight scintillators. Credit: Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/z156-xk5y
(a) Preselected data (blue) and three representative signal models, each containing 300 events (green, red, purple). (b) The same distribution for data after requiring QLC in all eight scintillators. Credit: Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/z156-xk5y
An international collaboration of physicists has presented its first results in its search for hypothetical particles that could help to explain the vast disparity between the strength of gravity and the three fundamental forces described by the Standard Model. Following three years of data collection at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC)'s FASER experiment, the FASER Collaboration found no evidence for these heavy, slow-moving "quirks."

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

All the same, their analysis offers important guidance in the ongoing search for the particles and could inspire similar searches at other large-scale particle colliders.

The collaboration's analysis has been published in Physical Review Letters.

To date, the Standard Model of particle physics offers the most reliable description of the universe's fundamental building blocks and the ways in which they interact with each other. In its current form, the model contains three core "gauge groups," which together encompass three of the four fundamental forces and the particles affected by them.

While the Standard Model has stood firm against every experiment physicists have thrown at it so far, there are numerous hints that its predictions are incomplete. The most well-known of these is its failure to explain the nature of dark matter—but another issue stems from the vast difference between the strength of gravity and the three other fundamental forces. For example, for reasons the Standard Model can't explain, the weak nuclear force is some 10²⁴ times stronger than gravity: a discrepancy known as the "gauge hierarchy problem."


How oscillating pairs of quirks could be detected by FASER. Credit: Eli Welch
How oscillating pairs of quirks could be detected by FASER. Credit: Eli Welch
Quirks and infracolor
One proposed solution to the problem is to introduce an entirely new gauge group to the Standard Model. In 2008, building on ideas dating back to the 1980s, Markus Luty and Junhai Kang at the University of Maryland proposed a hypothetical family of fundamental particles named "quirks." In some theories, quirks could interact with the Higgs boson—itself responsible for the masses of elementary particles.

Related video: Lab results so strange they left researchers quietly questioning physics itself (list25)
View on Watch
View on Watch

list25
Lab results so strange they left researchers quietly questioning physics itself
Physics is supposed to be the most reliable science.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
28:08
0
Crucially, this interaction could stop quantum effects from pushing the Higgs boson's mass many orders of magnitude higher than it is observed to be. Since gravity acts on mass rather than charge, this effect would ultimately keep gravity vastly weaker than the other three forces. Central to Luty and Kang's theory, quirks would interact via a new type of force, named "infracolor," which closely resembles the strong nuclear force.

"This force could hold quirk-antiquirk pairs together with a nearly unbreakable string," explains Eli Welch at the University of California, Irvine, primary author of the FASER Collaboration's latest analysis. "This would cause them to oscillate as they travel and make them difficult to simulate and detect." Yet if quirks exist, physicists predict that they could be produced in pairs in high-energy particle collisions, leaving distinctive traces in detectors.

EKS Resource Optimization - EKS Cost Management - Slash EKS Costs
EKS Resource Optimization - EKS Cost Management - Slash EKS Costs
Kubex
Kubex
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Searching at the LHC
In 2010, the first dedicated search for quirks was carried out at Fermilab's Tevatron collider. At the LHC, several of its nine detection experiments are sensitive to quirks—but these searches have relied either on tracing the paths of particles (a task made especially difficult by the complex, oscillating paths quirks are expected to take) or on spotting quirks that stop and annihilate inside the detector.

Now, over a decade after the Tevatron search, the first dedicated quirk search at the LHC has been carried out using its Forward Search Experiment (FASER), specifically designed to detect the neutrinos created in particle collisions and to search for new elementary particles.

Located 480 m (1,575 feet) downstream of the ATLAS experiment and shielded by more than 100 meters (328 feet) of rock, FASER sees very few background particles, making it ideally suited to searching for pairs of heavy quirks as they pass through the detector.

Scale to 448 Output Channels - Easy Measurement Automation - Integrated Qubit Control
Scale to 448 Output Channels - Easy Measurement Automation - Integrated Qubit Control
info.zhinst.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"By measuring their delayed arrival and slow speed, together with the unusually large energy deposits from both particles, we can identify quirks without having to trace their paths," Welch explains. "This makes our search far less sensitive to their unusual oscillations than conventional searches."

Beyond conventional signatures
In its latest study, the FASER Collaboration has now analyzed data collected by the detector between 2022 and 2024, with the specific goal of identifying a quirk signal. Its analysis didn't detect any evidence for the quirk pairs it was searching for, but still offered valuable new constraints on the theory.

"Our results rule out a broad range of possibilities within the quirk model we considered, including masses of particular interest to theories of physics beyond the Standard Model," Welch explains.

The FASER Collaboration is now hopeful that its initial results could lay promising groundwork for an expanded search for quirks at the LHC. In demonstrating that the particles' unusual behavior could be detected without relying on more conventional tracking methods, it now hopes that other collider experiments could also be motivated to take up the search.

"More broadly, it highlights the importance of looking beyond conventional particle signatures, as new physics could be hiding in signals that existing searches were not designed to detect," Welch adds.

Are videos of Tesla's new 'flying' car performing mid-air stunts real?
Story by Laerke Christensen • 3w • 4 min read


Tesla's Roadster car spirals over the rest of the company's product lineup. Tesla, Inc.
Tesla's Roadster car spirals over the rest of the company's product lineup. Tesla, Inc.

Claim:

Electric vehicle manufacturer Tesla posted videos on Oct. 15 authentically showing the company's new "flying" car performing mid-air stunts.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

Context:

Tesla posted the videos to showcase the official launch of the company's new Roadster model. The footage shows the car performing several mid-air stunts in front of a live audience. Attendees also posted their own videos from the event, corroborating Tesla's footage. Whether the vehicle actually "flew" during the demonstration is up to individual interpretation. It is unclear whether Tesla's final product would have the same capabilities as the "flying" car seen in the clips.

 
On Oct. 15, electric vehicle manufacturer Tesla and social media users posted videos purportedly showing the company's new "flying" car performing a number of mid-air stunts.

The footage depicts a launch event for the automaker's long-awaited Roadster model. It shows a prototype driving up an upside-down ramp before flipping off and landing on its wheels, flipping over a row of other Tesla products and accelerating from zero to 60 mph in 0.81 seconds.

Throughout the video, Tesla's CEO, Elon Musk, and his fellow hosts made references to the vehicle being a "flying car."

Snopes readers searched our site to find out more about the vehicle.


In short, there is nothing to suggest the video is fake, meaning it was not created using artificial intelligence tools or the product of digital editing. Tesla livestreamed the footage on YouTube on Oct. 15. The event showcased the company's new Roadster model and was conducted in front of a live audience. Some of those attendees posted their own clips on social media, corroborating Tesla's footage. Therefore, we rate this claim true.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Audience members posted their own videos (archived, archived), among other content, and reputable car magazine Top Gear also said its staff attended the event and saw the demos. Reputable news media outlets also covered the event.

Though the clips excited autophiles and technology experts worldwide, some people cast doubt over Tesla's claim that the new Roadster is a true "flying" car (archived, archived).

Global first: Scientists identify and trigger 'forbidden' vibrations in single molecules
Story by Joshua Shavit • 16h • 6 min read

Basel researchers used a calcium ion to read the state of a single trapped nitrogen molecular ion without consuming it.
Reversible laser pulses revealed exceptionally weak transitions and improved the fundamental vibrational frequency measurement tenfold.
The results support future molecular-clock and qubit research, but do not demonstrate a working molecular clock or new physics.
A transition described as “forbidden” sounds like something physics should prevent. In spectroscopy, the word means something more specific: an energy change that cannot occur through the usual, strongest interaction with light. Weaker interactions can still produce it, making the signal exceptionally difficult to locate and measure.

Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Researchers at the University of Basel have now found and reversibly controlled such transitions in individual nitrogen molecular ions. The study, published in Nature Communications, uses a second trapped particle to reveal the molecule’s state. Crucially, the measurement does not require destroying and replacing the molecule each time.

The work opens a practical route to investigating forbidden molecular vibrations, which could become useful references for timekeeping and quantum information. Related advances in atomic clocks show why controlling narrow transitions matters. Molecules offer additional internal motions that could provide different sensitivities and applications.

Stefan Willitsch led the team, with Aleksandr Shlykov and Meissa L. Diouf as co-first authors. Richard Karl, Mikolaj Roguski and Umesh C. Joshi also contributed. Their experiment establishes a spectroscopy and control method, adding to research on qubit control without yet delivering a molecular quantum computer.

Were Flydubai pilots who caused emergency landing Russian, Ukrainian? We investigated
Story by Laerke Christensen • 1mo • 5 min read


A flydubai Boeing 737 MAX 8 aircraft prepares for landing at Israel's Ben Gurion Airport in Lod on September 30, 2026. Jack Guez/AFP, accessed via Getty Images
A flydubai Boeing 737 MAX 8 aircraft prepares for landing at Israel's Ben Gurion Airport in Lod on September 30, 2026. Jack Guez/AFP, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

The pilots who got in a reported altercation on a Flydubai flight on Sept. 30, 2026, leading to an emergency landing, are Russian and Ukrainian.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

Context:

The Embassy of India in Saudi Arabia said the pilot who was the victim of the midair stabbing, Smit Machchhar, is Indian.

 
On Sept. 30, 2026, a claim (archived) circulated online that a fight between Russian and Ukrainian pilots forced a Flydubai plane to carry out an emergency landing in Saudi Arabia.

The claim spread as Flydubai, an Emirati airline, said in a statement that "an altercation" occurred on the flight deck of Flight FZ 1073, traveling from Dubai to Tel Aviv on Sept. 30. An on-duty crew member managed to "secure" the flight, the statement said, which landed in Saudi Arabia with all passengers and crew accounted for.

According to Cluster2 (archived), the company that owns the airport where the Flydubai plane landed, the captain and first officer sustained injuries and were taken to a hospital in Saudi Arabia. Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu said in a video statement (archived) on Sept. 30 that one pilot had stabbed another.

One X user who shared the claim about the pilots' nationalities in Arabic wrote:

The Emirates plane landed at Tabuk Airport after an emergency was declared on board.

Cause of the incident: A fight between the pilot and his co-pilot escalated to punching and stabbing.

The pilot was Russian, and his co-pilot was Ukrainian.


The claim also spread elsewhere on X (archived, archived).

According to Ukrainian media, Israeli media initially reported that the pilots on the Flydubai plane were Russian and Ukrainian. 

Later reporting by Reuters and Israeli media did not repeat this claim but rather said that anonymous Israeli officials said the pilot who allegedly stabbed another was from Oman.

The Embassy of India in Saudi Arabia said (archived) on X on Sept. 30 that the pilot who was the victim of the midair stabbing was Indian. Therefore, given that there were two pilots on board the plane, it was not possible for them to be Russian and Ukrainian, rendering such claims false. 

The embassy also wrote that the pilot, who it named as Smit Machchhar, had been hospitalized following the incident on board flight FZ 1073 but was in stable condition. 

At the time of this writing, Flydubai had not confirmed the identity or nationality of the pilots on the flight, or exactly what happened in the cockpit. The airline said in a statement:

At this early stage, the underlying reasons and motives behind this event are unknown and remain subject to a formal investigation. We urge all parties to refrain from premature speculation while authorities gather the facts.

We contacted the ministries of foreign affairs for Russia, Ukraine and Oman to ask them to confirm whether either of the FZ 1073 pilots held any of those nationalities. We also contacted the Israeli Prime Minister's Office to confirm the statement attributed to Israeli officials that the pilot who allegedly stabbed the other was Omani. We await replies to our queries.

What we know about 'altercation' on Flydubai plane
While Flydubai and Cluster2's statements were vague about what exactly led to the injury of one of Flight FZ 1073's pilots, Netanyahu gave a more detailed explanation in a Sept. 30 video statement, saying: 


This morning, a very serious security incident occurred on a flight from Dubai to Israel. Almost all the passengers on the flight were Israelis. During the flight, as they were approaching Israel, one of the pilots stabbed the other pilot, and it appears he tried to crash the plane with everyone on board. 

I spoke with one of the Israeli passengers. He told me that the plane went into a spin and began to nosedive. He told me that somehow, he managed to break into the cockpit with one of the crew members, and together they subdued the attacking pilot as he was trying to sabotage the plane's systems. Another flight crew member who was on the plane entered the cockpit and managed to stabilize the aircraft.

Netanyahu added that the suspected assailant was arrested and was "being questioned by the Saudi authorities." The statement did not contain any confirmation of the pilots' nationalities.

Tracking data for Flight FZ 1037 supported Netanyahu's statement that the plane had dipped suddenly while in midair. An altitude graph from Flightradar24 showed the plane had been cruising at 34,000 feet when it dropped to around 17,000 feet within minutes around 2.5 hours after departure. The flight briefly climbed back to around 21,000 feet before reducing altitude to around 15,000 feet and landing at Tabuk Regional Airport at around 7 a.m. UTC, four hours after it took off from Dubai.

FlightRadar24 reported that FZ 1037 had squawked code 7700, which indicates a general emergency, at 5:28 a.m., shortly after it experienced the sudden dip in altitude. It then squawked 7500, indicating unlawful interference, around seven minutes later. The plane switched back to squawking 7700 as it approached Tabuk Regional Airport.

Cluster2 said (archived) all passengers on FZ 1037 had left Tabuk Regional Airport by the afternoon of Sept. 30. Flydubai said in a statement that it had suspended flights to and from Israel while the Sept. 30 incident was under investigation.

Deepl.com provided translation from Hebrew into English.

Popular Mechanics
707.2K Followers
Scientists have hunted dark energy for decades. They may be chasing a cosmic ghost.
Story by Darren Orf • 1d • 3 min read


A new study finds a mathematical solution for the universe’s expansion within the original work of Albert Einstein.
A new study finds a mathematical solution for the universe’s expansion within the original work of Albert Einstein.
© bymuratdeniz - Getty Images
In 1916, Albert Einstein published his foundational paper on the General Theory of Relativity in the German-language science journal Annalen der Physik. Now, 110 years later, scientists are still debating just how right Einstein’s conception of the universe really was, especially as it relates to the accelerated expansion of the universe. Of course, at the time of his revolutionary publication, the world still didn’t know that the universe was expanding, so a year later, Einstein introduced a “lambda” cosmological constant to his equations to form a stable, non-collapsing universe.

Thanks to the pioneering work of Edwin Hubble in 1929, the world learned that the universe was actually expanding, and in response Einstein discarded lambda, calling it his “greatest blunder.” What Einstein couldn’t have known was that, decades after his death, lambda would make a comeback. When astronomers discovered in the late 1990s that the universe’s expansion was speeding up, the constant became central to the modern model of the cosmos, known as “Lambda-Cold Dark Matter,” or ΛCDM. Rising along with the renaissance of the cosmological constant was a concept known as dark energy, the force that made this accelerated expansion possible.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

This ΛCDM is a pretty tidy description of the universe with one glaring plot hole—we’ve never directly seen or measured dark energy. Because of this lack of evidence, some scientists have searched for other possible explanations, even disregarding Einstein entirely, but a new study by researchers at the University of California, Davis takes the opposite approach. According to their research, published earlier this year in the journal Proceedings of the Royal Society A, Einstein was actually more right than he ever knew.

Related video: We can't look beyond the Big Bang - The cosmic mystery that could hide an entire parent universe beyond our reach (Origins Explained)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Origins Explained
We can't look beyond the Big Bang - The cosmic mystery that could hide an entire parent universe beyond our reach
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
0:00
0
To build their case for a universe without dark energy, UC Davis mathematics professor emeritus Blake Temple and his colleagues turned to Friedmann spacetimes. These are a family of solutions to Einstein’s field equations originally developed by Alexander Friedmann, the Soviet mathematician who first proposed that the universe is expanding.

Global first: Scientists identify and trigger 'forbidden' vibrations in single molecules
Story by Joshua Shavit • 16h • 6 min read

Basel researchers used a calcium ion to read the state of a single trapped nitrogen molecular ion without consuming it.
Reversible laser pulses revealed exceptionally weak transitions and improved the fundamental vibrational frequency measurement tenfold.
The results support future molecular-clock and qubit research, but do not demonstrate a working molecular clock or new physics.
A transition described as “forbidden” sounds like something physics should prevent. In spectroscopy, the word means something more specific: an energy change that cannot occur through the usual, strongest interaction with light. Weaker interactions can still produce it, making the signal exceptionally difficult to locate and measure.

Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Researchers at the University of Basel have now found and reversibly controlled such transitions in individual nitrogen molecular ions. The study, published in Nature Communications, uses a second trapped particle to reveal the molecule’s state. Crucially, the measurement does not require destroying and replacing the molecule each time.

The work opens a practical route to investigating forbidden molecular vibrations, which could become useful references for timekeeping and quantum information. Related advances in atomic clocks show why controlling narrow transitions matters. Molecules offer additional internal motions that could provide different sensitivities and applications.

Stefan Willitsch led the team, with Aleksandr Shlykov and Meissa L. Diouf as co-first authors. Richard Karl, Mikolaj Roguski and Umesh C. Joshi also contributed. Their experiment establishes a spectroscopy and control method, adding to research on qubit control without yet delivering a molecular quantum computer.

Expand article logo  Continue reading


An illustration shows how a precisely controlled laser pulse excites normally forbidden vibrations in a single nitrogen molecule, while a calcium ion acts as a highly sensitive sensor to detect the molecule’s quantum state using quantum-logic spectroscopy. (CREDIT: Aleksandr Shlykov)

Were Flydubai pilots who caused emergency landing Russian, Ukrainian? We investigated
Story by Laerke Christensen • 1mo • 5 min read


A flydubai Boeing 737 MAX 8 aircraft prepares for landing at Israel's Ben Gurion Airport in Lod on September 30, 2026. Jack Guez/AFP, accessed via Getty Images
A flydubai Boeing 737 MAX 8 aircraft prepares for landing at Israel's Ben Gurion Airport in Lod on September 30, 2026. Jack Guez/AFP, accessed via Getty Images

Claim:

The pilots who got in a reported altercation on a Flydubai flight on Sept. 30, 2026, leading to an emergency landing, are Russian and Ukrainian.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

Context:

The Embassy of India in Saudi Arabia said the pilot who was the victim of the midair stabbing, Smit Machchhar, is Indian.

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

 
On Sept. 30, 2026, a claim (archived) circulated online that a fight between Russian and Ukrainian pilots forced a Flydubai plane to carry out an emergency landing in Saudi Arabia.

The claim spread as Flydubai, an Emirati airline, said in a statement that "an altercation" occurred on the flight deck of Flight FZ 1073, traveling from Dubai to Tel Aviv on Sept. 30. An on-duty crew member managed to "secure" the flight, the statement said, which landed in Saudi Arabia with all passengers and crew accounted for.

According to Cluster2 (archived), the company that owns the airport where the Flydubai plane landed, the captain and first officer sustained injuries and were taken to a hospital in Saudi Arabia. Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu said in a video statement (archived) on Sept. 30 that one pilot had stabbed another.

One X user who shared the claim about the pilots' nationalities in Arabic wrote:

The Emirates plane landed at Tabuk Airport after an emergency was declared on board.

Cause of the incident: A fight between the pilot and his co-pilot escalated to punching and stabbing.

The pilot was Russian, and his co-pilot was Ukrainian.


The claim also spread elsewhere on X (archived, archived).

According to Ukrainian media, Israeli media initially reported that the pilots on the Flydubai plane were Russian and Ukrainian. 

Later reporting by Reuters and Israeli media did not repeat this claim but rather said that anonymous Israeli officials said the pilot who allegedly stabbed another was from Oman.

Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The Embassy of India in Saudi Arabia said (archived) on X on Sept. 30 that the pilot who was the victim of the midair stabbing was Indian. Therefore, given that there were two pilots on board the plane, it was not possible for them to be Russian and Ukrainian, rendering such claims false. 

The embassy also wrote that the pilot, who it named as Smit Machchhar, had been hospitalized following the incident on board flight FZ 1073 but was in stable condition. 

At the time of this writing, Flydubai had not confirmed the identity or nationality of the pilots on the flight, or exactly what happened in the cockpit. The airline said in a statement:

At this early stage, the underlying reasons and motives behind this event are unknown and remain subject to a formal investigation. We urge all parties to refrain from premature speculation while authorities gather the facts.

We contacted the ministries of foreign affairs for Russia, Ukraine and Oman to ask them to confirm whether either of the FZ 1073 pilots held any of those nationalities. We also contacted the Israeli Prime Minister's Office to confirm the statement attributed to Israeli officials that the pilot who allegedly stabbed the other was Omani. We await replies to our queries.

What we know about 'altercation' on Flydubai plane
While Flydubai and Cluster2's statements were vague about what exactly led to the injury of one of Flight FZ 1073's pilots, Netanyahu gave a more detailed explanation in a Sept. 30 video statement, saying: 


This morning, a very serious security incident occurred on a flight from Dubai to Israel. Almost all the passengers on the flight were Israelis. During the flight, as they were approaching Israel, one of the pilots stabbed the other pilot, and it appears he tried to crash the plane with everyone on board. 

I spoke with one of the Israeli passengers. He told me that the plane went into a spin and began to nosedive. He told me that somehow, he managed to break into the cockpit with one of the crew members, and together they subdued the attacking pilot as he was trying to sabotage the plane's systems. Another flight crew member who was on the plane entered the cockpit and managed to stabilize the aircraft.

Netanyahu added that the suspected assailant was arrested and was "being questioned by the Saudi authorities." The statement did not contain any confirmation of the pilots' nationalities.

Tracking data for Flight FZ 1037 supported Netanyahu's statement that the plane had dipped suddenly while in midair. An altitude graph from Flightradar24 showed the plane had been cruising at 34,000 feet when it dropped to around 17,000 feet within minutes around 2.5 hours after departure. The flight briefly climbed back to around 21,000 feet before reducing altitude to around 15,000 feet and landing at Tabuk Regional Airport at around 7 a.m. UTC, four hours after it took off from Dubai.
