FlightRadar24 reported that FZ 1037 had squawked code 7700, which indicates a general emergency, at 5:28 a.m., shortly after it experienced the sudden dip in altitude. It then squawked 7500, indicating unlawful interference, around seven minutes later. The plane switched back to squawking 7700 as it approached Tabuk Regional Airport.

Cluster2 said (archived) all passengers on FZ 1037 had left Tabuk Regional Airport by the afternoon of Sept. 30. Flydubai said in a statement that it had suspended flights to and from Israel while the Sept. 30 incident was under investigation.

Deepl.com provided translation from Hebrew into English.

Popular Mechanics
707.2K Followers
Scientists have hunted dark energy for decades. They may be chasing a cosmic ghost.
Story by Darren Orf • 1d • 3 min read


A new study finds a mathematical solution for the universe’s expansion within the original work of Albert Einstein.
A new study finds a mathematical solution for the universe’s expansion within the original work of Albert Einstein.
© bymuratdeniz - Getty Images
In 1916, Albert Einstein published his foundational paper on the General Theory of Relativity in the German-language science journal Annalen der Physik. Now, 110 years later, scientists are still debating just how right Einstein’s conception of the universe really was, especially as it relates to the accelerated expansion of the universe. Of course, at the time of his revolutionary publication, the world still didn’t know that the universe was expanding, so a year later, Einstein introduced a “lambda” cosmological constant to his equations to form a stable, non-collapsing universe.

Thanks to the pioneering work of Edwin Hubble in 1929, the world learned that the universe was actually expanding, and in response Einstein discarded lambda, calling it his “greatest blunder.” What Einstein couldn’t have known was that, decades after his death, lambda would make a comeback. When astronomers discovered in the late 1990s that the universe’s expansion was speeding up, the constant became central to the modern model of the cosmos, known as “Lambda-Cold Dark Matter,” or ΛCDM. Rising along with the renaissance of the cosmological constant was a concept known as dark energy, the force that made this accelerated expansion possible.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

This ΛCDM is a pretty tidy description of the universe with one glaring plot hole—we’ve never directly seen or measured dark energy. Because of this lack of evidence, some scientists have searched for other possible explanations, even disregarding Einstein entirely, but a new study by researchers at the University of California, Davis takes the opposite approach. According to their research, published earlier this year in the journal Proceedings of the Royal Society A, Einstein was actually more right than he ever knew.

Related video: We can't look beyond the Big Bang - The cosmic mystery that could hide an entire parent universe beyond our reach (Origins Explained)

To build their case for a universe without dark energy, UC Davis mathematics professor emeritus Blake Temple and his colleagues turned to Friedmann spacetimes. These are a family of solutions to Einstein’s field equations originally developed by Alexander Friedmann, the Soviet mathematician who first proposed that the universe is expanding.

A 60-year search is over: Chemists isolate long-elusive molecules with boron-oxygen triple bonds
Story by Science X staff • 2w • 2 min read


Structure, bonding and electronic aspects of a newly prepared oxoborane. Credit: Lukas Lubczyk, Christian Markl, Dr. Thomas Kupfer, Dr. Rian Dewhurst / Universität Würzburg
Structure, bonding and electronic aspects of a newly prepared oxoborane. Credit: Lukas Lubczyk, Christian Markl, Dr. Thomas Kupfer, Dr. Rian Dewhurst / Universität Würzburg
In a new publication in the journal Nature Chemistry, chemists in professor Holger Braunschweig's team at the University of Würzburg, supported by computational work from the team of professor Ashwini K. Phukan at Tezpur University in India, detail their discovery of a method for preparing and isolating a range of surprisingly stable oxoboranes—a challenge that has stood for over 60 years.

Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Oxoboranes: Stumping the best minds in chemistry
The element boron does not like to form triple bonds—this challenge has motivated Würzburg chemist Braunschweig to make molecules with boron-containing triple bonds for over 15 years.

In 2012, his team reported the first molecule with a boron-boron triple bond, and in 2025 they reported the first neutral molecule with a boron-carbon triple bond. In the first row of the periodic table's p-block, just one challenge remained: organic oxoboranes, compounds with boron-oxygen triple bonds.

Since at least the early 1960s, oxoboranes have been the target of decades of synthetic attempts from a wide range of inorganic and organic chemists around the world. They have been trapped and detected under extreme conditions, their existence has been inferred from chemical reactions and a number of derivatives have been made—but isolation of stable examples remained elusive.

SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Creating the right conditions for serendipity
When Würzburg chemistry doctoral students Lukas Lubczyk and Christian Markl combined a molecule called a diboracumulene with different ketones—standard reagents in organic chemistry—they independently noted that the reactions had led to unusual orange or yellow solids. "The discovery was a pleasant surprise. I never thought the diboracumulene would react the way it did, let alone with multiple different, simple substrates. And when Christian came to me with his compound, I knew we were on to something," says Lubczyk.

It was a great surprise that, out of many other seemingly more likely outcomes, and despite decades of attempts to prepare these compounds, the elusive oxoboranes would be the main products of these reactions. "Nearly everybody in the field had given up on oxoboranes, as they were thought to be just too unstable to be isolated. So we were stunned to see them spontaneously form in these reactions and stick around long enough that we could study them," says Braunschweig.

Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
Epitope Binning Assays - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services - Surface Plasmon Resonance
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Three oxoboranes were prepared, showing that the method could lead to many new oxoboranes in the future. The team is now trying to see just how far the method can be pushed to prepare other elusive molecules across the periodic table.

More information: Lukas Lubczyk et al., Synthesis and isolation of stable oxoboranes, Nature Chemistry (2026). DOI: 10.1038/s41557-026-02272-w

Provided by Julius Maximilian University of Würzburg

This story was originally published on Phys.org.

Tiny crystals sent around the moon have revealed something unexpected about space radiation
Story by Kouceila Rekik • 2w • 5 min read


Credit: IFJ PAN | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: IFJ PAN | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
Thousands of microscopic radiation records collected aboard NASA’s Artemis I mission have revealed that radiation exposure near the Moon differs from low Earth orbit in a less dramatic way than scientists might expect. The findings, obtained using experimental crystal detectors, offer new insight into the risks astronauts will face during future lunar missions.

Epitope Binning Assays - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance
Epitope Binning Assays - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Thousands of Crystals Recorded Radiation During NASA’s Lunar Mission
When NASA’s Artemis I mission traveled around the Moon in late 2022, its Orion spacecraft carried two human-shaped mannequins packed with radiation detectors. Hidden inside these anatomical models were more than 10,000 small crystals, each designed to capture information about the energetic particles passing through the spacecraft.

Among them were several dozen experimental lithium fluoride detectors developed by scientists at the Institute of Nuclear Physics of the Polish Academy of Sciences (IFJ PAN) in Cracow. The researchers also contributed nearly 600 conventional thermoluminescent crystal detectors to the mission.

The experiment, known as MARE (Matroshka AstroRad Radiation Experiment), was designed to investigate how radiation penetrates the human body during travel beyond Earth’s protective magnetic environment.

The latest analysis, describedin the journal Radiation Measurements, presents a method for interpreting the particle tracks preserved inside the experimental crystals.

Unlike electronic instruments that measure radiation in real time, these detectors preserve physical evidence of particle interactions for later examination.

“The fluorescent radiation dose detectors based on lithium fluoride, developed at our institute, are passive in nature. In other words, they work like photographic film: once the crystals have been exposed to radiation, the tracks left by individual particles must be analyzed optically under a microscope,” explains Pawel Bilski (IFJ PAN), one of the co-authors of the paper.

This approach allowed scientists to examine individual particle tracks after Orion returned to Earth, providing a detailed record of radiation encountered during its journey.


Illustration showing the effect of ionization density on the structure of fluorescent tracks, using carbon and iron ion tracks as examples. The colour scale is arbitrary. (For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.)Credit: Radiation Measurements

(For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.)Credit: Radiation Measurements
© Daily Galaxy US
How Tiny Crystals Exposed Invisible Radiation Tracks
The experimental detectors work by recording microscopic changes in their crystal structure. When an energetic particle passes through lithium fluoride, it creates defects inthe crystal lattice. These defects become color centers that emit red fluorescence when illuminated with blue light.

Related video: Investigating the cosmic lithium problem and early galaxies (Some Guy Who Knows Stuff)

0
Under a fluorescence microscope, scientists can observe the paths left by individual particles without chemically treating the crystal. Conventional plastic track detectors, by comparison, require chemical etching before their recorded tracks become visible.

Yet translating these fluorescent patterns into accurate radiation measurements presented a challenge. Cosmic radiation contains particles with widely varying energies, masses, and directions of travel. Some particles produce bright, continuous tracks, while others leave faint sequences of separated dots.

The microscope also captures blurred light from sections of tracks outside its narrow focal plane, complicating measurements of fluorescence intensity.

To overcome these problems, researchers developed calibration procedures and software that reduced background interference and corrected distortions in the recorded signals.

“After applying a series of corrections and developing software to appropriately remove the background interfering with the measurements, our results differed by no more than 15% from those obtained using conventional track detectors across the entire overlapping range of energy deposition values. Moreover, we also recorded numerous particle tracks corresponding to energy deposits below the detection threshold of the detectors used to date,” Bilski emphasizes.

The results suggest that these millimeter-scale detectors can capture particle interactions that conventional track detectors may miss, expanding the range of radiation events available for scientific analysis.


Track of an iron ion entering the crystal at an angle of incidence of 42°. Panels (a) to (g) show images acquired with the focus set at different depths within the crystal, in steps of 1 μm. Panel (h) shows the maximum intensity projection of all images. The colour scale is arbitrary. (For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.) Credit: Radiation Measurements
Track of an iron ion entering the crystal at an angle of incidence of 42°. Panels (a) to (g) show images acquired with the focus set at different depths within the crystal, in steps of 1 μm. Panel (h) shows the maximum intensity projection of all images. The colour scale is arbitrary. (For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.) Credit: Radiation Measurements
© Daily Galaxy US
Radiation Near the Moon Reveals an Unexpected Difference
One of the most revealing findings concerns how radiation is distributed through the human body in different regions of space.

During the earlier MATROSHKA experiment aboard the International Space Station, low-energy protons trapped within Earth’s Van Allen radiation belts contributed to radiation exposure. As these particles slowed inside the anatomical mannequins, they deposited different amounts of energy at different depths.

Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - HTP SPR Binding Assay Services
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The result was an uneven distribution of radiation doses.

Near the Moon, the situation changes. Earth’s radiation belts are absent, while high-energy galactic cosmic rays become a more prominent source of exposure. These particles can penetrate the entire human body, depositing portions of their energy along their paths.

Consequently, the Artemis I detectors recorded a substantially more uniform dose distribution inside the mannequins.

“Low-energy protons, trapped by our planet’s magnetic field within the Van Allen belts, can account for the majority of the radiation dose received by astronauts in low Earth orbit. In the vicinity of the moon, this substantial contribution disappears. Our measurements from the MARE experiment indicate that, although total radiation doses near the moon are higher than those in Earth orbit due to the considerable levels of galactic radiation, the difference is not as large as one might intuitively expect,” Bilski notes.

The finding does not mean lunar radiation is harmless. Instead, it demonstrates that radiation exposure depends on particle energy, origin, and interaction with human tissue, not simply on distance from Earth.

What the Discovery Means for Future Moon Missions
The new detector technology could provide researchers with a more detailed picture of the radiation environment astronauts will encounter during extended missions beyond Earth.

Developed over roughly a decade, the lithium fluoride crystals offer several advantages: their small dimensions make them suitable for personal monitoring, their fine track resolution helps distinguish particle interactions, and their broad detection range allows scientists to examine different forms of ionizing radiation.

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Some versions can also detect neutron-related radiation. Crystals enriched in lithium-6 register secondary charged particles produced when neutrons interact with lithium nuclei, providing another way to investigate radiation exposure.

These capabilities could support future studies of spacecraft shielding, radiation distribution within the body, and astronaut monitoring.


a) Schematic illustration of the Zohar phantom with and without the AstroRad vest (the passive detector packages encircled with green lines); b) Photo of Helga (left) and Zohar (right) phantoms mounted in the Orion cockpit before launch. Source: DLR, NASA. (For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.)Credit: Radiation Measurements

Source: DLR, NASA. (For interpretation of the references to colour in this figure legend, the reader is referred to the Web version of this article.)Credit: Radiation Measurements
© Daily Galaxy US
Still, the Artemis I findings represent preliminary estimates rather than a complete assessment of human radiation risk. Absorbed dose alone cannot establish biological harm, which also depends on radiation type, exposure duration, and other factors. Solar particle events remain another concern for missions beyond Earth’s magnetic protection.

As NASA prepares for further Artemis program missions, the microscopic records preserved inside these crystals offer a valuable source of evidence. Their significance lies not only in showing how much radiation reaches the Moon’s vicinity, but also in revealing how different particles deposit energy inside a human-shaped body, information that can help researchers refine radiation protection strategies for future crews.

Who ordered Starlink, Starshield slowdown in Ukraine? What we know
Story by Laerke Christensen • 3w • 10 min read


U.S. President Donald Trump presents the National Medal of Science to Elon Musk during the 
U.S. President Donald Trump presents the National Medal of Science to Elon Musk during the

The claim that U.S. President Donald Trump or another White House official ordered the slowdown of Starlink and Starshield services in Ukraine in October is unproven.
Serhii Beskrestnov, a defense technology adviser to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, reported on Oct. 14 that SpaceX, the spaceflight and telecommunications company Elon Musk founded, had imposed speed restrictions on its Starlink and Starshield satellite internet services in Ukraine. Beskrestnov warned that the slowdown severely limited the flight speed of Ukraine's drones and "disrupted" a number of its military missions.
Reuters reported on Oct. 15 that Pentagon officials received an order to limit Starshield, SpaceX's military-grade satellite internet service, "from inside the White House." That same day, British newspaper The Telegraph reported that it was Musk who first approached Trump with the idea to slow down SpaceX services in Ukraine. The paper reported that one anonymous source said Trump "agreed" with Musk's idea. On Oct. 20, The Wall Street Journal also reported that one anonymous source said Trump and Musk spoke about slowing down Starshield speeds before Musk reportedly "ordered" restrictions.

Snopes could not independently verify Reuters', The Telegraph's or the Wall Street Journal's reports because they relied on anonymous sources. Considering the fact that Trump and members of his cabinet publicly and repeatedly denied having given the order to slow down Starlink or Starshield in Ukraine, we cannot give this claim a rating. The Pentagon and the White House did not provide relevant comment when asked about the above news reports.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Snopes will update this article if, or when, additional information surfaces. We have contacted SpaceX and an adviser to Zelenskyy for further information about who ordered the slowdown in SpaceX's services in Ukraine. We await replies to our queries.
On Oct. 14, Serhii Beskrestnov, a defense technology adviser to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, wrote on Facebook (archived) that SpaceX, the spaceflight and telecommunications company Elon Musk founded, had imposed restrictions on Starlink and Starshield, its commercial and governmental satellite internet services, in Ukraine.

Scientists may have finally found the source of the mysterious hum heard for 50 years
Story by Sarah Jones • 4mo • 3 min read


Credit: Idun Haugan, NTNU | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: Idun Haugan, NTNU | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
For more than half a century, people across the world have reported a strange low-frequency sound often described as a distant, steady rumble. A new study published in PLOS One suggests that many of these experiences may not originate in the environment at all, but could be linked to internal processes within the human auditory system.

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The phenomenon first gained attention in the 1970s in Bristol, UK, where residents complained of a persistent low drone, most noticeable at night. Despite repeated reports and public concern, no clear external source was ever identified at the time.

Since then, similar accounts have appeared across Europe and North America. The Guardian reports that hundreds of cases have been logged through global databases tracking the phenomenon, even though the “Hum” remains difficult to measure reliably in controlled conditions.

A Long Trail Of Similar Reports
After the early Bristol cases, similar descriptions emerged in other parts of the United Kingdom, including Hythe, Plymouth, Southampton, and Swansea. The reports tended to follow the same pattern: a low, continuous sound that seemed to appear without warning and without an identifiable origin.

Related video: Scientists have been searching for aliens for decades - then they detected a mysterious signal (Megaprojects)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Megaprojects
Scientists have been searching for aliens for decades - then they detected a mysterious signal

0:00
/
Duration 
14:50
0
Later, the phenomenon was also reported much farther away, including in Taos, New Mexico, and Kokomo, Indiana. This geographic spread helped push the Hum from a localised curiosity into a broader scientific question, since it appeared in environments with no obvious shared physical source.


Global distribution of reported “Hum” cases across continents. Credit: The World Hum Database Project
Global distribution of reported “Hum” cases across continents. Credit: The World Hum Database Project
© Daily Galaxy US
The World Hum Database Project, which has collected testimonies since 2012, continues to gather similar accounts. The descriptions are often consistent: a deep, monotonous drone that is hard to locate and sometimes more noticeable in quiet, indoor settings.

No Clear External Explanation Yet
Over the decades, researchers have explored several possible environmental causes. One early idea from the 1970s suggested that interactions between fast-moving jet streams and slower air masses could generate low-frequency vibrations. More recently, in 2015, French researchers proposed that continuous ocean wave activity might play a role.

Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis
Epitope Binning Assays - Surface Plasmon Resonance - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The Guardian notes that these hypotheses face a shared problem: very low-frequency sound is notoriously difficult to isolate in real-world environments. Because of its long wavelength, it can blend into background noise and resist precise localisation, making it hard to confirm a single source.


Frequency profile of reported low-frequency “Hum” experiences. Credit: PLOS One
Frequency profile of reported low-frequency “Hum” experiences. Credit: PLOS One
© Daily Galaxy US
Despite these efforts, no external explanation has consistently matched all reported cases. The phenomenon continues to appear irregularly, both in intensity and location.

Study Points to Internal Sound Activity
A recent study published in PLOS One takes a different approach by focusing on people who report hearing the sound. Researchers led by Markus Drexl at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology examined 28 participants in Germany who described low-frequency sound percepts (LFSPs).

Why Your Rotator Cuff May Not Be Healing (A Careful Review of Your Practices)
Why Your Rotator Cuff May Not Be Healing (A Careful Review of Your Practices)
Practical Health Magazine
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Only two participants showed unusually strong sensitivity to low-frequency sound during testing, suggesting that heightened external hearing is unlikely to explain most cases in the group.

The researchers also investigatedotoacoustic emissions, which are faint sounds generated by the inner ear itself. While these emissions can sometimes be detected under specific conditions, the study found no consistent link between them and the participants’ reported experiences.

In comments shared alongside the study, Drexl explained that:

“We know that there are people who hear low-frequency sounds that can actually be measured, even if other people don’t hear them,” he added in a release published by the Norwegian SciTech News that, “it’s not so easy to find the source of these sound waves, because it’s a struggle to localize low-frequency sounds.”

A Working Hypothesis of Low-Frequency Tinnitus
With external sources and otoacoustic emissions largely ruled out for the participants studied, the researchers point toward low-frequency tinnitus as a likely explanation for many cases. Unlike the more familiar high-pitched form, this version can present as a deep hum or vibration-like sensation, sometimes perceived as external despite being internally generated.

“What we know about the hearing system is mainly based on how we capture and process sound with higher frequencies,” he said. “We know less about how the auditory system handles and processes low-frequency sound, or infrasound.”


Factors associated with fluctuations in perceived low-frequency “Hum” symptoms. Credit: PLOS One
Factors associated with fluctuations in perceived low-frequency “Hum” symptoms. Credit: PLOS One
© Daily Galaxy US
The researchers do not present the issue as fully resolved. While internal auditory mechanisms may account for a significant portion of reported cases, the long history and wide distribution of the Hum suggest that more than one explanation could still be involved.

Is this photo of Bob Dylan meeting Pope Leo XIV real?
Story by Taija PerryCook • 1w • 2 min read


Pope Leo XIV (right) meets with American singer Bob Dylan (left) during a private audience at the Apostolic Palace on Nov. 4, 2026, in Vatican City, Vatican. Vatican Pool, accessed via Getty Images.
Pope Leo XIV (right) meets with American singer Bob Dylan (left) during a private audience at the Apostolic Palace on Nov. 4, 2026, in Vatican City, Vatican. Vatican Pool, accessed via Getty Images.

Claim:

A photograph from November authentically depicts Pope Leo XIV meeting with hoodie-clad American folk singer Bob Dylan.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

In early November, an image circulated online purportedly showing Pope Leo XIV meeting with American folk singer Bob Dylan, 85, who was dressed in a black hoodie. 

"What's the deal with the hoodie? Why is he wearing it all the time?" one Reddit user commented on a post. Other people wanted to know if the image was created using generative AI.

The image was authentic. The Vatican's official social media pages posted the image (archived) of the two men, who both hail from the American Midwest. 

"Pope Leo XIV met with American singer-songwriter Bob Dylan in a private audience on Wednesday, November 4," the photo's caption read. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading


Writer Salvatore Cernuzio contributed a story (archived) to the Vatican News about the meeting that read, in part:

The meeting between Pope Leo and Dylan—who wore a black hooded sweatshirt—was brief, consisting of greetings and a few lighthearted exchanges. The Holy Father presented the singer with a rosary, while Dylan reciprocated with a book: Chronicles: Volume One, his autobiography, which has been published in the United States and other countries around the world.

It wasn't the first time Dylan made a public appearance in a black hoodie, nor was it his first time meeting a pope (he also met Pope John Paul II in 1997). Though the singer was born and raised Jewish, he converted to Christianity in the 1970s.

Antonio Spadaro, an Italian priest who arranges meetings with public figures for the pope, formerly known as Robert Prevost, wrote in an Instagram post: "Born in Chicago in 1955, Prevost grew up with the American music of the era" (translation via a native speaker).

How ripples in space-time can reveal new exoplanets
Story by Francis Reddy • 1d • 4 min read


This artist's concept shows the LISA mission at work. Telescopes aboard each of the three spacecraft simultaneously transmit and receive infrared laser beams, measuring their mutual distances to extremely high precision across 1.6 million miles (2.5 million kilometers). Credit: ESA
This artist's concept shows the LISA mission at work. Telescopes aboard each of the three spacecraft simultaneously transmit and receive infrared laser beams, measuring their mutual distances to extremely high precision across 1.6 million miles (2.5 million kilometers). Credit: ESA
The LISA (Laser Interferometer Space Antenna) observatory, now in development, will detect gravitational waves, the tiny ripples in space-time caused by orbiting masses. Now two studies led by NASA astrophysicists show in new detail how LISA could reveal a new and unique population of exoplanets—worlds beyond our solar system—throughout our galaxy.

Epitope Binning Assays - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance
Epitope Binning Assays - SPR Antibody Affinity Analysis - Surface Plasmon Resonance
WuXi Biologics
WuXi Biologics
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

"The most common source for LISA is expected to be double white dwarf systems, which are binaries composed of white dwarfs in tight orbits," said Casey McGrath, a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Maryland, Baltimore County, and NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center in Greenbelt, Maryland. "If an exoplanet also orbits within one of these systems, its presence becomes imprinted on the gravitational wave signal in a way we'll be able to uncover."


NASA's infrared-sensitive James Webb Space Telescope, shown in this artist's concept, will be able to confirm planets detected by gravitational waves in binary white dwarf systems located within a few hundred light-years. Credit: NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center Conceptual Image Lab
NASA's infrared-sensitive James Webb Space Telescope, shown in this artist's concept, will be able to confirm planets detected by gravitational waves in binary white dwarf systems located within a few hundred light-years. Credit: NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center Conceptual Image Lab
Two papers published in The Astrophysical Journal detail the findings. The first, authored solely by McGrath, examines the detection method and determines the planet masses and orbital periods where it would work best. The second paper, by McGrath and co-authors, investigates how such planets could be confirmed using NASA's James Webb Space Telescope.

Related video: Possible 'hints' of life found on planet 124 light-years away in James Webb Space Telescope data (Dailymotion)
The possibility of detecting exoplanets around LISA double white dwarf systems was first proposed by Japanese astronomer Naoki Seto in 2008.

Led by ESA (European Space Agency), the LISA mission is slated for launch in the mid-2030s. As a collaborative partner, NASA is contributing the telescopes, other critical hardware, and engineering and scientific support as part of its mission to better understand how the universe works.

Planetary wobbles leave gravitational fingerprints
The LISA detection method is similar to one called radial velocity that uses light and is already employed to find exoplanets around nearby stars. As a planet orbits, its gravity tugs on its host star, imparting a small reflex motion, or wobble, onto it. This wobble causes the star's spectrum to oscillate, with spectral lines shifting from redder wavelengths as the star moves away from us to bluer ones as the star moves toward us. The shift is due to a phenomenon called the Doppler effect.

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"Something similar occurs with gravitational waves," said Scott Noble, a theoretical astrophysicist at NASA Goddard and a co-author on the confirmation paper. "By themselves, double white dwarfs will emit gravitational waves at a nearly continuous frequency. Add a sufficiently massive planet to the system, and its presence causes the binary to experience reflex motion, adding a Doppler modulation to the gravitational signal."

For LISA, this modulation will appear as sidebands, secondary signals at both higher and lower frequencies than the primary one.

McGrath likens the situation to modern radio communication, where some of the energy of a main radio carrier frequency goes into forming sidebands at adjacent upper and lower frequencies that carry messages (like voices or music). For gravitational waves, the sidebands carry messages about the presence of a planet.

Photonics Engineer Support - Ansys Lumerical Consulting - Let's Pressure-Test This
Photonics Engineer Support - Ansys Lumerical Consulting - Let's Pressure-Test This
SimuTech Group
SimuTech Group
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

A hidden population around stellar remnants
White dwarfs are the Earth-sized remnants of stars like the sun. While exoplanets have been detected around a few isolated white dwarfs, none have yet been found around a binary white dwarf, which means the LISA detections would represent a distinct new exoplanet population.

Astronomers estimate that our Milky Way galaxy contains 10 million double white dwarf systems with orbital periods of less than an hour, which generate gravitational waves in the right range for LISA to find. They expect LISA will resolve about 10,000 individual systems, as well as around 100 mixed binaries—with white dwarfs, neutron stars or black holes—orbiting in similar periods.

Webb could confirm LISA's planet detections
"Once LISA captures its first exoplanet detections, it will be critical for us to validate some of them," said Mary Anne Limbach, an assistant research scientist at the University of Michigan and a co-author on the confirmation paper. "These joint detections will allow us to establish that the gravitational signals correspond to genuine planets rather than false positives."

The team described a new method for confirming these planets by taking advantage of the Webb telescope's infrared sensitivity. A planet in a double white dwarf system produces a larger portion of infrared light than what's expected from the stars themselves. Webb's instruments can easily detect this infrared excess out to a distance of at least 500 light-years.

Using this technique for a test case system at this distance, the researchers find that Webb could detect an exoplanet about five times Jupiter's mass, while LISA could detect a sub-Jupiter exoplanet under ideal circumstances.

Measuring ripples across millions of miles
The LISA observatory will consist of a trio of spacecraft following Earth as it orbits the sun. They will form a vast triangular array stretching 1.6 million miles (2.5 million kilometers) on each side. Each spacecraft will include two telescopes that will use infrared laser beams to simultaneously transmit and receive signals between adjacent spacecraft. Through these telescopes, the spacecraft will measure minuscule changes—smaller than the width of a helium atom—in their relative distances, the signals of passing gravitational waves.

Gravitational waves were predicted by Albert Einstein's 1916 general theory of relativity and first detected by ground-based observatories in 2015. The waves form whenever massive objects accelerate, such as two stars in orbit around each other. They travel through space-time, moving at the speed of light, and are unaffected by objects they encounter along the way. These properties make them a valuable tool for probing the cosmos.

Additional NASA contributions to the mission include the laser system, devices to manage the buildup of electric charge on the proof masses, data analysis for identifying and characterizing individual gravitational wave sources, and additional scientific and engineering expertise.

More information: Casey McGrath, Receiving Exoplanet Messages with Gravitational-wave Carriers: The Gravitational-wave Radial Velocity Method, The Astrophysical Journal (2026). DOI: 10.3847/1538-4357/aea216

Casey McGrath et al., Multimessenger Exoplanets: Combining Infrared Excess and Astrometry with Gravitational-wave Detections of Double White Dwarf Exoplanets, The Astrophysical Journal (2026). DOI: 10.3847/1538-4357/aea07d

Provided by NASA

10 rumors we've investigated involving Pope Leo XIV
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 3w
We revisited our past coverage of the pope amid news that a tumor was removed from his right lung.
© Rocco Spaziani, accessed via Getty Images
In October, the Vatican announced that Pope Leo XIV had been diagnosed with lung adenocarcinoma, a common form of lung cancer, and had surgery to remove the lower lobe of his right lung. Days after undergoing surgery, Leo returned to the Vatican and said he would gradually return to his regular duties. His surgeon also said the pontiff would be allowed to go on his tour of Latin America in November.

Leo became leader of the Roman Catholic Church in May 2025. As the first pope from the United States and an anti-war advocate, Leo has featured in multiple rumors about his relationship with U.S. President Donald Trump — many of which have not been true.

For example, Snopes debunked a false claim that Leo said Trump was "destroying democracy" to stay out of prison. We also reported on an AI-generated false claim that Leo delivered a "fiery sermon" criticizing the U.S. president. 

For further reading, we compiled 18 rumors about Leo that we covered from 2025 to early 2026. Below is a collection of our most recent coverage of the pope.

Mic Mini Compact Wireless Microphone System (1x Transmitters, 1x Receiver)

for Camera & Smartphone (2.4 GHz)
With an ultracompact form factor and wide device compatibility, the DJI Mic Mini is designed to help content creators record improved audio in their next vlog, livestream, or social media video. The system can communicate to your DSLR/mirrorless camera or iOS/Android smartphone via wired or wireless connectivity. It can record clear and reliable audio from an extended distance of 1312' via miniature clip-on omnidirectional mic/transmitter. The tiny receiver can be mounted on your camera or plugged directly into the Lighting/USB port of a smartphone or laptop using the USB-C port or a separately available mobile phone adapter.

Live Science

Gravity can reconnect like the sun’s magnetic field, a fresh look at Einstein’s equations reveals
Story by Andrey Feldman • 2d • 4 min read

Magnetic reconnection — the process behind the sun's most violent outbursts — may have a counterpart in gravity.

According to a new theoretical study, sheet-like structures in the gravitational field can break apart and reconnect in new arrangements, much like the magnetic field lines that unleash solar flares.

How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Built entirely on Albert Einstein's theory of general relativity, the study spells out when this "gravitational reconnection" can happen. It also offers a way to measure how fast the theoretical reconnection proceeds, and suggests a change to how gravity and matter trade energy. The process could even play a role in black hole collisions, the researchers suggest.

From solar flares to space-time
Magnetic field lines are normally "frozen" into plasma — the hot, electrically charged gas that makes up stars — and carried along wherever it flows. The lines can be stretched and twisted, but not cut. Where this rule breaks down, they can snap and rejoin in a process called magnetic reconnection, releasing pent-up energy in explosive bursts such as solar flares.

Related video: Scientists cannot explain weak gravity unless another dimension exists (Sideprojects)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sideprojects
Scientists cannot explain weak gravity unless another dimension exists
There's a lot of bizarre theories
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
15:02
0
"In plasma physics, reconnection has become an essential part of our understanding of how magnetic fields reorganize and exchange energy with matter," Luca Comisso, an astrophysicist at Columbia University and first author of the study, told Live Science via email. "Could something similar be true for gravity?"

"Gravity and magnetism describe very different aspects of nature, so there's no obvious reason to expect them to behave alike," Comisso said. "But when we express Einstein's equations in a particular mathematical form, some of the same principles that govern magnetic fields also emerge in gravity. We wanted to understand whether those similarities extended to reconnection itself."

Comisso and study co-author Felipe Asenjo, a physicist at Adolfo Ibáñez University in Chile, used a mathematical framework known as the tetrad formulation. "Einstein's theory describes gravity through the geometry of space-time," Comisso explained. But in the tetrad formulation, "certain aspects of gravity can be expressed through quantities resembling electric and magnetic fields."

Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

In a prior study published in the journal Physical Review Letters earlier this year, the researchers applied this framework to gravity. That study suggested that gravitational fields can form two-dimensional structures called gravitational field sheets. Of the sheets' two dimensions, one stretches through space and the other through time — so each sheet is the trail a line leaves as time passes. A snapshot at any instant therefore catches each sheet as a line, akin to the field lines drawn around a magnet. And, like magnetic field lines frozen into a plasma, "under certain conditions, these sheets evolve without breaking or changing how they are connected," Comisso said.


An illustration depicting the sun’s magnetic field lines over an image taken by NASA’s Solar Dynamics Observatory. When magnetic loops get stretched to their limit, they snap and reconnect, triggering solar flares. (Image credit: NASA/SDO/AIA/LMSAL)
An illustration depicting the sun’s magnetic field lines over an image taken by NASA’s Solar Dynamics Observatory. When magnetic loops get stretched to their limit, they snap and reconnect, triggering solar flares. (Image credit: NASA/SDO/AIA/LMSAL)
Rewiring gravity
In the new study, published Oct. 28 in the journal Physical Review D, the researchers found that this is not always the case. "Gravitational field sheets can break and reconnect in a different arrangement, much like magnetic field lines do in plasmas," Comisso said.

The researchers showed that breaking the frozen-in rule doesn't, by itself, guarantee reconnection. Sometimes, the sheets merely slip relative to the flow that carries them, staying intact because they remain frozen into a different flow. Genuine reconnection happens only when no such alternative flow exists.

Gen AI and LLMs for Dummies - Download the Ebook - LLM and GenAI Applications
Gen AI and LLMs for Dummies - Download the Ebook - LLM and GenAI Applications
snowflake.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"We also derived equations describing how quickly this reconnection can occur and how it can affect the transfer of energy and momentum," Comisso said. In general relativity, gravity and matter can trade energy and momentum, with only their combined total conserved. The study shows that reconnection changes the local rate of that exchange.

Reconnection also alters gravitational helicity, the gravitational counterpart of a quantity that measures how twisted and interlinked magnetic field lines are. Frozen-in sheets keep their helicity fixed, but reconnection can create or destroy it.

The findings don't require new physics. "Everything follows from Einstein's general relativity," Comisso said.

"Einstein developed his theory more than a century ago, and it has passed an extraordinary number of experimental tests," he added. "Yet even after more than a century, there are still new insights to be gained from those same equations."

Clues from colliding black holes
What could trigger gravitational reconnection in nature remains an open question. Among the candidates the researchers suggested are the singularities thought to lurk at the hearts of black holes and the combined effect of gravitational ripples that are too small to track individually. Quantum effects linked to Hawking radiation ‪—‬ the faint glow that black holes are predicted to emit ‪—‬ could also play a part.

"We're still at the beginning, but one of the most interesting possibilities is that gravitational reconnection could play a role in extreme events, such as the merger of two black holes or the collision of neutron stars," Comisso said. "These are environments where gravity is extremely strong and the geometry of spacetime changes dramatically."

Such collisions send out ripples in space-time called gravitational waves, which detectors such as LIGO (the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory) have been recording since 2015. "We'd like to understand whether gravitational reconnection could contribute to transferring energy, and whether it might leave a recognizable signature in gravitational waves," Comisso added. "We don't know the answers yet, but these are questions that we can now begin to investigate."

Are US stores selling 3D-printed salmon manufactured by Israeli company?
Story by Emery Winter • 3w • 4 min read


Close up photo of a raw salmon steak marinated in plate garnish with a lettuce leaf and a wedge of lime. Roberto Machado Noa/LightRocket via Getty Images
Close up photo of a raw salmon steak marinated in plate garnish with a lettuce leaf and a wedge of lime. Roberto Machado Noa/LightRocket via Getty Images

Claim:

Some grocery stores in the U.S. are selling 3D-printed salmon meat manufactured by an Israeli company.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

In early October, some social media posts spread the claim that an Israeli company manufactures 3D-printed salmon meat and sells it at U.S. grocery stores. 

For example, a YouTube video (archived) claimed, "the first 3D-printed salmon just hit U.S. grocery shelves." It then explained the salmon was manufactured by an Israeli company called Steakholder Foods and that the product is not required to have a label indicating it was 3D-printed on its packaging. It also noted that the salmon contained a chemical used in laxatives.


Users also shared the claim on Facebook (archived) and Instagram (archived). Snopes readers sent emails asking whether it was true.

In short, on Sept. 3, Israeli company Steakholder Foods, which specializes in making 3D-printing technology for plant-based meats, announced the launch of its 3D-printed, plant-based meat and fish products, including salmon, in Northeastern U.S. retail stores under the brand name Perfecta. 

Therefore, we're rating this claim true.

Snopes has already fact-checked a claim about Steakholder Foods' 3D-printed meat hitting grocery-store shelves. That claim and the claim about salmon came from the same product announcement and thus share much of the same research.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

It's not clear which grocery stores or retailers have started selling or stocking the products. Snopes reached out to Steakholders Foods via a contact form on its website for more information. The company has not replied to Snopes' prior requests for information.

According to Steakholder Foods, the company uses plant-based ingredients and cultivates cells to create what ultimately becomes the 3D-printed meat.

Scientists just found millions of ancient batteries hidden deep beneath the Pacific in total darkness
Story by Arezki Amiri • 2h • 5 min read


The Ocean Floor Is Covered In Ancient Natural ‘batteries’ | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
The Ocean Floor Is Covered In Ancient Natural ‘batteries’ | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
In 2012, a scientific instrument descended 4,000 meters into the Pacific Ocean and recorded something its operator had no reason to expect. Instead of declining as organisms consumed it, the oxygen concentration in the surrounding water appeared to rise. At that depth, sunlight cannot penetrate, leaving no possibility of ordinary photosynthesis.

Quantum Computing Providers - Real-World Quantum Results - Book a Quantum Briefing
Quantum Computing Providers - Real-World Quantum Results - Book a Quantum Briefing
dwavequantum.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Andrew Sweetman, a deep-sea ecologist at the Scottish Association for Marine Science, initially suspected an equipment malfunction. His sensors seemed to be reporting the opposite of what years of ocean research had taught him to expect. Yet the unusual readings kept appearing on subsequent expeditions, even when he changed the instruments and checked the oxygen concentrations using chemical measurements.

Nearly a decade later, a television documentary playing in a São Paulo hotel bar offered him a possible explanation. It described the metallic rocks scattered across the deep Pacific seabed as a “battery in a rock.” Sweetman began wondering whether those ancient deposits could produce electricity and help generate oxygen without sunlight, a phenomenon researchers call dark oxygen.

The Oxygen Readings That Refused to Disappear
The observations came from the Clarion-Clipperton Zone, an enormous stretch of seabed between Hawaii and Mexico. Its plains lie thousands of meters below the surface, where temperatures hover near freezing and the water is perpetually dark.

Sweetman’s instruments were designed to measure seafloor respiration. Normally, animals and microorganisms consume dissolved oxygen as they break down organic matter. But the readings from this region suggested an unexpected addition of oxygen to the water.

Related video: Scientists discovered a hidden biosphere 10 km below Earth - it changes everything (Astrum)

Astrum
Scientists discovered a hidden biosphere 10 km below Earth - it changes everything
The measurements eventually became difficult to dismiss. In the team’s 2024 study published in Nature Geoscience, 25 seafloor chamber experiments began with average oxygen concentrations of approximately 185 micromoles per liter. Some subsequently reached concentrations as high as 819 micromoles per liter within 47 hours.


Polymetallic nodules scattered across the Pacific seabed. Credit: ROV KIEL 6000 / GEOMAR
Polymetallic nodules scattered across the Pacific seabed. Credit: ROV KIEL 6000 / GEOMAR
© Daily Galaxy US
The researchers also performed separate laboratory experiments, including tests with filtered seawater and chemical oxygen measurements. They argued that the combined results were difficult to explain through sensor errors or oxygen entering from outside the experimental chambers.

Governance for Clinical AI - Healthcare AI Observability - Monitor Healthcare AI Agents
Governance for Clinical AI - Healthcare AI Observability - Monitor Healthcare AI Agents
fiddler.ai
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

What made the location especially intriguing was the abundance of polymetallic nodules covering its sediment. These rounded deposits contain manganese and iron oxides, along with valuable metals such as cobalt, nickel and copper. Sweetman suspected that their unusual chemistry might be connected to the oxygen measurements.

A $50 Voltmeter Revealed an Unexpected Electrical Potential
After watching the documentary in November 2022, Sweetman contacted Franz Geiger, an electrochemist at Northwestern University. Geiger had previously investigated how thin metal layers interact with flowing saltwater to generate tiny electrical potentials.

He was skeptical that ocean-floor nodules could produce enough electricity to drive a chemical reaction. His earlier experiments had detected only a few millivolts, far below what would normally be needed to split water molecules.

B Cell Isolation and Expansion - STEMCELL Technologies - Methods and Products
B Cell Isolation and Expansion - STEMCELL Technologies - Methods and Products
stemcell.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

When Geiger began examining the nodules, however, the electrical readings proved much larger than anticipated. According to Popular Mechanics, he had expected to need highly sensitive equipment but found that an ordinary $50 voltmeter could measure the potentials.

The research team reported values reaching 0.95 volts at points on individual nodules.


A real polymetallic nodule photographed during the Northwestern research coverage. Credit: Franz Geiger / Northwestern University
A real polymetallic nodule photographed during the Northwestern research coverage. Credit: Franz Geiger / Northwestern University
© Daily Galaxy US
That was significant, although it did not establish that the rocks were actually producing oxygen. Electrolysis, the process of splitting water into hydrogen and oxygen using electricity, requires a theoretical minimum of approximately 1.23 volts, with additional energy needed under practical conditions.

Sweetman proposed that different regions of a nodule’s surface, or several nodules in electrical contact, might form a more complicated natural electrochemical system. The measured voltage was not sufficient by itself to demonstrate this mechanism, but it gave the researchers a testable explanation for their earlier observations.

Natural Batteries Built Over Millions of Years
Polymetallic nodules are among the slowest-growing geological formations on Earth. Their layers accumulate from dissolved metals in seawater and chemical processes involving seabed sediments, sometimes adding only a few millimeters over a million years.

They generally begin forming around a small piece of debris. As additional minerals accumulate, the nodules develop concentric layers resembling the rings of a tree. Geiger has described finding an ancient shark tooth inside one specimen, illustrating how long some of these deposits can preserve material from the past.

Changes in the surrounding water and sediment chemistry influence the composition of successive layers. One may contain more manganese, while another is relatively enriched in nickel or cobalt. Such differences interested Geiger because electrochemical reactions can involve materials with different chemical properties.


A deep-sea robotic arm holding a mineral nodule supporting marine life. Credit: GEOMAR
A deep-sea robotic arm holding a mineral nodule supporting marine life. Credit: GEOMAR
© Daily Galaxy US
Still, a layered metallic rock is not automatically a functioning battery. Researchers must establish both a sustained source of electrical energy and the chemical reactions responsible for any oxygen production.

Not all nodules have shown the same electrical behavior. The original researchers suggested that differences in mineral composition, surface chemistry and geological history might help explain why some appeared more active than others.

The question also matters beyond laboratory chemistry. These nodules are sought for metals used in batteries and other technologies. If they provide an oxygen source for deep-sea ecosystems, removing them could have consequences that current environmental assessments do not fully capture. That ecological role, however, remains unproven.

New Deep-Sea Instruments Will Test the Theory
The proposed discovery has attracted substantial criticism. Some scientists argue that the reported oxygen increases could result from experimental contamination or measurement artifacts rather than a previously unknown natural process. A reanalysis updated in September 2026 found no statistically significant relationship between nodule surface area and the oxygen increases in the examined experiments.

The original Nature Geoscience paper also received an editor’s note in April 2026 acknowledging concerns under review. Sweetman has defended the findings and continues to investigate the proposed mechanism.

The next phase involves equipment built specifically to answer questions the original instruments could not. Through a project supported by the Nippon Foundation’s Dark Oxygen Research Initiative, scientists have developed two landers, Alisa and Kaia, capable of operating at depths reaching 11 kilometers.

The instruments can measure oxygen alongside hydrogen, pH and other environmental conditions. Detecting hydrogen together with oxygen could provide important clues about electrolysis, although investigators would still need to establish where the gases originated.

By September 2026, Kaia had been shipped to the departure port for a planned expedition to the Clarion-Clipperton Zone. The new measurements are intended to determine whether the oxygen increases can be reproduced and whether the nodules themselves are responsible.

The United States Department of Agriculture is warning consumers to stay away from certain breakfast products sold at workplaces and in vending machines.

In a public health alert issued on Nov. 14, the USDA's Food Safety and Inspection Service (FSIS) said several ready-to-eat foods have been impacted by a recall of biscuits made by C.H. Guenther & Son. Products like breakfast sandwiches that use the biscuits could be contaminated with listeria, the agency said.

Listeria is a harmful bacterium that can cause serious and even fatal illness in some people, including children, older people and pregnant people. While it's most dangerous to certain groups, listeria can sicken anyone, so FSIS advises against eating the affected products.

As of Nov. 14, FSIS had only one biscuit product on its health alert. However, it expects to add more recalls as progress. The ready-to-eat biscuits were sold in vending machines in six states, including Arkansas, Louisiana, Mississippi, Oklahoma, Tennessee and Texas.

Physicists bridge worlds of quantum matter
Story by Marietta Fuhrmann-Koch • 10mo • 3 min read


Illustration of the transition from a static impurity (left) that disrupts its environment completely, to a mobile impurity (right) whose motion restores order through the emergence of a quasiparticle. Credit: Eugen Dizer (generated with AI)
Illustration of the transition from a static impurity (left) that disrupts its environment completely, to a mobile impurity (right) whose motion restores order through the emergence of a quasiparticle. Credit: Eugen Dizer (generated with AI)
A new unified theory connects two fundamental domains of modern quantum physics: It joins two opposite views of how a single exotic particle behaves in a many-body system, namely as a mobile or static impurity among a large number of fermions, a so-called Fermi sea.

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Get Your Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
Get Your Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
splunk.com
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

This new theoretical framework was developed at the Institute for Theoretical Physics of Heidelberg University. It describes the emergence of what is known as quasiparticles and furnishes a connection between two different quantum states that, according to the Heidelberg researchers, will have far-reaching implications for current quantum matter experiments.

Contrasting models of impurity behavior
Differing views prevail in quantum many-body physics on how impurities, i.e., exotic electrons or atoms, behave among many other particles. According to the established quasiparticle model, a single particle moves through a sea of fermions, such as electrons, protons, or neutrons, and interacts with its neighbors. It drags these surrounding particles along, forming a new composite object known as a Fermi polaron, a quasiparticle that behaves like an individual particle but emerges from the coordinated movement of the impurity and the particles interacting with it.

Related video: Quantum discovery at MIT surprises physicists (WooGlobe)
View on Watch
View on Watch

WooGlobe
Quantum discovery at MIT surprises physicists
Current Time 
/
Duration 
100
The quasiparticle model has become a cornerstone for understanding strongly interacting systems, from cold atomic gases to solid-state and nuclear matter, as Eugen Dizer, a doctoral candidate at the Institute for Theoretical Physics at Heidelberg University, explains.

Standing in contrast is a phenomenon known as Anderson's orthogonality catastrophe. It occurs when an impurity is extremely heavy and effectively immobile, which changes the many-body system dramatically. The wave functions of the fermions are modified so much that they completely lose their original character and form a complex background that prevents coordinated movement and thus the emergence of quasiparticles.

A new theoretical framework emerges
For decades, physicists have lacked a theory connecting these two states. With the help of various analytical methods, the Heidelberg researchers have succeeded in harmonizing these two descriptions of mobile and static impurities in quantum systems. Their paper is published in Physical Review Letters.

This Color-Changing Dragon Mug Could Be the Coolest Christmas Gift
This Color-Changing Dragon Mug Could Be the Coolest Christmas Gift
Z_ib_ally
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"The theoretical framework we developed explains how quasiparticles emerge in systems with an extremely heavy impurity, connecting two paradigms that have long been treated separately," explains Dizer, a member of the Quantum Matter Theory working group led by Prof. Dr. Richard Schmidt.

Underlying the new theory is the finding that even very heavy impurities perform slight motions when their environment adjusts. This opens an energy gap, allowing quasiparticles to emerge from the complex, strongly correlated background. The Heidelberg researchers showed that this mechanism naturally explains the transition from so-called polaronic to molecular quantum states.

Implications for future research and experiments
According to Prof. Schmidt, these latest research results provide a powerful description of impurities that can be extended to different spatial dimensions and various types of interactions. "Our research not only advances the theoretical understanding of quantum impurities but is also directly relevant for ongoing experiments with ultracold atomic gases, two-dimensional materials, and novel semiconductors," adds the Heidelberg physicist.

More information: Xin Chen et al, Mass-Gap Description of Heavy Impurities in Fermi Gases, Physical Review Letters (2025). DOI: 10.1103/h2f7-dhjh. On arXiv: DOI: 10.48550/arxiv.2507.15957

Provided by Heidelberg University

Musk deleted 'Vote red today!' post after Democrats' midterm gains
Story by Jordan Liles • 2w • 3 min read


Tech entrepreneur Elon Musk sits at a table with clear beverage glasses and flowers, in front of a microphone, while wearing a dark suit, white collared shirt and dark tie, with a closed-mouth facial expression and people in suits and ties standing behind him. An X post that reads, 
Tech entrepreneur Elon Musk sits at a table with clear beverage glasses and flowers, in front of a microphone, while wearing a dark suit, white collared shirt and dark tie, with a closed-mouth facial expression and people in suits and ties standing behind him. An X post that reads,

Claim:

A screen-recorded video authentically shows an Election Day X post by Elon Musk reading "Vote red today!" that he, or someone managing his account, deleted after Republicans' 2026 midterm election losses.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

The Nov. 3 U.S. midterm elections saw major gains for Democrats both in the House and Senate, including key Senate victories in Michigan, South Carolina and Texas. News media outlets reported the results as a sweeping rebuke of President Donald Trump's administration, with vote tallies exhibiting sizable differences from his second-term victory in 2024.

In the hours after the election results began coming in, some people began claiming a screen-recorded video showed that tech entrepreneur Elon Musk — who reportedly contributed $474 million to support Republicans in the midterms — had deleted his post from Election Day that read, "Vote red today!"

For example, one X user's popular post featured the screen recording showing the post still loaded on the person's device but not properly displaying after refreshing Musk's feed. That clip appeared to originate from a different user, with many (archived) others (archived) reposting (archived) the content.


Others shared (archived) a screenshot with a message reading, "The post you are trying to reply to has been deleted or is not visible to you."

Musk deleted 'Vote red today!' post after Democrats' midterm gains
(@PollTracker2024 via X)

In sum, Musk, or another person who manages his X account, truly deleted the post reading, "Vote red today!"

While knowledgeable users can create fake screenshots and videos, we found definitive proof that the post once existed. Someone archived the post prior to its removal. As of that archiving, the post had nearly 12 million views.

Attempts to access the original post displayed the message, "Hmm...this page doesn't exist. Try searching for something else."

We also confirmed firsthand that DuckDuckGo search results still displayed a link to the deleted post, which we further archived.
