Morning Overview
Does the universe hide extra dimensions in plain sight?
Story by Alexander Clark • 11mo • 8 min read


Does the universe hide extra dimensions in plain sight?
Does the universe hide extra dimensions in plain sight?
For more than a century, physicists have suspected that the familiar three dimensions of space and one of time might be only a sliver of reality. Extra dimensions, if they exist, would not just be a science‑fiction flourish but a structural feature of the cosmos that could help explain gravity, quantum mechanics and even why the universe looks the way it does. The puzzle is whether those hidden directions are forever beyond reach or quietly shaping everything we see.

I want to trace how serious researchers have tried to smuggle extra dimensions into the laws of nature, why so many are willing to bet on ideas that have not yet left a trace in the lab, and how new experiments are starting to probe whether those unseen directions are real. The result is a story in which bold mathematics, powerful machines and a stubborn lack of evidence all collide.

Why physicists keep reaching for extra dimensions
The modern push toward extra dimensions starts from a problem, not a fantasy: our best theory of gravity and our best theory of particles do not fit together. General relativity describes the geometry of space‑time, while quantum field theory governs the subatomic world, and a consistent theory of quantum gravity would have to reconcile these two pillars without breaking the precision tests each has already passed. That tension is what drives theorists to consider radical extensions of space and time rather than simple tweaks to existing equations.

In that context, it is striking that Many physicists are willing to tolerate the current lack of experimental proof for extra dimensions because the framework that uses them, string theory, promises a single mathematical language for all forces, including gravity on the largest scales. In that view, the price of adding unseen directions is justified by the payoff of a unified description of nature, even if the supporting evidence so far is entirely theoretical.

From strings to M‑theory: how many dimensions are on the table?
Once I follow the logic of string theory, the dimensional stakes rise quickly. In its simplest form, the theory replaces point‑like particles with tiny vibrating filaments, and the equations only make sense if those strings move in a space‑time with more than four dimensions. One overview of the field notes that Conclusion String theory aims to unify all fundamental interactions, which is why its practitioners accept such a counterintuitive starting point.

As the mathematics evolved, several versions of string theory were folded into a broader framework called M‑theory, which treats strings as part of a family of higher‑dimensional objects and pushes the total to ten or eleven dimensions. A popular explanation of these ideas describes how Hidden dimensions and Parallel realities emerge naturally from the geometry of M‑theory, while a discussion of The Grand Design emphasizes that Stephen Hawking and Leonard Mlodinow treated M‑theory as the leading candidate for a complete description of a multiverse built from such extra directions.

What it means to live in a world of strings and branes
To make sense of these abstractions, I find it helpful to start with the basic shift in what counts as a fundamental building block. Instead of indivisible points, string theory proposes that everything is made of tiny, one‑dimensional strands whose different vibrational patterns show up as different particles. A technical review puts it bluntly that At the heart of string theory lies this replacement of point‑like entities with extended objects, and that Instead of particles we should picture tiny vibrating strings.

Once strings are allowed, the theory also admits higher‑dimensional surfaces called branes, which can span several of the extra directions and host entire universes. A detailed account of these developments notes that Mariño and others have used new mathematical tools to study nonperturbative effects in theories composed of extended objects such as strings, while a separate summary explains that in string theory everything in the universe is made of loops of energy vibrating in a space‑time with more than the familiar three dimensions of space and one of time.

How extra dimensions can be “hidden” from everyday life
If extra dimensions are real, the obvious question is why we do not bump into them. The standard answer is that they could be compactified, curled up so tightly that only very small or very energetic probes would notice them. One explanation of string theory’s geometry stresses that But for the math to work there have to be more than four dimensions, and that these extra directions can be so constrained that they are effectively invisible in ordinary experiments.

Cosmologists have extended that idea to the shape of the universe itself, suggesting that the extra directions might be rolled up into tiny loops embedded in a larger cosmic structure. A discussion of the universe’s global geometry describes how The extra dimensions are imagined as submicroscopic loops, like threads in an uncut carpet pile, so that we do not notice them unless we zoom in to a very small scale, while a separate analysis of quantum fields notes that And the answer for any successful description of nature is that additional dimensions have to be super, super tiny.

The long history of compact dimensions
Extra dimensions may sound like a late‑twentieth‑century invention, but the idea of compact directions dates back about a century. In the 1920s, theorists tried to unify gravity and electromagnetism by adding a small circular dimension to space‑time, effectively wrapping one coordinate into a loop. A modern explainer on gravity and higher dimensions notes that But the idea of compact extra dimensions is much older than string theory and credits Oscar Klein with one of the first attempts to use them to unify electromagnetism with gravity.

That early work has become part of the standard backstory for today’s higher‑dimensional models. A report on the search for such effects notes that The idea of extra dimensions dates back to at least the 1920s, when Oskar Klein built on earlier work to try to unite the forces of electromagnetism and gravity. That historical continuity matters, because it shows that extra dimensions were introduced to solve concrete physics problems long before they became a staple of speculative fiction.

Braneworlds and the possibility of parallel universes
In more recent decades, theorists have pushed the brane concept into full‑blown cosmological models. In these scenarios, our observable universe is a three‑dimensional membrane floating in a higher‑dimensional bulk, with gravity and perhaps other fields leaking into the extra directions. A technical overview notes that Braneworld models have become a key framework in modern theoretical physics, offering promising ways to address hierarchy problems and the unification of forces, while a separate paper emphasizes that As an extra-dimensional theory the braneworld scenario provides an alternative framework for issues like the cosmological constant.

Some of the most imaginative versions of this picture treat branes themselves as entire universes that can collide, cycle or sit side by side. In a wide‑ranging conversation about cosmology, one physicist explains that Another idea is that there are objects called branes, short for membranes, which are higher‑dimensional versions of strings and on which a universe like ours could be confined along one of the dimensions. A more popular summary of Brane Theory puts it in everyday language, proposing that our universe is just one of many branes or parallel universes in a higher‑dimensional space, an idea that has become the subject of much research and speculation.

Can particle colliders crack open the higher dimensions?
For all their mathematical elegance, extra dimensions will remain a philosophical curiosity unless experiments can test them. High‑energy colliders are one of the few tools capable of probing the tiny scales where compact dimensions might reveal themselves, either through missing energy signatures or the production of new particles. A detailed overview of collider searches notes that The search for extra dimensions has focused on the idea that our universe could be trapped on a membrane in a higher‑dimensional space‑time and that such scenarios could be tested at high‑energy particle accelerators.

Those accelerators are not abstract. Current research using machines like the Large Hadron Collider at CERN in Geneva is explicitly aimed at pinning down the laws of physics at the smallest scales and testing whether a multiverse or extra dimensions are a possible or even necessary consequence of mathematics. At the same time, data‑analysis specialists such as Jennifer Ngadiuba, Associate Scientist, Fermi National Accelerator Laboratory Friday, Accelerating new physics searches at the LHC described on April 12, 2024 how advanced anomaly‑detection techniques are being used to sift through collisions at CERN for any hint of phenomena that standard models, without extra dimensions, cannot explain.

What current theory actually predicts about hidden dimensions
Even without a collider breakthrough, theorists have sharpened what extra dimensions would imply. One widely cited summary notes that Specifically, string theory requires a total of 10 or 11 dimensions, depending on the version, even though we do not experience them in daily life. The same discussion points out that in many theoretical models the universe is thought to have more than four dimensions, with the extra ones being compactified or hidden from our perception, a point echoed in a separate note that In many theoretical models the universe is built exactly this way.

Mathematicians and physicists have also worked out how those extra directions might effectively shrink into the physics we see. One study describes how The leading mathematical explanation of physics, which posits tiny bits of vibrating string in nine spatial dimensions, can be squeezed into three dimensions that look like the ones observed in everyday life. A broader review of the field concludes that As our understanding of these theories deepens and technological advances open new avenues for exploration, the question of extra dimensions remains one of the most exciting frontiers in science.

The experimental verdict so far: elegant, but unproven
For all the theoretical sophistication, the empirical scorecard is blunt. No experiment has yet produced a clear sign that extra dimensions exist, whether through missing energy at colliders, deviations in gravity at short distances or exotic cosmic signatures. A candid assessment of the idea notes that So while it’s a cool idea, it is currently not supported by any evidence, even if it remains fun to imagine extra dimensions adding richness to the universe hidden in plain sight.

That gap between ambition and data has not stopped theorists from refining their models or debating their meaning in public forums. In the The Theory of Everything: 2011 Asimov Debate, panelists emphasized that But when they follow the particular mathematical equations in string theory, they are led directly to the idea of extra dimensions, even if those equations have not yet been matched to experiment. For now, the universe may or may not hide extra dimensions in plain sight, but the effort to find them is already reshaping how I, and many physicists, think about what “space” really means.

Profile Picture


An uncomfortable question has loomed over France ever since student protests against school conditions morphed into riots and looting led by masked perpetrators dressed in black: Who was behind them?

Far-right leader Marine Le Pen says the rioters came from neighborhoods with large immigrant populations. Far-left firebrand Jean-Luc Mélenchon says the real problem was law enforcement, accusing authorities of brutal and chaotic tactics that fueled the violence. The French government, meanwhile, blames “ultraleft” agitators.

5X Match For Safe Water
5X Match For Safe Water
CARE
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Any attempt to get to the heart of the matter, however, immediately runs into a uniquely French form of resistance: a longstanding, deeply rooted practice of colorblindness.

In France, it is illegal for the state to collect statistics on race, ethnicity and religion. That means law enforcement can’t document whether French citizens detained in the rioting are descendants of immigrants or not. Corporations, academics and others outside the state are also heavily restricted from gathering such data and are subject to five years in prison and a €300,000 fine for breaking the rules. It’s therefore hard to factor ethnicity or creed into public discussions of the riots because, in the eyes of the French state, these factors don’t exist.

To Americans, reared on multiculturalism, hyphenated identities and entrenched racial politics, it might seem impossible to not talk about race and cultural difference. But in France, these concepts carry deep stigma, as they run counter to the country’s founding principles.

These $44 Bluelume Reading Glasses That Can See Far and Close, Smart Zoom

lua.moss

call to action icon

France considers its republic a model of Enlightenment universalism. What makes someone French isn’t ethnicity but their allegiance to universalist values—liberty, the rule of law, democracy. There is a French word for efforts to divide France along ethnic lines—communautarisme. And it is widely regarded as a precursor to ethno-religious separatism and even civil war.

The recent unrest, however, shows that these fractures already exist, regardless of whether France’s colorblind republic sees them. The protests began in the Paris banlieues, the poor outskirts where much of the country’s Muslim population resides. The public school system there has for decades been a fulcrum of France’s struggle to assimilate immigrant communities. Fights over Muslim headscarves and French secularism have spilled far beyond the classroom, dividing the country’s electorate, often along cultural and ethnic lines.


French students, teachers and education staff protested this month to demand better school conditions.
French students, teachers and education staff protested this month to demand better school conditions.
© Firas Abdullah/EPA/Shutterstock
Polls show Le Pen and Mélenchon—the candidates who embody this partition—are most likely to make the runoff in next spring’s presidential election. Candidates in the center, drowned out by the turmoil, have seen support shrink.

“The politics of identity are replacing social classes in the language of political battles,” said Gilles Kepel, a prominent researcher who has studied the emergence of ethnic enclaves in France. “Those who ostensibly deplore these new fractures as abominable are in fact benefiting from them, if not outright causing them.”

Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Science &Theory
Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Science &Theory
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

France’s universal republic, in other words, is producing the exact outcome it was designed to prevent.

The French Revolution was fought to replace an order based on bloodlines with a national identity based on civic ideals of equality, freedom and brotherhood.

To do that, revolutionary leaders aimed to overturn feudal structures and unite the mosaic of ethnicities and languages that shaped 18th century France. More than 80% of its inhabitants at the time couldn’t speak French fluently. Local vernaculars ranged from German to Gaelic, Basque, Occitan, Flemish, Yiddish, Walloon, Norman and countless shades of Patois. Schools and the military were the universalist crucibles in which the new French citizens would be forged.

As the industrial revolution brought an influx of Belgians, Italians, Swiss and Spaniards in the 19th century, France dropped all questions regarding religious beliefs or ethnic origin from its census and class became the main dividing line within French society.

In letters they sent home toward the end of World War II, Black American soldiers stationed in liberated Europe wrote about the solace they found in colorblind France. They could enter cafes, hotels, theaters and restaurants without facing any restrictions. Romantic relations between U.S. soldiers and locals seemed almost entirely free of any racial divides.

To the French, however, the War was the ultimate cautionary tale of what could happen if the state abandoned universalism. When the Vichy regime adopted its first antisemitic laws in 1940, its government actually didn’t know with certainty who fell under the statute. Its solution was to force Jewish citizens to register with the police—which almost 90% did—leading many to be deported to Nazi death camps.

Vichy was a puppet fascist regime at odds with the traditions of the Republic. Postwar governments weren’t free of contradictions either. France’s colonial rule in North Africa also made a mockery of the universalist ideals as Algeria’s Muslim population enjoyed limited political rights compared with native French settlers. Algeria’s brutal war of independence brought a violent resolution to this contradiction in 1962—and ushered in a new wave of immigration.


Algerian nationalists rebels held aloft by Muslim demonstrators in Algiers in December 1960.
Algerian nationalists rebels held aloft by Muslim demonstrators in Algiers in December 1960.
© AP
In the 1950s and 1960s, almost two million people—including Muslims, Christians and Sephardic Jews—moved from North Africa to France. The newcomers joined and soon replaced blue-collar locals in the brutalist high-rise suburbs—the banlieues—that fast-growing France was building to replace urban slums in Paris and other large cities.

The lines of French society were being redrawn, yet the state remained officially unaware of the country’s changing complexion. The shame of Jewish deportations had led postwar France to double down on the colorblind approach, passing a data protection act in 1978 that banned the collection and holding of records revealing racial origins or religious beliefs precisely because of the way it was weaponized.

Into this information void stepped Jean-Marie Le Pen, who co-founded the far-right National Front with former SS Nazi members in 1972. To him, being French was a matter of lineage. The Nazi gas chambers, he said, were “a point of detail in the history of the World War II.” Now, he said, France was being overrun by immigrants, particularly those from France’s former colonies in Africa.


Jean-Marie Le Pen gives a press conference in March 1993.
Jean-Marie Le Pen gives a press conference in March 1993.
© Michel Lipchitz/AP

Jean-Marie Le Pen leads a march with his daughter Marine in May 1985.
Jean-Marie Le Pen leads a march with his daughter Marine in May 1985.
© Herve Merliac/AP
“They will be in charge. And the French will keep their heads down, get off the sidewalks and lower their eyes,” Le Pen told Le Monde in 2003, warning that 25 million Muslims would one day live in France. A year later Le Pen told a far-right publication that people were telling him: “Monsieur Le Pen, it’s already the case right now!”

Le Pen had no data to back up his figures, but neither did his opponents in the political establishment to dispute him. The government at the time relied on researchers who estimated France’s Muslim population was between four and five million. The estimates were largely based on the number of people who emigrated from Muslim majority countries and did not precisely account for their descendants.

To many mainstream politicians, engaging in debate over cultural differences—and whether such differences played a role in the socio-economic isolation of French Muslims—smacked of racism and validated Le Pen’s rhetoric. Le Pen was fined for his comments, which French courts ruled were forms of hate speech.

“It was impossible to talk about immigration, because the moment there was a problem, and we wanted to discuss it, someone would say: ‘But you sound like Jean-Marie Le Pen. You’re talking like National Front!” said Malika Sorel-Sutter, a French essayist of Algerian origins and a member of the European Parliament.

The 21st century brought a wave of violence that placed new forms of pressure on France’s republican ideals. The first major riots erupted in banlieues across the country in 2005 in response to the deaths of two teenagers who were electrocuted while hiding from police. The newsroom of satirical magazine Charlie Hebdo, which had published cartoons of the Prophet Muhammad, was the site of a massacre in 2015 by Al-Qaeda gunmen who were French citizens born of Algerian parents. History teacher Samuel Paty was then beheaded by a Chechen refugee at his middle-school outside Paris in 2020 after he showed the Charlie Hebdo cartoons to his class as part of a lesson on free speech.


The scene outside the Charlie Hebdo newsroom after the 2015 massacre.
The scene outside the Charlie Hebdo newsroom after the 2015 massacre.
© Remy de la Mauviniere/AP
Each crisis was greeted as a wake-up call that threatened the foundations of France’s universal model. But so were any attempts to understand their root causes. A push by researchers to survey tens of thousands of people around the 2005 riots collided with the ban on collecting ethnic data. The French privacy watchdog, which vets scientific surveys before they are conducted, allowed the researchers to ask about religion, nationality and perceived discrimination but barred them from asking respondents to identify themselves by race, ethnicity or skin color.

“If there’s discrimination in terms of income levels, to fight against it we have to know who’s earning a lot and who’s making less,” said Hugues Lagrange, a sociologist who took part in the study.

President Emmanuel Macron proposed a law against “Islamist separatism” in an effort to confront radical forms of Islam that his government said fueled the Charlie Hebdo and Paty killings. But the country was immediately consumed by debates about what “Islamist separatism” even meant. The government defined it as the dominance of religious rules over state authority in some Muslim communities, drafting legislation that aimed to tighten oversight of mosques and Muslim associations. Opponents, including Mélenchon, saw it as an attempt to stigmatize French Muslims as a group and as a violation of universal values. The law eventually passed, but without any mention of Islamist separatism.


French President Emmanuel Macron during the 2026 Bastille Day military parade in Paris.
French President Emmanuel Macron during the 2026 Bastille Day military parade in Paris.
© Blondet Eliot/Abaca/ZUMA Press
Over time, politicians at the farthest ends of the political spectrum have learned how to talk around the topic of race even as they engage in identity politics.

Marine Le Pen, who took over her father’s party, has called France the “least racist” country on the planet, campaigning as a law-and-order candidate who aims to unify the nation. Her campaign’s flagship proposal, meanwhile, is a referendum that would amend the constitution to create a two-tier legal system granting different rights to citizens and noncitizens, a clear departure from universalism.

Mélenchon, meanwhile, has capitalized on support among French Muslims following his refusal to condemn Hamas as a terrorist organization and attend an antisemitism march in the wake of the Oct. 7, 2023 attacks on Israel. He won 62% of the French Muslim vote in the 2024 European elections according to exit polling.


Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc Melenchon before a political debate ahead of the France's 2027 presidential election.
Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc Melenchon before a political debate ahead of the France's 2027 presidential election.
© Blondet Eliot/Abaca/ZUMA Press
His presidential platform calls for the creation of a “Nouvelle France” that represents the “heirs of immigration.” Rather than openly embrace American-style multiculturalism, Mélenchon talks of the “creolization” of French society, a concept that recognizes cultural differences without treating them as fixed identities.

In the wake of the recent riots, Mélenchon has played a lead role in policing the speech of presidential candidates in the dwindling center.

Former Prime Minister Édouard Philippe, the political establishment’s leading presidential candidate, was recently asked to characterize the looters.


Former Prime Minister Édouard Philippe takes part in a rally against racism and to pay tribute to the victims of the racist attack that took place in the Yvelines region in La Verriere last month.
Former Prime Minister Édouard Philippe takes part in a rally against racism and to pay tribute to the victims of the racist attack that took place in the Yvelines region in La Verriere last month.
© Simon Wohlfahrt/AFP/Getty Images
“There were, in fact, a certain number—a significant number, probably—of young French people who I consider French from an immigrant background,” he said. For Philippe, the riots were a sign the French model was failing in its mission of cultural integration.

“We have to look in the mirror and ask how do we build a nation? How do we make it for those who arrived one, two or three generations ago?”

Mélenchon swiftly wrote on X that Philippe was “dividing the country” with “racialist” rhetoric.

Just days after the mid-October riots, France was confronted with the very ethnic violence it feared identity politics might produce. A gunman authorities identified as “Kevin T.” went on a shooting spree in a banlieue of Paris, killing a man of African descent and wounding seven others, including a 12-year-old girl wearing a headscarf who was shot multiple times.


People march to honor the memory of Pengo Bossio Wangi, who was killed last month after a drive-by shooting in Trappes, in the Yvelines department, near Paris.
People march to honor the memory of Pengo Bossio Wangi, who was killed last month after a drive-by shooting in Trappes, in the Yvelines department, near Paris.
© Pedro Nunes/Reuters
The banlieue he targeted, Trappes, is celebrated for producing some of the country’s most famous actors of African descent. The shooter later told police he went there looking for “Muslim, African, North African types.” When police raided his residence they found a copy of Mein Kampf and stickers that read “100% French,” which the shooter said he had designed himself.

“This is a turning point,” Macron told reporters as he visited Trappes in the aftermath, warning the public to “not fall into the trap of one camp against another.”

A giant planet just disappeared – and astronomers know why
Story by Josh Milton • 15h • 3 min read

Now you see it, now you don’t. Scientists have discovered the first known ‘disappearing planet’.

TOI-2535 b is an exoplanet – a planetary body beyond our solar system -around 5,878,606,000,000,000 miles away from Earth.

Astronomers have been watching this toasty ball of gas since 2019, until March, when it suddenly went poof.

Download Your Free Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
Download Your Free Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Now a team of astronomers at the University of California, Los Angeles, knows why TOI-2535 b pulled a vanishing act.

And no, it’s not aliens, Samuel Yee – co-author of the team’s paper published in Astrophysical Journal Letters – tells Metro. Nor has it become invisible.

Astronomers spot exoplanets when they move, or transit, in front of their stars, appearing as a speck that briefly dims the starlight.


An artist’s impression of 10 hot Jupiter exoplanets (Picture: Nasa/ESA)
An artist’s impression of 10 hot Jupiter exoplanets (Picture: Nasa/ESA)
What is TOI-2535 b like?
It’s a type of world called a hot Jupiter, a gas giant (like its namesake) that orbits very close to its host star.

TOI-2535 b does one full solar lap in just 8.25 Earth days, 10 times faster than the orbit of the closest planet to our sun, Mercury.

Transits are also how stargazers figure out what an exoplanet is made of.

Related video: Astronomers discovered a dead star glowing green after it apparently swallowed part of a planet (Anton Petrov)

As the celestial body drifts past the star, its exoplanet’s atmosphere, if it has one, is illuminated.

The gases change the colour of the starlight that reaches Earthly telescopes, allowing scientists to guess the air’s chemical composition.

TOI-2535 b’s filtered starlight shows it’s made up of hydrogen and helium like Jupiter, with surface temperatures of nearly 1,000°C.

‘This is similar to a solar eclipse, when the Moon passes in front of the Sun, blocking its light,’ Yee says.

‘The only difference is that the planet only blocks a much smaller fraction of its star’s light. In TOI-2535 b’s case, about 1%.’

Transits tend to happen like clockwork, yet those snapped by Nasa’s Transiting Exoplanet Survey Satellite were always early or late by six hours.

Over time, these transits become shallower and shorter before the exoplanet suddenly seems to evaporate into the vacuum of space.

Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Download Your Free Guide - Implementing Containers

Yee found these groovy transits suggest the planet didn’t disappear but got pulled aside by a nearby planet, whose orbit is tilted relative to TOI-2535 b.

‘The two planets’ orbits are tilting relative to each other, and their gravitational interactions cause their orbits to wobble, somewhat akin to a top,’ Yee says.

‘The planet is still there, but we no longer see it transit its star.’

Yee found these groovy transits suggest the planet didn’t disappear but got pulled aside by a nearby planet, whose orbit is tilted relative to TOI-2535 b.

‘The two planets’ orbits are tilting relative to each other, and their gravitational interactions cause their orbits to wobble, somewhat akin to a top,’ Yee says.

This is the first ‘disappearing planet’, or more officially, a formerly transiting exoplanet.

The gas giant won’t be seen for at least another 30 years, according to the UCLA team’s calculations.

But we might see TOI-2535 b’s neighbour for the first time before that, the team’s findings show.

‘Just as there are planets whose transits are “disappearing”,’ Yee adds, ‘there are also planets whose transits are “appearing”, caused by the same mechanism!’

Did Ilhan Omar marry her brother? Name dispute revives unfounded rumor
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 3w • 10 min read


Ilhan Omar stands at a podium. Alex Kormann, accessed via Getty Images.
Ilhan Omar stands at a podium. Alex Kormann, accessed via Getty Images.

The claim that U.S. Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., was married to her brother is unfounded.
The latest rumor stems from a New York Post story claiming Omar's father's name was Nur Said Elmi — not Nur Omar Mohamed — and that Omar has a sister in the United Kingdom named Leila Nur Said Elmi who allegedly lived with Omar's ex-husband Ahmed Elmi, suggesting they were all siblings. A legal motion from the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia also referred to Omar as "llhan Nur Said Elmi."
A recent New York Times report citing unnamed former and current officials said an investigation during former U.S. President Barack Obama's tenure found some evidence that Omar had entered into a fake marriage with a man suspected of being her brother, but the case stalled because the possible crime fell outside the five-year statute of limitations.
Snopes found that the claims lack evidence because they appear to be entirely circumstantial and unsubstantiated. Having possibly similar names does not prove that Omar is related to Leila Nur Said or Ahmed Elmi. Additionally, the claims did not confirm Ahmed Elmi's parentage or his relationship to Leila Nur Said. The claims also ignore Somali naming conventions in which children receive first names tied to their paternal lineage.
Snopes will update this report if additional information surfaces. We have reached out to Omar, the U.S. attorney's office and an individual believed to be Ahmed Elmi, and are waiting for responses.
For years, U.S. Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., has been the subject of claims that she committed immigration fraud by marrying her brother, which Snopes has determined were unfounded. In October, President Donald Trump's administration reopened an investigation against Omar in which it claimed to have more evidence about her family history and alleged she had committed identity fraud, leading to federal agents raiding her home in Washington, D.C.

Imperfections reveal unexpected links between light and atomic vibrations in ultrathin boron nitride
Story by Science X staff • 1w • 4 min read


Photoluminescence (left) and reflection optical microscopy (right) images of a hexagonal boron nitride flake positioned on top of a holey carbon substrate. Credit: Prabhdeep Singh, Uni Kiel
Photoluminescence (left) and reflection optical microscopy (right) images of a hexagonal boron nitride flake positioned on top of a holey carbon substrate. Credit: Prabhdeep Singh, Uni Kiel
First, one person stands up, followed by their neighbors, one after another, until a wave sweeps through the stadium. Atoms within a material move in a similar way: rather than remaining completely still in their positions, they vibrate together around their respective positions. In physics, these collective lattice vibrations are known as "phonons." Some of them can interact with light and change its energy in the process.

Master Kubernetes performance with our detailed eBook and metrics guide.
Master Kubernetes performance with our detailed eBook and metrics guide.
Datadog
Datadog
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

A research team at Kiel University (CAU) has now investigated such a process in an exceptionally thin material: hexagonal boron nitride. A study by Professor Nahid Talebi, published in the journal Advanced Functional Materials, shows that tiny irregularities in the material's atomic structure can do more than emit individual light particles. These defects also interact particularly strongly with the vibrations of the atomic lattice, potentially enabling coherent light emission.


The interaction between quantum emitters and phonons produces coherent light emission at a longer wavelength. Credit: Nahid Talebi, Uni Kiel
The interaction between quantum emitters and phonons produces coherent light emission at a longer wavelength. Credit: Nahid Talebi, Uni Kiel
Atomic defects open optical possibilities
Hexagonal boron nitride consists of boron and nitrogen atoms arranged in a honeycomb structure. It is only a few atomic layers thick. The symmetry of this structure influences how light can interact with the lattice vibrations within it. For certain vibrations, the regular arrangement prevents such an interaction.

Related video: Scientists can detect certain objects without a photon ever touching them (Aperture)
View on Watch
View on Watch
Aperture
Scientists can detect certain objects without a photon ever touching them
Video Player is loading.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
38:30
0
However, atomic order in materials is never perfect. Sometimes atoms are missing, or individual atoms occupy positions other than those intended. In hexagonal boron nitride, these point defects can act as so-called quantum emitters: they emit individual light particles, making them of interest for the development of optical and quantum technology applications.

Lattice vibrations can support coherent emission
The team led by Talebi from Kiel University's Institute of Experimental and Applied Physics investigated these quantum emitters. When quantum emitters interact with the vibrations of the atomic lattice, this can sometimes disrupt their delicate quantum properties.

In quantum physics, this is known as decoherence: the ordered relationship between quantum states is lost. The researchers in Kiel now show that the interaction with phonons can also have a different effect: it enables coherent light emission. In this process, the emitted light waves maintain a fixed relationship with one another.

Download Your Free Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
Download Your Free Guide - How To Deploy Kubernetes - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

To investigate this connection, the researchers illuminated hexagonal boron nitride with laser light and analyzed the light subsequently emitted by the material. In addition to the known light emission from the point defects, they detected another light signal: the emitted light had less energy than the incoming laser light, meaning that its wavelength—and therefore its color—had changed. This energy difference corresponds to specific phonons in the atomic lattice.

The surprising finding is that, according to the rules of crystal symmetry, these types of phonons would normally not be able to participate in this form of light scattering. However, the measurements show that the interaction between quantum emitters and phonons can nevertheless enable such coherent light emission.

Laser and electron beams reveal different processes
To narrow down the cause of the signal, the researchers also excited the hexagonal boron nitride with an electron beam. They found that both types of excitation produce the known light emission from the defects. However, the additional signal occurred only when the material was illuminated with laser light.

Sondo AI Studio - Sondo AI Generator - Create Stunning Music Videos
Sondo AI Studio - Sondo AI Generator - Create Stunning Music Videos
sondo.ai
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"The electron beam directly excites the electronic states of the defects, whereas the laser light enables an energy exchange with the lattice vibrations," explains first author Prabhdeep Singh, who carried out the optical measurements. The comparison helped the researchers distinguish between the two processes. Bharti Garg, also a first author of the study, performed the electron-beam measurements.

More defects unlock another light signal
The researchers then investigated whether the number of point defects influences which lattice vibrations interact with light. To do so, they treated the hexagonal boron nitride with argon plasma, creating additional point defects of the same type. This produced a second light signal, which the researchers attributed to the interaction between defects and another type of phonon.

The results suggest that the number of defects can be used to influence which lattice vibrations are involved in light emission.

"Our results show that point defects in a material can not only impair its optical properties but also enable new interactions. This could lead to the development of novel light sources with quantum properties," says Talebi.

In the long term, the physicist and her research group aim to find out how quantum emitters and phonons can be deliberately brought together and how their interaction can be controlled.

More information: Prabhdeep Singh et al., Defect‐Mediated Inelastic Light Scattering by Infrared Phonons in Hexagonal Boron Nitride, Advanced Functional Materials (2026). DOI: 10.1002/adfm.78847

Provided by Kiel University

This story was originally published on Phys.org.

Did Hegseth's team break one of Eisenhower's historic globes?
Story by Joey Esposito • 1mo • 4 min read


A side by side image. On the left, a color photo of President Dwight D. Eisenhower in a brown suit standing next to a globe and American flag. On the right, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth in a blue suit saluting. . Mehmet Eser/Anadolu via Getty Images/Getty/Snopes Illustration
A side by side image. On the left, a color photo of President Dwight D. Eisenhower in a brown suit standing next to a globe and American flag. On the right, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth in a blue suit saluting. . Mehmet Eser/Anadolu via Getty Images/Getty/Snopes Illustration

Claim:

U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth’s team broke one of former President Dwight D. Eisenhower’s historic globes while attempting to move it into Hegseth’s office.

Rating:

True (About this rating?)

Context:

News of the incident broke in October, but it occurred in February.

 
Rumors that a team working for U.S. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth accidentally damaged an historic artifact in the Pentagon while trying to move it into Hegseth's office circulated in October.

People on social media spread the news of the alleged blunder, purporting that Hegseth wanted one of former President Dwight. D. Eisenhower's historic globes to be rehoused in his work space. 

According to the claim, a crew attempting to move the globe dropped and damaged it.



The claim was true, Pentagon officials confirmed. 

The story first came to light with a report from The Washington Post published on Oct. 22, citing sources "familiar with the incident." The paper also noted that an unnamed source, not the Pentagon, provided the photographs included in the report. 

The Washington Post wrote that instead of "trained curators" relocating the object in question, as is standard for moving historical objects within the Pentagon, "Hegseth's staff dispatched its own team to relocate an Eisenhower globe" on Feb. 3.

According to the report, Hegseth's team "lost control of the artifact and dropped it, sending the globe crashing onto its base and impaling the fragile shell near Antarctica, according to those familiar with the incident. The resulting gash appears to be several inches wide." 

In addition, photos showed that the globe appeared to have become detached from its mount. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

An official for the Defense Department confirmed the accuracy of The Washington Post's reporting in an email to Snopes via email and added: 

An accident occurred while staff were transporting a replica of the Eisenhower globe as part of its relocation. Accidents happen, we are grateful no one was harmed, and the globe is being repaired. It is unsurprising that the Washington Post is once again dedicating time to another inconsequential non-story.

Profile Picture

Of all the Cathar castles, the one at Peyrepertuse is especially stunning - Sissoupitch/iStockphoto
Of all the Cathar castles, the one at Peyrepertuse is especially stunning - Sissoupitch/iStockphoto
Cathars, eh? If you’ve travelled in Occitanie – specifically, from around Montpellier to Toulouse, via Perpignan – you will have seen the word affixed to pretty much anything, from hotels to hairdressers. Most notably, the great castles which top peaks as France rises towards Spain are invariably termed châteaux Cathares.

5X Match For Safe Water
5X Match For Safe Water
CARE
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

You may be left wondering who or what the Cathars were. In recent decades, the topic of the Cathars’ identity has been subject to controversy. Before it was sure. Now not so much. Maybe only the French could get so frothed up about events from the 13th century.

The issue is live at the moment because the so-called châteaux Cathares are French candidates for 2026 Unesco World Heritage status. The French take this very seriously. They love labels. Much is at stake.

The usual view is that the Cathars were 13th-century gnostic heretics. Opposed to the wealth and abuses of the mainstream Catholic Church, they stripped belief back to a sort of cosmic dualism. Existence was split between good and bad. Good resided only in the spirit. Everything material, the human body and its needs included, was bad. In brief, the purpose of life was to struggle against the devilish prison of the flesh to work one’s way towards the pure spiritual world of heaven.

Related video: The medieval faith the church tried to erase (MedievalMadness)
View on Watch
View on Watch

MedievalMadness
The medieval faith the church tried to erase
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
13:51
0
The ideal Cathar rejected most of the Bible (bar the Gospels), most of the sacraments and the cross. He or she – Cathars had gender equality sorted 800 years ago – would ideally be vegetarian, celibate and poor.

‘Kill them all!’
As the usual story goes, this doctrine took hold among the peasants, bourgeois and independent nobles of what was then Languedoc. Inevitably, it also wrapped itself around the regional seigneurs’ political wish to maintain their distance from the (Catholic) French crown. With equal inevitability, the heresy truly irked Pope Innocent III. He enjoined the king of France to stamp it out. Northern French nobles signed up fast, more for the chance of getting their hands on loot and southern land than from concern for religious orthodoxy.

What became known as the Albigensian Crusade – more like four campaigns than one – turned Languedoc, from 1209, into a war zone, punctuated by atrocities. Sacking Béziers in 1209, the crusaders killed 10,000 to 20,000 inhabitants. Of those, maybe fewer than 850 were Cathars. “Kill them all!” cried the papal legate. “The Lord will recognise His own.”


In this 13th-century illustration, Raymond VII of Toulouse is depicted submitting to King Louis IX of France - Universal History Archive/Universal Images Group Editorial
In this 13th-century illustration, Raymond VII of Toulouse is depicted submitting to King Louis IX of France - Universal History Archive/Universal Images Group Editorial
Heretics everywhere were torched by the hundred. After the capture of the village of Lavaur, north-east of Toulouse, by Simon de Montfort, key crusade leader (and father of the subsequent Simon, Earl of Leicester, leader of English barons), the local lord was hanged, his wife bunged down a well, 80 knights executed and 400 Cathars burned at the stake. So it was across the region.

Download Your Free Guide - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Download Your Free Guide - Orchestration 101: Kubernetes - Kubernetes Challenges
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Gradually, the crusaders gained the upper hand, until the last few hundred Cathars found refuge 4,000ft (1,200m) up in the peak-topping fortified village of Montségur, on the northern edge of the Pyrenees. This was built, and inhabited, before the invention of vertigo. Just looking up from below makes me dizzy. Catholic authorities reckoned it another “synagogue of Satan”. They ordered it be wiped out. A siege lasting 11 months saw to that in 1244. Some 230 Cathars who refused to renounce were roasted.

Bar some death throes, that was that for Catharism. Also, effectively, for Languedoc’s independence from the French crown – an outcome which had always been the French monarchy’s main motivation.


Lastours sits amid lush Pyrenean greenery - Veronika Kovalenko/iStockphoto
Lastours sits amid lush Pyrenean greenery - Veronika Kovalenko/iStockphoto
An outcome which also explains why the heresy has been co-opted as a key element of the Occitan self-image: independent but ever-threatened, rebellious against central authority (or any authority, really), resistant on the rugby pitch as in battle, loudly colourful. It’s an alluring selling point. And if it overlooks the essence of Catharism, well, would you bang on about celibacy and asceticism if you wished to tempt people to your beaches, vineyards or hairdressers?

Song To Video AI - Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Maker
Song To Video AI - Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Maker
FlyuAI
FlyuAI
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

More crucially, perhaps, what the “usual story” overlooks is that, according to some contemporary historians, the Cathars never actually existed. Not as such, anyway. Here indeed is a cat among the pigeons.

A veneer of rebelliousness
Simply put, the claim is that no medieval records refer to “Cathars”. There were undoubtedly protests against the Catholic church’s excesses, but these were not confined to Languedoc. And there’s no record of an organised alternative (therefore heretical) church, or of a dualist system of beliefs. The amorphous protests were falsely fashioned into something far more systematic by the pope and the northern barons to justify their crusade, land grab and attachment of Languedoc to the kingdom of France. They needed a strong, threatening enemy to legitimise their marauding. So they invented one.

Catharism apparently wasn’t much mentioned until the 19th century, when brought into the spotlight by an Alsatian historian, Charles Schmidt. And only in the 1960s did the term (and story) gain widespread currency as a sort of foundational myth of Languedoc’s regional identity – at a time when it needed one. Right then, Languedoc-Roussillon was setting its stall out for tourists. A romantic veneer of rebelliousness would do no harm.


Queribus is among the most-visited of the so-called ‘Cathar castles’ - Pascal Boegli/getty
Queribus is among the most-visited of the so-called ‘Cathar castles’ - Pascal Boegli/getty
The controversy roars on. But at least one thing is sure: that the vertiginous “Cathar castles” – to which we are all directed when visiting – aren’t Cathar at all. They were once fortified, hill-cresting villages where “heretics” holed up. But, when the French kings killed off the population and took over, they had the villages demolished. They were replaced with proper, cutting-edge medieval châteaux – to keep at bay insurgent Spaniards (or, more precisely, Aragonese).

Thus, what we see today – and the object of this year’s Unesco bid – is an ensemble of rock-topping frontier castles embodying what was finest about late 13th-century military buildings. That’s the claim for the Unesco bid, and I’m with them. I’ve been round most of the seven castles – Quéribus, Puilaurens, Lastours, Montségur and, especially, Peyrepertuse. At 2,600ft, this is the most spectacular. You can barely tell where the rock ends and the castle begins. The views are astounding, over rugged and secretive mountains where, halfway to heaven, the Corbières cede to the Pyrenees.


Peyrepertuse welcomes 100,000 visitors per year - getty
Peyrepertuse welcomes 100,000 visitors per year - getty
A swirl of legend billows round the crags. Not far away is Rennes-le-Château, long an HQ for esoterics. Or, to put it another way, Dan Brown aficionados convinced that the village hosted the tomb of a Christ who survived crucifixion, fled to France, married and founded the French monarchy. Meanwhile, the nearby mountain settlement of Bugarach starred as the only place in Europe which would escape the end of the world predicted – apparently, by the Mayan calendar – for December 21, 2012. Such elements give the district a decent claim to be Europe’s Weirdo Capital.

But it’s the Cathars – or “heretics” – who lend the properly profound subtext to the soaring, jagged landscape. Whichever version of the story you favour, it matches the ambient grandeur.

How to do it
If you’ve a serious interest in the Cathar and Albigensian stories, try James McDonald’s Cathar Castle Tours. Available are small-group, seven-night, English-language tours of key sites with a fellow who knows more about Catharism than most. Included are accommodation, visits and five lunches. Excluded are travel to Cathar country, breakfasts and dinners (£3,050pp or £5,660 for two sharing).

Otherwise, you might stay in central Narbonne at the boutique, three-star Hôtel La Résidence, slotted into a 19th-century townhouse (doubles from £96). Or, just outside Narbonne, at the altogether swisher Château Capitoul – a four-star hotel flanked by top-end self-catering villas and tens of acres of land. On-site are a gastronomic restaurant and a brasserie (hotel doubles from £222, four-sleeping villa from £295).


Château Capitoul: a four-star hotel flanked by top-end self-catering villas and tens of acres of land
Château Capitoul: a four-star hotel flanked by top-end self-catering villas and tens of acres of land
In Narbonne, try dining at Ô Juste near the indoor market (mains from £21). And, while out and about among the castles, the Auberge La Batteuse in Duilhac-sous-Peyrepertuse is a simple and sustaining spot when you descend from the Peyrepertuse château (grills from £15, three-course menu £26). The Auberge du Vigneron fulfils a similar function in Cucugnan for those visiting Quéribus (mains from £16).

A 2,000-year-old Chinese tomb contained ancient Taoist manuscripts - then researchers found a different version of the sacred text
For more than 2,000 years, Laozi has been celebrated as the legendary founder of Daoism and the supposed author of the Tao Te Ching, one of ancient China's most influential philosophical texts. According to traditional accounts, he worked as an archivist during the Zhou Dynasty, challenged Confucius over his teachings, and eventually disappeared after writing his famous book at a western border crossing. However, ancient historical records contain conflicting accounts of his identity, while archaeological discoveries at Mawangdui and Guodian reveal that the Tao Te Ching developed through different manuscript traditions rather than a single clearly documented author. As Daoism evolved, Laozi was transformed from a mysterious philosopher into a divine figure whose authority was embraced by Chinese rulers and religious movements. Was Laozi a real historical teacher whose life became surrounded by legends, or did ancient China gradually construct a founder for one of its most influential philosophical traditions?

A twist between hidden dimensions may explain mass
Story by Michelle Starr • 11mo • 4 min read


A Twist Between Hidden Dimensions May Explain Mass
A Twist Between Hidden Dimensions May Explain Mass
The masses of fundamental particles such as the Z and W bosons could have arisen from the twisted geometry of hidden dimensions, a new theoretical paper has demonstrated.

The work has outlined a way to bypass the Higgs field as the source of particle masses, offering a new tool for understanding how the Higgs field itself might have emerged, as well as a possible means of addressing some of the persistent gaps in the Standard Model of particle physics.

Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
FlyuAI
FlyuAI
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

"In our picture," says theoretical physicist Richard Pinčák of the Slovak Academy of Sciences, "matter emerges from the resistance of geometry itself, not from an external field."

Related: The Higgs Boson Might Not Be The Portal to New Physics After All

The Higgs field was first proposed in the 1960s as a way to explain why fundamental particles have mass – a huge problem that had been thwarting efforts to build a consistent model of particle physics. It was thanks, in part, to the Higgs field that physicists could build the Standard Model we rely on today.


YouTube Thumbnail
YouTube Thumbnail
frameborder="0″ allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen>
Here's how it works. Imagine the Universe is filled with an invisible sticky goo. Any particles moving through the Universe are also moving through this goo, and each particle interacts with it slightly differently.

Particles that interact strongly with the goo as though wading through mud behave as "heavy", like W and Z bosons. Particles that barely interact are "light", like electrons. Photons don't interact with it at all. This interaction is known as the Higgs mechanism, and it very neatly explains particle masses.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Related video: New insights from dark matter research challenge existing black hole theories (WooGlobe)
View on Watch
View on Watch

WooGlobe
New insights from dark matter research challenge existing black hole theories
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
1:21
0
We know the Higgs field is real because its quantum ripple, the Higgs boson, was finally and very confidently discovered at the Large Hadron Collider in 2012. However, that doesn't mean that the Higgs mechanism is the whole story.

We still don't know, for example, why the Higgs field has the properties it does. Nor does the Higgs solution explain dark matter, or dark energy, or why the Higgs field even exists in the first place.

Did Ilhan Omar marry her brother? Name dispute revives unfounded rumor
Story by Nur Ibrahim • 4w • 10 min read


Ilhan Omar stands at a podium. Alex Kormann, accessed via Getty Images.
Ilhan Omar stands at a podium. Alex Kormann, accessed via Getty Images.

The claim that U.S. Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., was married to her brother is unfounded.
The latest rumor stems from a New York Post story claiming Omar's father's name was Nur Said Elmi — not Nur Omar Mohamed — and that Omar has a sister in the United Kingdom named Leila Nur Said Elmi who allegedly lived with Omar's ex-husband Ahmed Elmi, suggesting they were all siblings. A legal motion from the U.S. attorney for the District of Columbia also referred to Omar as "llhan Nur Said Elmi."
A recent New York Times report citing unnamed former and current officials said an investigation during former U.S. President Barack Obama's tenure found some evidence that Omar had entered into a fake marriage with a man suspected of being her brother, but the case stalled because the possible crime fell outside the five-year statute of limitations.
Snopes found that the claims lack evidence because they appear to be entirely circumstantial and unsubstantiated. Having possibly similar names does not prove that Omar is related to Leila Nur Said or Ahmed Elmi. Additionally, the claims did not confirm Ahmed Elmi's parentage or his relationship to Leila Nur Said. The claims also ignore Somali naming conventions in which children receive first names tied to their paternal lineage.
Snopes will update this report if additional information surfaces. We have reached out to Omar, the U.S. attorney's office and an individual believed to be Ahmed Elmi, and are waiting for responses.
For years, U.S. Rep. Ilhan Omar, D-Minn., has been the subject of claims that she committed immigration fraud by marrying her brother, which Snopes has determined were unfounded. In October, President Donald Trump's administration reopened an investigation against Omar in which it claimed to have more evidence about her family history and alleged she had committed identity fraud, leading to federal agents raiding her home in Washington, D.C.

Physicists have measured 'negative time' in the lab
Story by Howard Wiseman • 6mo • 5 min read


Credit: Pixabay/CC0 Public Domain
Credit: Pixabay/CC0 Public Domain
As Homer tells us, Odysseus made an epic journey, against the odds, from Troy to his home in Ithaca. He visited many lands, but mostly dwelt with the nymph Calypso on her island. We can imagine that his wife, Penelope, would have asked him about that particular time. Odysseus might have replied, "It was nothing. In fact, it was less than nothing. Negative five years I dwelt with Calypso. How else could I have arrived home after only ten years? If you don't believe me, ask her."

Use Cases For AI Agents - Agentic Observability E-book - 3 Agentic Obs. Capabilities
Use Cases For AI Agents - Agentic Observability E-book - 3 Agentic Obs. Capabilities
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

Quantum particles, it turns out, are just as wily as Odysseus, as we have shown in an experiment published in Physical Review Letters. Not only can their arrival time suggest that they dwelt with other particles for a negative amount of time, but if one asks those other particles, they will corroborate the story.

Photons dwelling with atoms
Our experiment used photons—quantum particles of light—and the against-the-odds journey they must undertake to pass straight through a cloud of rubidium atoms.

These atoms have a "resonance" with the photons, meaning the energy of the photon can be transferred temporarily to the atoms as an atomic excitation. This allows the photon to "dwell" in the atomic cloud for a time before being released.

Related video: Scientists built an atomic clock to find flaws in Einstein’s theory (Veritasium)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Scientists built an atomic clock to find flaws in Einstein’s theory

34:27
0
For this resonance to be effective, the photon must have a well-defined energy, matching the amount of energy required to put a rubidium atom into an excited state.

But, by a form of Heisenberg's famous uncertainty principle, if the energy of the photon is well defined, then its timing must be uncertain: the pulse of light the photon occupies must have a long duration. This means we can't know exactly when the photon enters the cloud, but we can know on average when it enters.

If a photon like this is fired into the cloud, the most likely outcome is that its energy will be transferred to the atoms, and then re-emitted as a photon traveling in a random direction. In such cases, the photon is scattered, and fails to arrive at its Ithaca.

Photon arrival times
But if the photon does make it straight through, a strange thing happens. Based on the average time when the photon enters the cloud, one can calculate the expected average time it would arrive at the far side of the cloud, assuming it travels at the speed of light (as photons usually do).

Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
FlyuAI
FlyuAI
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

What one finds is that the photon actually arrives far earlier than that. In fact, it arrives so early it appears to have spent a negative amount of time inside the cloud—to exit, on average, before it enters.

This effect has been known for decades and was observed in a 1993 experiment. But physicists had mostly decided not to take this negative time seriously.

That's because it can be explained by saying that only the very front of the long-duration pulse makes it straight through the atomic cloud, while the rest is scattered. This leads to a successful (non-scattered) photon arriving earlier than would be naively expected.

Asking the atoms
However, Aephraim Steinberg, one of the authors of that 1993 paper, was not so quick to accept this dismissal of the negative time as an artifact. In his laboratory at the University of Toronto, he wanted to find out what happened if one queried the rubidium atoms in the cloud to find out how long the photon had spent dwelling among them as an excitation.

Download Your Free Guide - Kubernetes Challenges - How To Deploy Kubernetes
Download Your Free Guide - Kubernetes Challenges - How To Deploy Kubernetes
Splunk
Splunk
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

After an initial experiment with inconclusive results, he asked me, as a quantum theorist, for help in working out what to expect.

When we talk of querying the atoms, what this means in practice is continuously making a measurement on the atoms while the photon is passing through the cloud, to probe whether the photon's energy is currently dwelling there. But there is a subtlety here: measurements in quantum physics inevitably disturb the system being measured.

If we were to make a precise measurement of whether the photon is dwelling in the atoms at each instant of time, we would prevent the atoms from interacting with the photon. It is as if, merely by watching Calypso closely, we would stop her getting her hands on Odysseus (or vice versa). This is the well-known quantum Zeno effect, which would destroy the very phenomenon we want to study.

Our experiment
The solution is to make, instead, a very imprecise (but still very accurately calibrated) measurement. That is the price paid to keep the disturbance negligible. Specifically, we fired a weak laser beam—unrelated to the single photon pulse—through the cloud of atoms, and measured small changes in the phase of the beam's light to probe whether the atoms were excited.

Any single run of the experiment gives only a very rough indication of whether the photon dwelt in the atoms, but averaging millions of runs yields an accurate dwell time.

Amazingly, the result of this weak measurement of dwell time, when the photon goes straight through the cloud, exactly equals the negative time suggested by the photons' average arrival time. Prior to our work, no-one suspected that these two times, measured in entirely different ways, would be equal.

Crucially, the negative value of the weakly measured dwell time cannot be explained by imagining that only the front of the photon's pulse gets through, unlike the time inferred from the arrival time.

So what does this all mean? Is a time machine just around the corner?

Sadly, no. Our experiment is fully explained by standard physics.

But it does show that negative dwell time is not an artifact. However paradoxical it may seem, it has a directly measurable effect on the atomic cloud that the photon traverses. And it reminds us that there are still lands to discover on the odyssey that is quantum research.

More information: Daniela Angulo et al, Experimental Observation of Negative Weak Values for the Time Atoms Spend in the Excited State as a Photon Is Transmitted, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/gjfq-k9dv

Did DOGE lay off 400 FAA technicians responsible for maintaining critical navigational systems?
Story by Aleksandra Śliż-Wrona • 5d • 8 min read


U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy with an airplane in the background and a DOGE logo on the right. doge.gov, Kevin Dietsch and Hartmut Kosig, accessed via Getty Images and Canva.com.
U.S. Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy with an airplane in the background and a DOGE logo on the right. doge.gov, Kevin Dietsch and Hartmut Kosig, accessed via Getty Images and Canva.com.

Claim:

The Department of Government Efficiency laid off 400 Federal Aviation Administration technicians responsible for maintaining and upgrading critical air navigation systems.

Rating:

Mostly False (About this rating?)

What's True:

The FAA fired several hundred employees in February 2025. However …

 
What's False:

DOGE itself did not have the authority to make the firings. In all, 352 probationary employees — meaning those in their first year — were let go, according to the FAA; at least 132 were reinstated the following month, according to a union representing them. According to the FAA, no airway transportation systems specialists, who maintain critical navigation systems, were among those dismissed.

 
In mid-November, following a nationwide ground stop ordered by the Federal Aviation Administration, social media users shared claims that the Department of Government Efficiency, a Trump administration initiative to cut government spending and jobs, laid off hundreds of technicians responsible for maintaining and upgrading critical air navigation systems. 

"DOGE laid off 400 technicians in charge of maintaining and upgrading critical FAA navigational systems," one Facebook post (archived) read.


Did DOGE lay off 400 FAA technicians responsible for maintaining critical navigational systems?
Did DOGE lay off 400 FAA technicians responsible for maintaining critical navigational systems?
(Facebook user Tom Riley)

Versions of the rumor spread on multiple social media platforms, including X and Facebook. Some posts suggested the alleged layoffs contributed to the Nov. 13 ground stop. Snopes readers also asked about a possible connection between the layoffs and the disruption.

In short, the FAA fired 352 probationary employees in February 2025, but they were not 400 technicians maintaining critical navigation systems, as the posts claimed. According to the FAA, no airway transportation systems specialists, who maintain such equipment, were among those dismissed. The firings did affect employees in other aviation safety and support roles, and some were later reinstated. There was no evidence that the job cuts directly contributed to the Nov. 13 ground stop. As such, we have rated this claim as mostly false.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

We have reached out to the FAA with questions about the firings and whether they affected FAA operations. We will update this article if we receive a response.

FAA fired fewer than 400 employees in February 2025
The job cuts mentioned in social media posts did happen, although the number of employees was slightly lower than social media users claimed. In February 2025, the Trump administration fired 352 probationary FAA employees, according to Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy. Of those, 132 were represented by the Professional Aviation Safety Specialists union.

Trump prepares new tariffs on China and dozens of countries. Beijing could face higher levies: report
Story by Merin Rebecca Thomas • 7h • 2 min read


Shipping containers at HHLA Container Terminal Burchardkai in Hamburg, Germany. European exporters face renewed uncertainty over U.S. trade policy.
Shipping containers at HHLA Container Terminal Burchardkai in Hamburg, Germany. European exporters face renewed uncertainty over U.S. trade policy.
© smartschwarz/Pixabay
President Donald Trump's administration is preparing to increase tariffs on imports from China and dozens of other countries as part of its efforts to protect American manufacturers from foreign competitors benefiting from government subsidies.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer could announce the findings of a months-long investigation into foreign industrial subsidies as early as this week, The Wall Street Journal reported Tuesday, citing people familiar with the plans.

China is expected to face higher duties than several other major U.S. trading partners because of administration concerns about the scale of Beijing's industrial subsidies.

Japan, South Korea and the European Union could face overall tariff rates of 15% or less under previously negotiated trade agreements, while India and Vietnam could face higher duties, the Journal reported.

The final tariff rates remain unclear, and the timing of the announcement could change. The anticipated tariffs are expected to affect most imports from the targeted economies, although products already subject to separate national security tariffs, including automobiles, steel and aluminum, could be excluded.

The Office of the U.S. Trade Representative declined to comment, while the White House did not respond to the Journal's requests for comment.

The anticipated measures follow investigations launched by Greer in March under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974 into manufacturing practices across 16 economies, including China and the European Union.

No magnets needed: Extreme voltage drives completely new kind of Hall effect
Story by Rachel Leeson • 1d • 4 min read


The concentric loops illustrate how two electronic Fermi surfaces—the momentum-space states most responsive to voltage—stretch and distort as an applied electric field increases. Dashed lines show their undisturbed equilibrium shapes, while progressively larger solid loops trace their deformation under stronger fields. Credit: Rice University/Shouvik Sur
The concentric loops illustrate how two electronic Fermi surfaces—the momentum-space states most responsive to voltage—stretch and distort as an applied electric field increases. Dashed lines show their undisturbed equilibrium shapes, while progressively larger solid loops trace their deformation under stronger fields. Credit: Rice University/Shouvik Sur
The Hall effect, a physical phenomenon in which a current arises perpendicular to a voltage applied across a material, has been of particular interest to physicists since it was discovered more than a century ago. Over time, physicists have broadened their understanding of the Hall effect, describing quantum and anomalous versions that appear under nonclassical conditions. Recently, Rice University's Qimiao Si published a paper in Physical Review Letters describing a new variant called the fully nonequilibrium response that once again expands how physicists understand the Hall effect.

Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
Quantum Information Processing - Welcome to Submit - Journal of Quantum Information
scirp.org
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

"Our work epitomizes how research often spirals upward," said Si, the Harry C. and Olga K. Wiess Professor of Physics and Astronomy, director of Rice's Extreme Quantum Materials Alliance and corresponding author on this study. "It can be traced back to a theoretical proposal for a strongly correlated topological semimetal state that my group advanced previously."

Si and collaborators at the Technical University of Vienna studied a material realization of this strongly correlated topological semimetal state. Part of their work included studying the Hall effect in this material, which both gave them key information and opened up new questions.

"The Hall measurement provided evidence for the basic tenet of the topological state," Si said. "While that validation was gratifying, the experiment turned up something puzzling on its own."

Related video: Scientists found something inside a tiny magnetic ring... and it changes everything (Veritasium)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Veritasium
Scientists found something inside a tiny magnetic ring... and it changes everything
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
35:52
0
A signal without a magnetic field
In the classical Hall effect, a voltage is run through a metal in a magnetic field and a current is generated in a direction perpendicular to the voltage. The presence of a magnetic field breaks the time-reversal symmetry (TRS), where motion run in reverse does not obey the same physical rules as the original motion. If an external magnetic field or internal magnetism is absent, researchers would expect TRS, where the same rules govern motion forward and backward in time.

In the quantum anomalous Hall effect, a voltage run through a material causes the same perpendicular current, but it does so without the presence of an external magnetic field. Instead, TRS breaking happens through the internal magnetism of the materials.

"We can measure TRS breaking by comparing voltage to current," said Shouvik Sur, a former Rice postdoctoral fellow and the first author on this study. "Generally, if the two are proportional, there will be a linear relationship and we assume there is TRS breaking. However, if the voltage is proportional to current squared, which produces a nonlinear relationship, TRS remains unbroken."

Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
Sondo Ai - Free AI Music Video Generator - Song To Video AI
FlyuAI
FlyuAI
·
Sponsored
call to action icon

The data the team looked at from the topological semimetal material showed it was generating a perpendicular current to a voltage, where the current was proportional to the voltage: an indication of TRS breaking. But this semimetal didn't have an internal magnetic field, and there was no external magnetic field applied. So why were the data showing evidence of TRS breaking?

"This is where experimentalists and theorists collaborate in an especially rewarding way," Si said. "We theorists are always happy to see experimental data in support of our ideas. But if the data revealed a further puzzle, we have the chance to build new frameworks that allow for further understandings."

Stronger voltages mimic broken symmetry
When the team considered the Hall effect of the topological semimetal state closely, they found that its quantum nonlinear anomalous Hall effect could actually have a two-stage response. In the first stage, with a weak voltage, the effect behaved as expected with the voltage proportional to the current squared. But as the voltage increased, the material could enter into a second stage, which mimicked breaking of TRS.

In that stage, the electrons temporarily acquire extra momentum due to the applied voltage, and the current becomes directly proportional to the voltage. However, the state is only temporary: Once the voltage drops back down, it returns to its former state showing TRS. Where this bend, or transition point, is, Sur explained, depends on the material in question. Some transition at a very low voltage; others at a higher voltage. They called this the fully nonequilibrium response.

"What's interesting about these results is that our understanding of the Hall effect was originally based on what we call equilibrium physics, where external forces on a system, like voltage, are rather weak," Sur said. "Now, we are looking at a system that is not in equilibrium due to a strong external force, and it is responding to changes in this force. This is a much harder thing to understand. Here, we found something that can look like magnetism in an equilibrium system, something we understand quite well, but it is not magnetism and it is not in an equilibrium system."

New support for topological semimetals
The study brought new insights into the quantum nonlinear Hall effect in a nonequilibrium state, but it also sheds new light on the physics of topological semimetals in equilibrium.

"Our work supports the existence of a strongly correlated topological semimetal state," Si said. "It demonstrates how studying nonequilibrium quantum phenomena can deepen our understanding of quantum states of matter in equilibrium."

More information: Shouvik Sur et al., Fully Nonequilibrium Hall Response from Berry Curvature, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/cj8b-4sp4. On arXiv: arxiv.org/abs/2411.16675

Anthropic falls below $83 IPO price in swift reversal after record listing
Story by Medha Singh • 6h • 3 min read
