© Thomson Reuters
By Medha Singh

Nov 24 (Reuters) - Anthropic's shares slipped below their $83 initial public offering price in early trading on Tuesday, a striking unwind just days after the Claude developer's record-breaking Nasdaq debut.

The stock was last down 5.4% at $82.78, extending a steep retreat from its $111.50 opening price on November 19.

It had closed 17% above the IPO price in its debut trading session but has since been swept up in a broader technology selloff that pushed the S&P 500 and the Dow into confirmed correction territory the previous week.

Anthropic's rapid reversal has added to concerns about stretched AI valuations and the vast sums being poured into computing infrastructure, with rising interest rates threatening to push those costs even higher. As the first frontier AI company to go public, Anthropic is widely seen as a proxy for the sector.

"If we do see a slowdown in the data-center buildout, then that obviously can impact the speed at which Anthropic can develop and therefore future revenue and therefore its valuation," said Fiona Cincotta, senior market analyst at StoneX.

Anthropic missed out on a broad Wall Street rally driven by AI-related stocks after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as chief executive on Monday.

Related video: Dark Side of Anthropic's IPO - What Wall Street Isn't Telling You & How It Changes Everything (Think School)

Anthropic is not alone among blockbuster technology IPOs in quickly surrendering early gains and falling below its offer price. SpaceX, whose $85.7 billion June listing was the world's biggest before Anthropic, took about a month to fall below its offer price.

In 2012, Facebook, now Meta Platforms, slipped below its offer price on its second trading day.

The comparisons show that a swift loss of an IPO premium is not unprecedented even for highly anticipated listings, and that an early break below the offer price is not necessarily a guide to longer-term performance.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

"Like most IPOs, it probably came in overpriced and it is now the real price discovery as to what the true value should be," said Peter Andersen, founder of Andersen Capital Management.

After touching $2 trillion in valuation on debut day, Anthropic was last trading at a $1.5 trillion valuation, which is about 40 times its sales over the last 12 months.

0:3131 secondsinterview in the hope of stopping him blurting out  his proof - which he very nearly did anyway. Oh,

Chapter 3: The Einstein birthday gift
1:461 minute, 46 secondsIt’s not an exaggeration to say that Godel and  Einstein were best friends. The aging Einstein  
1:521 minute, 52 secondsonce said that the only reason he continued  to show up at Princeton was for his walks with  
1:571 minute, 57 secondsGodel. And so when the occasion of Einstein’s  70th birthday approached, it’s no surprise that  
2:042 minutes, 4 secondsGodel enthusiastically agreed to contribute  to a book of tributes to Einstein’s work.  
2:102 minutes, 10 secondsAt first Godel thought to write a  reflection on the relationship between   Einsteinian spacetime and the ideas of the  philosopher Immanuel Kant. As was his wont,  
2:202 minutes, 20 secondsGodel fell down a rabbit hole and in the process  of writing this one essay, he came to understand  
2:262 minutes, 26 secondsgeneral relativity as well as anyone in the  world. And then he stumbled on something deeply   worrying—an inconsistency at the core of his  best friend’s theory. The rabbit hole deepened.
2:392 minutes, 39 secondsThe deadline for the essay came and went—but  so important was Godel’s contribution that the   book had to wait. When it was finally done, the  reason for Godel’s obsession became clear. He’d  
2:502 minutes, 50 secondsfound a solution to the field equation—the  core of general relativity—that proves that  
2:552 minutes, 55 secondsEinstein’s theory by itself does not guarantee  a clean chain of cause and effect that it was  
3:033 minutes, 3 secondssupposed to. He’d built a spacetime metric—a  universe—where any point in space and time  
Chapter 4: Understanding spacetime
3:103 minutes, 10 secondsthere are return loops that bring you  back to the location before you left.
3:173 minutes, 17 secondsBy the time of Godel’s discovery we already  knew of solutions to the Einstein equation  
3:223 minutes, 22 secondsthat allow time travel. But all of them  require something that can be argued is  
3:283 minutes, 28 secondsimpossible—negative energy density. So, just  add a prohibition of this impossible stuff  
3:353 minutes, 35 secondsand the broken solutions go away. This  prohibition is called the weak energy   condition. With the Einstein equation plus  the weak energy condition, a deterministic  
3:463 minutes, 46 secondsuniverse with unambiguous causal ordering  seemed guaranteed. That is until Godel. He  
3:543 minutes, 54 secondsfound a new way to time travel and to break causal  structure—no impossible ingredients required—and  
4:024 minutes, 2 secondsthe result is the Godel universe, where time  travel isn’t just possible—it’s inevitable.
4:094 minutes, 9 secondsLet’s take a trip into the Godel universe,  first by time traveling back to relativity 101.
Chapter 5: Relativity and time travel
4:164 minutes, 16 secondsIn relativity theory, space and time  are not cleanly separated—they’re   both part of a 4-dimensional object called  spacetime. Depending on motion and gravity,  
4:284 minutes, 28 secondsspace and time become mixed, trading into each  other. The classic way to depict this is with  
4:344 minutes, 34 secondsa spacetime diagram, with just one dimension  of space. Everything moves up through time,   but if you’re also moving through space you  move on a sloped line. Shallower is faster,  
4:444 minutes, 44 secondsand 45 degrees represents the speed of light.  Because nothing can travel faster than light,  
4:494 minutes, 49 secondsyou can only ever influence parts of the future  in this cone—your forward lightcone. Meanwhile,  
4:554 minutes, 55 secondsyour past lightcone represents the parts of  the past that could have had an influence on   you. No possible signal could connect  you to regions outside these cones.
5:075 minutes, 7 secondsSo you can travel or can have traveled  any path inside these cones. We call  
5:135 minutes, 13 secondsthese “time-like paths” because on these,  your motion up through time is greater   than your motion sideways through space.  Paths into the forbidden zones are called  
5:235 minutes, 23 secondsspace-like—more space than time is traversed  in these, and that, supposedly, is impossible.
5:295 minutes, 29 secondsBut let’s try anyway. Say we jump in our  rocketship and accelerate away from the Earth,   quickly reaching a large fraction of  lightspeed. Relativity tells us that  
5:395 minutes, 39 secondsmy time axis shifts relative to the direction of  the axis back on Earth. From Earth’s perspective,  
5:465 minutes, 46 secondsmy time and space coordinates get mixed—my  clock slows and my length contracts. If  
5:525 minutes, 52 secondsI actually reach the speed of light, my clock  stops, and if I can go faster … well, from the  
5:585 minutes, 58 secondsperspective of some observers my clock actually  reverses and I appear to move backwards in time.  
6:046 minutes, 4 secondsThis is equivalent to breaking free of  your light cone. Once you can do that,   the past is accessible to you. And a long time ago  we did some episodes that showed how this works.
6:176 minutes, 17 secondsThe impossibility of superluminal motion is  directly connected to the impossibility of time  
6:236 minutes, 23 secondstravel in special relativity—both are baked  in via the Lorentz transformation. This is   absolute protection of the causal structure  of spacetime in the absence of gravity.
6:346 minutes, 34 secondsGravity changes things because it bends the path  of light, so it can tip our future light cone. For  
6:426 minutes, 42 secondsexample, approaching the black hole light paths  are bent, and so our lightcone bends towards  
6:506 minutes, 50 secondsthe event horizon until our entire accessible  future lies inside the black hole. Below the  
6:586 minutes, 58 secondsevent horizon the light cone tips fully sideways,  which means the “down” direction becomes your new  
7:057 minutes, 5 secondstime coordinate. Now, if you could turn around and  keep your lightcone aligned like this you really  
7:137 minutes, 13 secondscould travel back in time. But in a black hole  you can’t turn around your future is only down.
7:197 minutes, 19 secondsWell, let me actually revise that. In a rotating  black hole—a Kerr black hole—there’s a region  
Chapter 6: Frame dragging effects
7:267 minutes, 26 secondsdeep within where angular motion becomes  timelike. Space is spinning so quickly  
7:327 minutes, 32 secondsthat a circular path gets you back to  where you started at the same time as  
7:377 minutes, 37 secondsyou left. We call this a closed timelike  curve—a CTC. It’s timelike in this case  
7:457 minutes, 45 secondsbecause you never violated relativity  by leaving your forward light cone.  
7:517 minutes, 51 secondsThese regions of the Kerr black hole may not  really even exist, and in any case it’s a   pretty useless sort of time travel, because  your stuck behind the event horizon anyway.
8:028 minutes, 2 secondsWe can force closed timelike curves into  the accessible parts of the universe by bending  
8:088 minutes, 8 secondsspacetime in various exotic ways(wormholes, warp  drives etc) but in all cases these require some  
8:148 minutes, 14 secondssort of negative energy. But then came Godel  of course. Even before Roy Kerr came up with  
8:218 minutes, 21 secondsthe rotating black hole solution, Godel realized  that rotational motion was the key to time travel.
8:298 minutes, 29 secondsThe general effect he relies on is called  frame dragging—the twisting of spacetime  
8:358 minutes, 35 secondsdue to a rotating mass. And we’ve measured  this. Gravity probe B sent a gyroscope in  
8:428 minutes, 42 secondsorbit around the Earth. A gyroscope is supposed  to always point in the same direction. In fact,  
8:488 minutes, 48 secondsGodel calls the device an “inertial compass” in  his paper. But the gyroscope in Gravity Probe B  
8:568 minutes, 56 secondsslowly swiveled from its original pointing  as it orbited due to both the gravitational  
9:029 minutes, 2 secondscurvature and the twisted spacetime—the  frame dragging caused by Earth’s rotation.  
9:099 minutes, 9 secondsThis angular shift is accompanied by a  time shift, but in the case of Earth’s  
9:159 minutes, 15 secondsframe dragging is a far too weak an effect for  that component to be measured. But in principle,   if you travel around Earth the right way  your clock slows due to the angular motion.
9:289 minutes, 28 secondsIt’s never enough to freeze time altogether  though. Even around a rapidly rotating black   hole there are no closed timelike curves on  the outside of a black hole—only deep within.
9:409 minutes, 40 secondsBut Godel realised that this frame dragging  effect could still break causality if it  
9:469 minutes, 46 secondshappens in a spacetime where the events  can add up. Now the key is to describe a  
Chapter 7: The Gödel universe
9:529 minutes, 52 secondsspacetime which has a fundamental twist to it  everywhere—all points feel frame dragging—not  
10:0010 minutesjust around a single spinning objects. We say  it has global vorticity. All spacetime points  
10:0810 minutes, 8 secondsrotate relative to neighbors—not like every  spot spinning, but rather like it's a vortex  
10:1410 minutes, 14 secondsof worldlines in an infinite 4-D spacetime that  has no center. There are other requirements—a  
10:2310 minutes, 23 secondsnegative curvature “hyperbolic” geometry is  needed to allow the center-less rotation, and  
10:3010 minutes, 30 secondsa balance of smooth positive matter and negative  dark energy to keep the universe static in size.
10:3710 minutes, 37 secondsBut tuned right, Godel showed that such a  universe can be full of closed timelike curves.
10:4510 minutes, 45 secondsLet’s see what it looks like. This saddle shape  is a representation of a 2-D hyperbolic space.  
10:5110 minutes, 51 secondsWe’re reserving the real Z direction for time so  that we can stack these planes to represent a 4-D  
10:5810 minutes, 58 secondsuniverse. Zooming in it looks flat locally, but  the negative curvature is still there. You don’t  
11:0511 minutes, 5 secondsnotice anything weird moving through this space,  even though the underlying twisting is there.   You notice this when time moves forward, and  neighboring particles follow twisted paths.
11:1911 minutes, 19 secondsFrom any point in this space, as you travel  outwards your lightcone tilts relative to  
11:2611 minutes, 26 secondsthe reference frame of your starting point. And  beyond a certain distance—the Godel horizon—it  
11:3211 minutes, 32 secondshas tilted far enough to allow travel into  the past. Or at least, to claw back some  
11:3911 minutes, 39 secondsof the time you spent traveling. But with a  carefully planned route, traveling in a loop,   you can steer your future light cone to  eventually contain your starting point.
11:5111 minutes, 51 secondsAnother way to think about it is that, by  mixing time with the angular coordinate,  
11:5611 minutes, 56 secondsyour motion in a loop takes the place of and  even reverses temporal motion. That’s right,  
12:0412 minutes, 4 secondsGodel invented the time-turner—turn enough  and you travel backwards in time. But with  
12:1112 minutes, 11 secondsrestrictions. You have to travel out beyond  the Godel horizon from your starting point,   which means you can’t just spin on  the spot and move into the past.
12:1912 minutes, 19 secondsThe time-traveling possibilities of the Godel  universe are fun, but they aren’t the real point.  
12:2612 minutes, 26 secondsThe real point is that this solution to the  Einstein equation is a counter-example to the  
12:3112 minutes, 31 secondsidea that baseline general relativity—the Einstein  equation plus the weak energy condition—are enough  
12:3712 minutes, 37 secondsto ensure sensible universes. Before this, any  valid GR spacetime could be sliced into layers of  
Chapter 8: Causality and the future
12:4612 minutes, 46 secondsconsecutive “nows”, where the exact configuration  of everything—the particles, the fields—of each of  
12:5212 minutes, 52 secondsthose slices can be used to generates the next  slice. This perfect  global determinism isn’t  
13:0213 minutes, 2 secondstrue in Godel’s universe, and the past and future  become tangled and not clearly definable. Godel  
13:1113 minutes, 11 secondsproved that general relativity doesn’t guarantee  spacetimes that have a rigid causal ordering. At  
13:1713 minutes, 17 secondsleast in some cases, like in Godel’s universe,  we can’t say whether A caused B or B caused A.
13:2613 minutes, 26 secondsWhat Godel really showed was that the prohibition  against negative energy wasn’t enough to ensure  
13:3213 minutes, 32 secondssensible universes. Others came along and  proposed new conditions. “Global Hyperbolicity”  
13:4013 minutes, 40 secondswas proposed as an explicit requirement—which  has nothing to do with hyperbolic geometries.  
13:4713 minutes, 47 secondsIt basically states that for any physically  reasonable solution to general relativity,  
13:5213 minutes, 52 secondsany constant-time slice must fully determine  the next constant time slice, and that has to  
14:0014 minutesbe true however you do that time slicing. Then  came Stephen Hawking’s Chronology Protection  
14:0714 minutes, 7 secondsConjecture, which argues that any spacetime that  allows closed timelike curves is unstable—feedback  
14:1614 minutes, 16 secondsfrom time-traveling vacuum energy will cause  the ultimate reverb and collapse the universe.
14:2414 minutes, 24 secondsSo, that’s really what Godel gave his  friend Einstein for his birthday—the   impetus for future generations of physicists  to keep working on Einstein’s greatest theory.  
14:3314 minutes, 33 secondsGodel didn’t break GR, but he showed  the rest of us where some of the few   remaining cracks are. Cracks which may, in  time, lead new Einsteins and new Godels to  
14:4414 minutes, 44 secondsdeeper theories. Really quite the birthday  gift, Godel’s time-turning spacetime.

0:000 secondsImagine two black holes falling toward each other. Space twists, time slows, the objects circle faster, the
0:088 secondssurrounding universe shakes, and then at the final moment, the geometry around them violently reorganizes.
0:1717 secondsPhysicists already knew that collision could send gravitational waves across the universe. But a new way of reading
0:2525 secondsEinstein's equations suggests something stranger may be happening inside the chaos. Gravity's connections may break,
0:3333 secondsthen reconnect in a different arrangement. And when they do, energy can move out of the gravitational field
0:4040 secondsand into matter. It sounds like somebody has taken a pair of scissors to spaceime. The real story is subtler and
0:4747 secondsmuch more useful because this idea could give physicists a new way to understand the most violent events in the universe
0:5555 secondswithout treating a supercomput as a very expensive crystal ball. The clue came from the sun. Above its surface,
1:031 minute, 3 secondsenormous loops of magnetic field are dragged, stretched, and twisted by plasma. Most of the time, those field
1:101 minute, 10 secondslines move with the plasma as if they are frozen into it. Their shape can change, but their connections remain
1:171 minute, 17 secondsintact until they don't. Under the right conditions, magnetic field lines snap into a new configuration. Energy stored
1:251 minute, 25 secondsin the field is suddenly released into particles, heat, and motion. That process, magnetic reconnection, helps
1:341 minute, 34 secondspower solar flares, and some of the most explosive events in astrophysics. Luca Kamisso has spent much of his career studying that kind of plasma physics.
1:441 minute, 44 secondsThen he and Felipe Asenho asked an outrageous question. Could gravity obey a similar hidden rule? At first, the
1:531 minute, 53 secondscomparison seems wrong. Magnetism is a force acting through a field. Einstein's gravity is the geometry of space and
2:012 minutes, 1 secondtime itself. A magnet has field lines you can sketch around it. Spacetime does not come with convenient little arrows
2:092 minutes, 9 secondssaying bend here. But Einstein's equations can be written in more than one mathematical language. The familiar
2:162 minutes, 16 secondsversion describes spacetime using a metric, a system that tells you how distances and time intervals behave in
2:242 minutes, 24 secondscurved geometry. The researchers used an equivalent formulation built from tetrds, local measuring frames attached
2:312 minutes, 31 secondsto every point in spaceime. Think of each tetrad as a tiny floating laboratory carrying its own rulers and
2:402 minutes, 40 secondsclock. Put enough of those laboratories together and they describe the same curved universe as Einstein's original
2:482 minutes, 48 secondsequations. But in this form, gravity begins to resemble the mathematics of electricity, magnetism, and moving
2:562 minutes, 56 secondsplasma. That resemblance expose the first hidden rule. Under ideal conditions, certain structures in the
3:033 minutes, 3 secondsgravitational field are frozen into the evolution of spaceime. Their connections are preserved. Gravity can bend them,
3:123 minutes, 12 secondsstretch them, fold them, and drag them through an astronomical disaster, but it cannot casually swap which parts are
3:203 minutes, 20 secondsconnected. There is one important difference. Magnetic field lines live in three-dimensional space. Gravity evolves
3:283 minutes, 28 secondsthrough four-dimensional spaceime. So the gravitational structures are not best imagined as individual strings.
3:363 minutes, 36 secondsThey are more like two-dimensional sheets threaded through space and time.
3:403 minutes, 40 secondsThese are mathematical surfaces, not cosmic wallpaper. But they give physicists a way to track the organization of the gravitational field.
3:493 minutes, 49 secondsAlmost like following the grain inside a piece of wood while the wood is being twisted. In the ideal case that grain
3:563 minutes, 56 secondsremains continuous. The new paper asks what happens when the ideal rule fails.
4:014 minutes, 1 secondThe answer is gravitational reconnection. The connectivity of those gravitational field sheets can change.
4:084 minutes, 8 secondsTwo regions that once belonged to different connections can leave the event joined in a new way. But here the story takes an important turn. Merely disturbing the field is not enough.
4:204 minutes, 20 secondsSometimes an apparent break can be removed by changing the mathematical flow used to follow the field. Is like watching traffic from the wrong moving
4:284 minutes, 28 secondscar. The lane seemed to slide sideways but the road never changed. Genuine gravitational reconnection occurs only
4:364 minutes, 36 secondswhen no such change of viewpoint can restore the original frozen connection.
4:424 minutes, 42 secondsThat distinction turns a dramatic analogy into a measurable theory. Kamiso and Asenho derived ways to calculate a
4:514 minutes, 51 secondsgravitational reconnection rate. How quickly gravitational flux changes connectivity. They also showed that
4:584 minutes, 58 secondsreconnection changes gravitational holicity, a measure of how twisted and linked the field structure is. And then
5:065 minutes, 6 secondscomes the part that gives this idea real teeth. Gravity and matter exchange energy and momentum in a new framework.
5:145 minutes, 14 secondsGravitational reconnection changes the local rate of that exchange. The geometry does not merely rearrange
5:225 minutes, 22 secondsitself like an abstract diagram. Its reorganization can affect where energy goes. That is why black holes matter.
5:315 minutes, 31 secondsWhen two black holes merge, spacetime is pushed into one of nature's most extreme states. Today, physicists predict the
5:395 minutes, 39 secondsresult by solving Einstein's equations numerically. They choose initial conditions, run enormous simulations,
5:485 minutes, 48 secondsand calculate the gravitational wave signal that instruments such as LIGO should hear. Those simulations are astonishingly successful. They are also
5:575 minutes, 57 secondsbrutally complicated. A single result can hide the underlying rule inside billions of calculations. Gravitational
6:056 minutes, 5 secondsreconnection offers a different kind of tool. Instead of asking only what did the simulation produce, physicists can
6:136 minutes, 13 secondsask which connections were allowed to survive, which ones changed, and how much energy moved when they did. That
6:206 minutes, 20 secondscould expose patterns shared by many different collisions even before every detail has been calculated. The same
6:276 minutes, 27 secondsframework may matter around neutron star mergers, collapsing stars, unresolved gravitational waves, or places where
6:356 minutes, 35 secondssingular behavior makes the ideal flow fail. It could even provide a common language for studying how quantum
6:426 minutes, 42 secondseffects or modified theories of gravity disturb spacetime's usual rules. The next step is to put the mathematics into
6:496 minutes, 49 secondsspecific cosmic events and ask what signature reconnection would leave. a change in the energy released, a pattern in matter near the collision, or a
6:586 minutes, 58 secondsfeature hidden inside a gravitational wave. That is the larger reversal. For more than a century, Einstein's equations have been treated as the
7:077 minutes, 7 secondsmachine that tells matter how to curve spaceime and spaceime how to move matter. But the equations may also
7:147 minutes, 14 secondscontain something like a wiring diagram, a hidden record of which regions of the gravitational field are connected, which connections are protected, and how that
7:237 minutes, 23 secondsprotection can fail. In ordinary conditions, gravity keeps its connections. In the universe's most
7:307 minutes, 30 secondsviolent moments, it may tear up the diagram and wire itself back together.
7:357 minutes, 35 secondsAnd somewhere inside that reconnection may be the missing rule that turns a black hole collision from a computer
7:437 minutes, 43 secondscalculation into a story we can actually understand.

Chapter 1: Gravitational Reconnection Explained
0:000 secondsGravitational reconnection gives us a new way to picture how gravity exchanges energy with matter.
0:055 secondsReports this November describe a theoretical discovery inside Albert Einstein's existing equations, with an unexpected connection to solar flares. Einstein's
0:1414 secondsequations are apparently still releasing bonus material. I want to show you what actually reconnects, why that matters, and how physicists can find something new
0:2222 secondswithout changing the laws. The payoff is a clearer picture of gravity's most extreme behavior.
0:2828 secondsThe researchers are Luca Comisso at Columbia University and Felipe A. Asenjo at Universidad Adolfo Ibáñez in Chile. Their study,
0:3636 secondsReconnection of Gravitational Fields, appeared in Physical Review D. They explored a mathematical rewriting of general relativity, Einstein's description of
0:4545 secondsgravity through curved space and time. The equations changed outfits; the physics kept its identity.
0:5151 secondsThis is Tech, Decoded. Subscribe to Science Tech News for more explanations of what discoveries actually mean. Let's start with the Sun.
0:5959 secondsThe Sun contains plasma, a gas of electrically charged particles.
1:031 minute, 3 secondsMagnetic fields thread through it, and we draw curved lines to describe their direction and connections. Picture two pairs of connected regions. During magnetic reconnection,
1:121 minute, 12 secondsthe pairing changes. Stored magnetic energy can then heat particles and accelerate them, helping power solar flares. The Sun takes rearranging its connections rather seriously.
1:221 minute, 22 secondsThat sequence matters: changing the field's organization gives physicists a mechanism for understanding where the energy goes.

Chapter 1: The unsettling predictions
0:000 secondsSomewhere right now, a satellite orbiting Earth is using Einstein's equations to correct your GPS location to within a few meters. Those same
0:099 secondsequations predicted black holes decades before we photographed one. They predicted gravitational waves a century before we detected them. By any measure,
0:1818 secondsEinstein's field equations are the most successful theory of gravity ever written. So, here is the question that should genuinely disturb you. What if
0:2525 secondsthose same equations, the ones that work so perfectly, are also predicting something that should be impossible? Not a small rounding error, not a minor
0:3434 secondsinconsistency buried in the math, but a full logical mathematically valid prediction that says the universe can
0:4141 secondscontain regions where cause and effect simply stop working. No exotic matter required, no violation of any known law
0:4848 secondsof physics, just Einstein's equations followed honestly to their conclusion.
0:5353 secondsIn 1915, when Einstein first published his general theory of relativity, he handed humanity a tool so powerful that
1:001 minutephysicists are still finding new things inside it. But power cuts both ways. The same framework that explains how time
1:071 minute, 7 secondsslows near a black hole also produces solutions, perfectly valid solutions that allow a traveler to loop back through time and arrive before they
1:151 minute, 15 secondsdeparted. Solutions where the question, did A cause B or did B cause A has no answer. These are not science fiction
1:221 minute, 22 secondsscenarios. They are peer-reviewed mathematics. And if you want to understand why some of the most rigorous minds in physics lose sleep over this,
1:301 minute, 30 secondsstay with me. Because what Einstein's equations predict about the deep structure of reality is far stranger than anything he was willing to admit.
Chapter 2: The geometry of gravity
1:381 minute, 38 secondsBefore we go deeper, we need to make sure we are standing on solid ground because the most common mistake people make when discussing general relativity
1:461 minute, 46 secondsis treating it like an upgrade to Newtonian gravity. It is not. It is something far more radical. Newton looked at gravity and said, "Masses attract each other across empty space.
1:561 minute, 56 secondsThe earth pulls the moon. The sun pulls the earth." Simple, clean, and for 200 years perfectly accurate. But Newton
2:032 minutes, 3 secondshimself admitted he had no idea how that pulling actually worked. He called it action at a distance and quietly moved on. Einstein refused to move on. What
2:122 minutes, 12 secondsgeneral relativity actually says is this. There is no such thing as a gravitational force. None. What we experience as gravity is the result of
2:202 minutes, 20 secondsmass and energy bending the fabric of spacetime itself and everything else simply following the straightest possible path through that bent
2:272 minutes, 27 secondsgeometry. The moon is not being pulled toward Earth. The moon is following the most natural unbothered path available to it through a region of spaceime that
2:352 minutes, 35 secondsEarth has curved. This is where the field equations come in. Einstein's 10 field equations describe the precise mathematical relationship between two
2:432 minutes, 43 secondsthings. the geometry of spaceime on one side and the distribution of mass and energy on the other. Change the mass, you change the geometry. Change the
2:512 minutes, 51 secondsgeometry, you change how everything moves. The two are locked together in a continuous feedback loop. Now, here is the critical detail that most
2:592 minutes, 59 secondsexplanations skip over. The equations do not tell what shape to have. They tell you what shapes are allowed given a
3:063 minutes, 6 secondsparticular distribution of matter and energy. Think of it less like a blueprint and more like a rule book. You describe the contents of your universe,
3:153 minutes, 15 secondshow much matter, how much energy, how it is distributed, and the equations tell you which space-time geometries are mathematically consistent with that
3:233 minutes, 23 secondsdescription. And this is precisely where the disturbance begins because that rule book turns out to be extraordinarily permissive. Physicists have been feeding
3:313 minutes, 31 secondsdifferent configurations of matter and energy into Einstein's equations since 1915. And the solutions that come back are not always comfortable. Some are
3:403 minutes, 40 secondselegant, some are bizarre, and a few of them describe universes that seem to violate the most basic assumptions we hold about reality. The equations do not
3:483 minutes, 48 secondsknow what is reasonable. They do not filter for comfort. They simply compute, and what they compute in certain cases should give every one of us pause. So,
Chapter 3: Three problematic solutions
3:563 minutes, 56 secondslet us look at the evidence, not speculation, not philosophy. Three specific mathematically valid solutions to Einstein's field equations that each
4:044 minutes, 4 secondsreveal a different way the theory breaks down at its own edges. The first is the Schwarz solution. In 1916, just weeks
4:124 minutes, 12 secondsafter Einstein published his field equations, a German physicist named Carl Schwarzild solved them for the simplest possible case, a single perfectly
4:204 minutes, 20 secondsspherical non-rotating mass sitting alone in space. It was supposed to be a clean textbook result. Instead, it contained something that made Einstein
4:284 minutes, 28 secondshimself deeply uncomfortable. At a certain critical radius around any sufficiently massive object, the equations produce a boundary where time
4:364 minutes, 36 secondsas measured from the outside appears to stop completely. Cross that boundary, what we now call the event horizon, and the equations do something even
4:434 minutes, 43 secondsstranger. The coordinate that was previously behaving like space begins behaving like time. And the coordinate that was behaving like time begins
4:514 minutes, 51 secondsbehaving like space. Inside a black hole, according to the Schwarz solution, falling toward the singularity is not a
4:584 minutes, 58 secondsdirection. It is a destiny. The singularity is not a place in space you are moving toward. It is a moment in time you cannot avoid. And at that
5:065 minutes, 6 secondsmoment, the equations produce an output of infinite density, infinite curvature, and zero volume. The mathematics does
5:145 minutes, 14 secondsnot approximate infinity here. It arrives at it exactly. Physicists have a word for this. They call it a breakdown.
5:215 minutes, 21 secondsThe equation is not giving a wrong answer. It is giving an answer that has no physical meaning, like a calculator that returns an error. Except the
5:295 minutes, 29 secondscalculator is our best theory of gravity. Kurt Girdle, working as Einstein's closest colleague at Princeton, fed a different configuration
5:365 minutes, 36 secondsinto the field equations, a universe filled with uniformly distributed rotating matter, balanced by a cosmological constant. What came back
5:455 minutes, 45 secondswas a spaceime where closed timelike curves exist everywhere. Not in exotic corners of the universe, everywhere. A
5:525 minutes, 52 secondsclosed timelike curve is a path through spaceime that loops back on itself in time. Follow it long enough and you return to your own past. And critically,
6:006 minutesGirdle solution requires none of the exotic ingredients that other time travel solutions demand. No negative energy, no wormholes, just rotating
6:096 minutes, 9 secondsmatter which we observe throughout our universe. And Einstein's equations followed honestly. This is not a fringe result. It has been in the literature
6:176 minutes, 17 secondsfor over 70 years and no one has found a mathematical error in it. The third is the cosmological constant problem. When Einstein first wrote his field
6:256 minutes, 25 secondsequations, he included a term called the cosmological constant represented by the Greek letter lambda. He later removed it, famously calling it his greatest
6:336 minutes, 33 secondsblunder. But in 1998, observations of distant supernova revealed that the universe is not just expanding but accelerating in its expansion. Something
6:426 minutes, 42 secondsis pushing spaceime apart. That something is now called dark energy. And lambda came back. Here is where it becomes genuinely alarming. Quantum
6:516 minutes, 51 secondsfield theory, our best framework for understanding particle physics, predicts a value for the energy density of empty space. When you calculate that value and
6:596 minutes, 59 secondscompare it to the observed value of the cosmological constant, the discrepancy is 10 to the power of 120. That is not a
7:077 minutes, 7 secondsrounding error. That is the largest mismatch between theory and observation in the entire history of science.
7:127 minutes, 12 secondsEinstein's equations accommodate this number. They do not explain it. And that distinction between accommodating and explaining is where the real disturbance
7:207 minutes, 20 secondslives. Here is where the story becomes genuinely unsettling because everything we have discussed so far assumes that general relativity is operating alone.
Chapter 4: The clash with quantum mechanics
7:297 minutes, 29 secondsBut the universe does not operate in isolated theoretical frameworks. And when you force general relativity to share the stage with our other great
7:377 minutes, 37 secondstheory of physical reality, something breaks. That other theory is quantum mechanics. General relativity describes the universe at the largest scales.
7:467 minutes, 46 secondsgalaxies, black holes, the curvature of spaceime across billions of light years.
7:517 minutes, 51 secondsIt is a theory of smooth continuous geometry. In general relativity, spacetime is a fabric and that fabric can be bent and twisted, but it is
7:597 minutes, 59 secondsalways well- behaved, always differentiable, always describable by clean mathematics. Quantum mechanics describes the universe at the smallest
8:088 minutes, 8 secondsscales. atoms, electrons, photons, the behavior of energy when you compress it into the tightest possible spaces. And
8:168 minutes, 16 secondsat those scales, reality does not behave smoothly at all. It behaves probabilistically. Particles do not have definite positions until you measure them. Energy comes in discrete packets.
8:268 minutes, 26 secondsThe act of observation changes the system being observed. These two theories are each individually the most precisely tested frameworks in the
8:348 minutes, 34 secondshistory of science. Quantum electronamics which sits within quantum mechanics has been verified to an accuracy of one part in 1 billion.
8:428 minutes, 42 secondsGeneral relativity predicted the rate of time dilation in GPS satellites to a precision that if ignored would cause your navigation to drift by several
8:508 minutes, 50 secondskilome per day. Both theories are right and they are fundamentally incompatible with each other. When you try to apply quantum mechanical principles to
8:588 minutes, 58 secondsgravity, the mathematics collapses. You get infinities that cannot be removed.
9:029 minutes, 2 secondsIn quantum field theory, physicists handle problematic infinities through a process called renormalization.
9:089 minutes, 8 secondsEssentially subtracting infinities in a controlled way that preserves meaningful predictions. This works for electromagnetism. It works for the strong and weak nuclear forces. It does
9:179 minutes, 17 secondsnot work for gravity. The infinities that appear when you try to quantize general relativity are not manageable.
9:239 minutes, 23 secondsThey are catastrophic. This means we currently have no valid theory describing what happens at the plank scale. The regime where both quantum
9:319 minutes, 31 secondseffects and gravitational effects become simultaneously significant. That regime exists. It existed during the first
9:389 minutes, 38 secondsmoments after the big bang. It exists at the center of every black hole. And our two best theories when pointed at it together simply contradict each other
9:469 minutes, 46 secondsand fall silent. Physicists have been searching for a theory of quantum gravity since the 1930s. String theory
9:539 minutes, 53 secondsremains mathematically elegant but has produced no confirmed experimental prediction. Loop quantum gravity offers a different approach but remains
10:0210 minutes, 2 secondsincomplete. As of 2025, the Large Hadron Collider at CERN has found no evidence of the graviton, the hypothetical
10:0910 minutes, 9 secondsparticle that would mediate quantum gravity. The universe is operating by rules we do not fully possess. And the places where Einstein's equations break
10:1710 minutes, 17 secondsdown are precisely the places where those missing rules would have to live.
Chapter 5: Implications for reality
10:2110 minutes, 21 secondsLet us stop talking about equations for a moment because it is very easy to hear words like singularity and cosmological constant and feel a comfortable distance
10:2910 minutes, 29 secondsfrom them. These sound like problems belonging to physicists and laboratories scrolling on whiteboards in buildings you will never visit. Abstract, remote,
10:3710 minutes, 37 secondssafely theoretical. But here is what is actually true. The dark energy that is currently accelerating the expansion of the universe, pulling galaxy clusters
10:4610 minutes, 46 secondsapart at scales so vast that light itself will eventually never bridge the gap is not out there somewhere. It is here. It is in the room you are sitting
10:5410 minutes, 54 secondsin right now. It is in the space between the atoms that make up the screen you are looking at. It is in the space between the neurons firing in your brain
11:0111 minutes, 1 secondas you process this sentence. Empty space, according to our best current understanding, is not empty at all. It is saturated with energy we cannot
11:0911 minutes, 9 secondsexplain. governed by a number our equations cannot predict expanding a universe we cannot ultimately stop. You are not watching this from the outside.
11:1711 minutes, 17 secondsThere is no outside and consider what the godal solution implies on a personal scale. If there exist geometrically valid configurations of spaceime where
11:2611 minutes, 26 secondsclosed timelike curves are possible where the boundary between past and future becomes negotiable then the intuition you carry every second of your
11:3311 minutes, 33 secondslife the feeling that the past is fixed and the future is open is not a law of physics. It is a local condition, a feature of the particular region of spacetime you happen to inhabit.
11:4311 minutes, 43 secondsSomewhere in the solution space of Einstein's equations, there is a universe where the question of whether you caused something or whether that something caused you has no answer. That
11:5211 minutes, 52 secondsis not a thought experiment. That is a mathematical result. Now to be precise and precision matters here, this does not mean your experience of time is an
12:0112 minutes, 1 secondillusion in any simple sense. The evidence strongly suggests that our universe does not contain closed timelike curves. But the fact that
12:0912 minutes, 9 secondsgeneral relativity permits them without additional constraints tells us something profound. Our deepest theory of gravity does not consider your
12:1612 minutes, 16 secondsexperience of cause and effect to be fundamental. It has to be told separately to protect it. And so far, no one has written that instruction into a
12:2412 minutes, 24 secondstheorem that holds without exception. At this point, it would be intellectually dishonest not to present the other side of this argument clearly. There is a
Chapter 6: Interpretations of failure
12:3212 minutes, 32 secondsserious wellsupported position within the physics community that says everything we have just discussed is not a crisis. It is a signpost. And there is an important difference between the two.
12:4112 minutes, 41 secondsThe mainstream view holds that when Einstein's equations produce singularities, closed timelike curves or values that seem physically unreasonable. The correct interpretation
12:5012 minutes, 50 secondsis not that reality is broken. The correct interpretation is that we have pushed the equations beyond their domain of validity. Every physical theory has a
12:5712 minutes, 57 secondsboundary. Newtonian mechanics works perfectly until velocities approach the speed of light. Thermodynamics works perfectly until you reach the scale of
13:0513 minutes, 5 secondsindividual molecules. General relativity on this view works perfectly until you reach the plank scale at which point a deeper theory takes over. The breakdown
13:1413 minutes, 14 secondsis a message not a malfunction. Steven Hawking formalized this intuition with his chronology protection conjecture proposed in 1992. He argued that the
13:2313 minutes, 23 secondslaws of physics conspire to prevent closed timelike curves from forming in any physically realizable situation.
13:2913 minutes, 29 secondsQuantum effects near any wouldbe time machine would generate runaway feedback, a kind of infinite energy buildup that would destroy the structure before it
13:3613 minutes, 36 secondscould be used. The universe on this view protects its own causal order. Roger Penrose made a similar move with his cosmic censorship conjecture, arguing
13:4513 minutes, 45 secondsthat singularities are always hidden behind event horizons and therefore never capable of influencing the outside universe in ways that would violate
13:5313 minutes, 53 secondspredictability. These are serious proposals from serious physicists. But here is the detail that demands honesty.
13:5913 minutes, 59 secondsNeither conjecture has been proven. Both remain undemonstrated hypothesis.
14:0414 minutes, 4 secondsHawking himself acknowledged late in his career that counterexamples to cosmic censorship likely exist. The universe does not appear to have received the
14:1114 minutes, 11 secondsmemo that it is supposed to protect us from these conclusions. The guardrails we have proposed are elegant. They are just not yet confirmed. But this is not
14:2014 minutes, 20 secondsa story without a next chapter. And the next chapter is closer than most people realize. We are currently living through what may be the most consequential era
Chapter 7: Testing the boundaries
14:2814 minutes, 28 secondsin observational physics since Galileo pointed a lens at the night sky. The instruments we are building and in some cases have already built are sensitive
14:3714 minutes, 37 secondsenough to begin probing the exact boundary regions where general relativity either holds or begins to fracture. For the first time in history,
14:4514 minutes, 45 secondsthe questions we have been discussing tonight are moving from the domain of pure theory into the domain of testable prediction. Consider the Einstein
14:5214 minutes, 52 secondstelescope. This is a next generation gravitational wave detector currently in the planning and early development phase in Europe with operation targeted for
15:0015 minutesthe mid 2030s where LIGO which first detected gravitational waves in 2015 was sensitive enough to hear the collision

15:2315 minutes, 23 secondsplank scale that the signal itself would carry imprints of quantum gravitational effects. If the signal matches general relativity exactly, that tells us
15:3115 minutes, 31 secondssomething profound. If it deviates even slightly, that tells us something more profound still. Then there is the James Webb Space Telescope which has been
15:4015 minutes, 40 secondsreturning data since 2022 and continues to refine our measurements of the universe's expansion rate. Here is the problem. It is sharpening into focus.
15:4815 minutes, 48 secondsThere is currently a persistent statistically significant disagreement between two independent methods of measuring how fast the universe is

16:0416 minutes, 4 secondscontinues to confirm this tension rather than dissolve it, the implication is that our standard cosmological model, which is built directly on Einstein's
16:1216 minutes, 12 secondsequations plus a cosmological constant, is missing something real. The next generation event horizon telescope known
16:1916 minutes, 19 secondsas the NGHT, is being developed to image the region immediately surrounding black hole singularities with dramatically higher resolution than the image of
16:2716 minutes, 27 secondsMessier 87 captured in 2019. What we see at those scales, specifically whether the boundary behaves exactly as general
16:3516 minutes, 35 secondsrelativity predicts or shows subtle deviations will constrain theories of quantum gravity in ways that pure mathematics cannot. These are not
16:4416 minutes, 44 secondsdistant dreams. These are funded projects with engineering teams and launch schedules. The answers are coming. The question is whether we are prepared for what they might say.
Chapter 8: The search for deeper truth
16:5316 minutes, 53 secondsEinstein died in 1955 in a hospital room in Princeton, New Jersey, with a set of handwritten equations on the nightstand beside him. He had spent the last 30
17:0217 minutes, 2 secondsyears of his life searching for a unified field theory, a single mathematical framework that would bring gravity and electromagnetism together
17:0917 minutes, 9 secondsinto one complete description of reality. He did not find it. By most accounts, he knew he would not find it.
17:1517 minutes, 15 secondsHe kept working anyway. Girdle outlived him by 24 years. Toward the end of his life, Girdle developed an intense debilitating paranoia. He became
17:2317 minutes, 23 secondsconvinced that people were attempting to poison his food. He refused to eat unless his wife prepared every meal personally. When she was hospitalized
17:3117 minutes, 31 secondsfor 6 months in 1977, Girdle stopped eating almost entirely. He died in January of 1978, weighing 65 lbs. The
17:4017 minutes, 40 secondsman who had found the deepest crack in the foundation of general relativity, who had looked further into the logical structure of mathematics than perhaps
17:4717 minutes, 47 secondsanyone before him was ultimately destroyed by a failure of trust in the most basic features of his immediate reality. There is something in that
17:5517 minutes, 55 secondswhich resists easy interpretation. Two men who saw further into the nature of reality than almost anyone in history,
18:0218 minutes, 2 secondsand what they saw did not resolve into comfort. It resolved into deeper questions. So here is the question I want to leave with you. If our most
18:0918 minutes, 9 secondssuccessful theory of gravity is mathematically permitted to produce universes without stable cause and effect, without a guaranteed arrow of time, without a clean separation between
18:1818 minutes, 18 secondspast and future, what does that tell us about the relationship between mathematics and truth? Are we discovering the structure of reality, or
18:2618 minutes, 26 secondsare we discovering the structure of our own reasoning? Einstein's equations may be the most powerful lens humanity has ever constructed. But every lens, no
18:3418 minutes, 34 secondsmatter how precisely ground, has a focal length beyond which the image begins to blur.

2:272 minutes, 27 secondsmulti-dimensional structures with rapidly increasing complexity. [music] Second, coordinate independence. A tensor in its true mathematical

3:093 minutes, 9 secondsnot the same in every orientation. For example, a stress [music] tensor describes how forces are distributed inside a material, capturing effects

5:395 minutes, 39 secondsand back, from your perspective on the ship, the journey might only take a couple of years. You'd experience a few
5:475 minutes, 47 secondsyears of time passing. You'd age a few years, but when you returned to Earth,
5:545 minutes, 54 seconds20 years would have passed. Your friends would be 20 years older. Your children might now be older than you. This isn't
6:026 minutes, 2 secondsscience fiction. This is what Einstein's equations predict. And it's been confirmed by experiments countless
6:106 minutes, 10 secondstimes. We've tested this with particles in accelerators.
6:156 minutes, 15 secondsUnstable particles that normally decay in micros secondsonds live much longer when they're traveling at 99.9%
6:246 minutes, 24 secondsof light speed. Their internal clocks run slower from our perspective.
6:296 minutes, 29 secondsWe've tested it with atomic clocks on airplanes. When you fly an atomic clock around the world and compare it to an
6:376 minutes, 37 secondsidentical clock that stayed on the ground, the traveling clock shows less time has passed.
6:446 minutes, 44 secondsThe difference is tiny, just a few hundred nanconds, but it's measurable and real, and it matches Einstein's
6:526 minutes, 52 secondspredictions exactly. The implications are profound and unsettling. There's no universal now. No cosmic clock ticking
7:017 minutes, 1 secondat the same rate everywhere in the universe. When you say something is happening right now on a distant planet, that statement has no absolute meaning.
7:127 minutes, 12 secondsDifferent observers moving at different speeds would disagree about what right now means. They would disagree about
7:207 minutes, 20 secondswhich events are simultaneous, which events happen at the same time.
7:257 minutes, 25 secondsTime is not a river flowing at a constant rate for everyone. It's more like a fabric that can stretch and
7:327 minutes, 32 secondscompress depending on how you're moving through it. But Einstein wasn't done.
Chapter 3: General relativity
7:387 minutes, 38 secondsSpecial relativity only dealt with objects moving at constant speeds. It didn't handle acceleration.
7:467 minutes, 46 secondsIt didn't handle gravity. And gravity was a major problem. Newton's law of gravity had worked brilliantly for over
7:557 minutes, 55 seconds200 years. You could use it to predict the motion of planets with incredible accuracy. You could calculate the trajectory of cannonballs.
8:058 minutes, 5 secondsYou could predict the orbits of moons around their planets. It was one of the great triumphs of classical physics.
8:138 minutes, 13 secondsBut Newton's gravity had a problem that became obvious once Einstein developed special relativity.
8:198 minutes, 19 secondsNewton said that gravity acts instantaneously across any [music] distance. If the sun suddenly
8:268 minutes, 26 secondsdisappeared, Earth would immediately feel the change in gravity and fly off into space. But special relativity said nothing can travel faster than light.
8:388 minutes, 38 secondsInformation can't propagate instantaneously.
8:428 minutes, 42 secondsIf the sun disappeared, Earth shouldn't know about it until at least 8 minutes later, the time it takes light to travel
8:498 minutes, 49 secondsfrom the sun to Earth. This was a fundamental conflict.
8:558 minutes, 55 secondsNewton's gravity and Einstein's relativity couldn't both be right. One of them had to give way. Einstein spent
9:049 minutes, 4 secondsyears thinking about this problem. He struggled with it, made false starts, published preliminary ideas that turned
9:129 minutes, 12 secondsout to be wrong. The breakthrough came in 1907 when he had what he later called the happiest thought of his life.
9:229 minutes, 22 secondsImagine you're standing on the ground.
9:259 minutes, 25 secondsYou feel your weight, the force of gravity pulling you down toward Earth's center. Now imagine someone in a
9:339 minutes, 33 secondswindowless room in space, far from any planets or stars.
9:389 minutes, 38 secondsIf their room is sitting still in empty space, they'd float weightlessly. But if their room is accelerating upward, if a
9:479 minutes, 47 secondsrocket engine is pushing it faster and faster, they'd feel a force pushing them toward the floor. They'd feel weight exactly like they feel weight on Earth.
9:589 minutes, 58 secondsEinstein realized something remarkable.
10:0210 minutes, 2 secondsBeing in an accelerating room far from any gravity is indistinguishable from standing still in a gravitational field.
10:1010 minutes, 10 secondsYou can't tell the difference.
10:1310 minutes, 13 secondsThere's no experiment you can do inside that room to determine whether you're on Earth's surface experiencing gravity or
10:2010 minutes, 20 secondsin a rocket in deep space accelerating upward. The effects are identical.
10:2710 minutes, 27 secondsNow imagine the opposite situation.
10:3110 minutes, 31 secondsImagine you're in an elevator and the cable breaks. You're in freef fall accelerating downward.
10:3910 minutes, 39 secondsDuring those terrifying seconds before you hit the ground, you'd feel weightless. If you let go of your phone,
10:4710 minutes, 47 secondsit wouldn't fall to the floor of the elevator. It would float next to you because you're both falling at the same rate.
10:5410 minutes, 54 secondsThis is exactly what astronauts on the International Space Station experience. They're not beyond Earth's gravity.
11:0211 minutes, 2 secondsThey're still well within it, only about 250 mi up. They feel weightless, not because there's no gravity, but because
11:1111 minutes, 11 secondsthey're in continuous freefall. They're falling toward Earth, but they're also moving sideways fast enough that they
11:1811 minutes, 18 secondskeep missing Earth. They're in orbit, continuously falling but continuously missing the ground. Einstein realized
11:2711 minutes, 27 secondsthat being in freef fall is indistinguishable from being in empty space with no gravity at all. And being
11:3511 minutes, 35 secondsin a rocket accelerating upward is indistinguishable from standing on the surface of a planet feeling gravity pull you down. You can't tell the difference.
11:4611 minutes, 46 secondsIf you're in a closed room with no windows, there's no experiment you can do to determine whether you're sitting on
11:5311 minutes, 53 secondsEarth's surface feeling 1g of gravity or sitting in a rocket in deep space accelerating upward at 1g. The effects
12:0312 minutes, 3 secondsare identical. This is called the equivalence principle. Acceleration and gravity are equivalent. They're the same
12:1212 minutes, 12 secondsthing. just viewed from different perspectives.
12:1612 minutes, 16 secondsAnd this was the key insight that led Einstein to his theory of general relativity.
12:2312 minutes, 23 secondsIf acceleration and gravity are the same thing and if special relativity says that acceleration affects space and
12:3012 minutes, 30 secondstime, then gravity must also affect space and time. Gravity must curve space and time. It took Einstein another 8
12:4012 minutes, 40 secondsyears to work out the mathematics. From 1907 to 1915, he struggled with the equations. He
12:4812 minutes, 48 secondslearned advanced mathematics he'd never studied before. He made mistakes, published wrong results, corrected them,
12:5612 minutes, 56 secondsand tried again. It was the hardest intellectual work of his life.
13:0213 minutes, 2 secondsHe later said the final year from late 1914 to November 1915 was the most
13:1013 minutes, 10 secondsexhausting and demanding time of his entire career. But in November 1915, he
13:1813 minutes, 18 secondsfinally got the equations right. He published his theory of general relativity and physics would never be the same.
13:2713 minutes, 27 secondsGeneral relativity says that mass and energy curved spaceime and objects moving through curved spaceime follow
13:3413 minutes, 34 secondscurved paths. That's what we experience as gravity. It's not a force pulling objects together across empty space.
13:4413 minutes, 44 secondsIt's objects following the straightest possible paths through warped geometry.
13:4913 minutes, 49 secondsThose paths just happen to curve because the geometry itself is curved. The classic analogy is a rubber sheet.
13:5913 minutes, 59 secondsImagine stretching a flat rubber sheet tort. If you place a heavy ball in the middle, it creates a dip, a curve in the
14:0714 minutes, 7 secondssheet. Now, if you roll a smaller ball across the sheet, it will curve toward the heavy ball. Not because the heavy
14:1514 minutes, 15 secondsball is pulling it with some invisible force, but because the sheet itself is curved. The small ball is trying to roll
14:2314 minutes, 23 secondsin a straight line, but the curved sheet forces it to follow a curved path. This is what mass does to spacetime. The sun
14:3214 minutes, 32 secondswith its enormous mass curves spacetime around it. Earth is trying to move in a
14:3914 minutes, 39 secondsstraight line through space. But the curved spacetime around the sun forces Earth to follow a curved path. That
14:4814 minutes, 48 secondscurved path is Earth's orbit. We experience this as the Sun's gravity pulling on Earth. But from Einstein's perspective, there's no pull at all.
15:0015 minutesThere's just Earth moving through curved spaceime.
15:0415 minutes, 4 secondsNow, this rubber sheet analogy is helpful, but it's also misleading in important ways. The rubber sheet is a
15:1215 minutes, 12 secondstwo-dimensional surface curving in three-dimensional space. But spacetime is fourdimensional.
15:2015 minutes, 20 secondsThree dimensions of space plus one of time. And it's not curving in some
15:2715 minutes, 27 secondshigher dimensional space. It's curving in on itself. This is genuinely hard to visualize because we can't picture
15:3615 minutes, 36 secondsfourdimensional geometry. Our brains evolved to understand three spatial dimensions, not four-dimensional spaceime.
15:4615 minutes, 46 secondsAlso, the rubber sheet analogy makes it look like gravity is pulling the balls down, but that's our Earth's gravity
15:5415 minutes, 54 secondsdoing the pulling in the analogy. In real spaceime, there's no external force.
16:0116 minutes, 1 secondThe curvature itself is gravity. Objects follow what are called geodessics, which are the straightest possible paths through curved spaceime.
16:1216 minutes, 12 secondsA planet orbiting a star is following a geodic.
16:1716 minutes, 17 secondsIt's moving in what it perceives as a straight line through spacetime.
16:2216 minutes, 22 secondsBut because spacetime is curved, that straight line curves back on itself, creating an orbit. Einstein struggled
16:3116 minutes, 31 secondsfor years to find the right mathematical language to express these ideas.
16:3716 minutes, 37 secondsHe needed tensor calculus, a branch of mathematics that deals with objects that have multiple indices and transform in
16:4616 minutes, 46 secondsspecific ways when you change coordinate systems. He'd never studied this in detail during his university education.
16:5516 minutes, 55 secondsHe had to learn it from his mathematician friend Marcel Grossman.
17:0017 minutesTogether they worked through the mathematics, trying different formulations, making mistakes, publishing preliminary versions that
17:0817 minutes, 8 secondsturned out to be wrong. At one point, Einstein thought he had the right equations, published them, then realized
17:1717 minutes, 17 secondsthey didn't reduce to Newton's law of gravity in the appropriate [music] limit. That should have been a red flag.
17:2517 minutes, 25 secondsAny new theory of gravity has to match Newton's predictions when gravitational fields are weak and objects are moving
17:3317 minutes, 33 secondsslowly because Newton's theory works perfectly well in those situations.
17:4017 minutes, 40 secondsEinstein's preliminary equations didn't do this. So he went back to the drawing board. The breakthrough came in November 1915.
17:5017 minutes, 50 secondsEinstein was in Berlin working feverishly on the equations. He gave a series of lectures at the Prussian
17:5817 minutes, 58 secondsAcademy of Sciences presenting his progress week by week as he refined the
18:0418 minutes, 4 secondstheory. On November 25th, 1915, he presented the final form of the field equations.
18:1318 minutes, 13 secondsThese are now called Einstein's field equations, and they're among the most important equations in all of physics.
18:2118 minutes, 21 secondsThey look deceptively simple when written in modern notation, but they're actually a set of 10 coupled nonlinear partial differential equations.
18:3218 minutes, 32 secondsThey're incredibly difficult to solve.
18:3618 minutes, 36 secondsEven today with powerful computers, we can only solve them exactly for very simple, highly symmetric situations.
18:4618 minutes, 46 secondsFor more realistic scenarios, we have to use approximations or numerical simulations.
18:5318 minutes, 53 secondsBut despite their complexity, Einstein's equations make clear predictions. And those predictions have been tested over and over again for more than a century.
Chapter 4: Evidence and confirmation
19:0419 minutes, 4 secondsEvery single test has confirmed the theory. The first major test came almost immediately. Remember that problem with
19:1219 minutes, 12 secondsMercury's orbit that Newton's theory couldn't explain? Mercury orbits the sun in an ellipse.
19:1919 minutes, 19 secondsAnd that ellipse slowly rotates over time, a phenomenon called precession.
19:2619 minutes, 26 secondsNewton's equations predicted how much Mercury should precess based on the gravitational influence of the other
19:3319 minutes, 33 secondsplanets. But observations showed Mercury was precessing about 43 arcsecury more than predicted.
19:4319 minutes, 43 secondsThat's about 118th of a degree per century. Incredibly small, but real and measurable.
19:5119 minutes, 51 secondsScientists had searched for explanations for decades.
19:5519 minutes, 55 secondsMaybe there was an undiscovered planet called Vulcan, orbiting between Mercury and the sun, pulling on Mercury with its
20:0220 minutes, 2 secondsgravity. Astronomers searched for Vulcan for years. It didn't exist.
20:0920 minutes, 9 secondsMaybe Newton's laws needed some small adjustment.
20:1420 minutes, 14 secondsNothing worked. When Einstein applied his new equations to Mercury's orbit, they predicted exactly the right amount of precession.
20:2320 minutes, 23 seconds43 arcsec per century, matching the observations perfectly.
20:2920 minutes, 29 secondsEinstein later said that when he realized this, his heart pounded so hard he was afraid he might have a heart
20:3720 minutes, 37 secondsattack. It was confirmation that his theory was right. The next major test came in 1919.
20:4620 minutes, 46 secondsEinstein's theory predicted that light should bend when it passes near a massive object. This was revolutionary.
20:5520 minutes, 55 secondsNewton's theory said light always travels in straight lines. But Einstein said light follows geodessics through
21:0421 minutes, 4 secondscurved spacetime and near a massive object like the sun, those geodessics are curved.
21:1221 minutes, 12 secondsDuring a total solar eclipse in 1919, the British astronomer Arthur Eddington led expeditions to observe stars near
21:2021 minutes, 20 secondsthe sun's edge. Normally, you can't see stars near the sun because the sun is so
21:2721 minutes, 27 secondsbright. But during a total eclipse, when the moon blocks the sun's light, you can see stars in the sky right next to where the sun appears.
21:3821 minutes, 38 secondsEdington measured the positions of these stars and compared them to their normal positions when the sun wasn't nearby.
21:4621 minutes, 46 secondsThe stars appeared shifted exactly as Einstein predicted.
21:5121 minutes, 51 secondsTheir light had been bent by the sun's gravity as it passed near the sun on its way to Earth. The deflection was tiny,
22:0022 minutesabout 1.75 arcsec, about 12,000th of a degree, but it was measurable and it
22:0822 minutes, 8 secondsmatched Einstein's prediction. When the results were announced, Einstein became world famous overnight.
22:1622 minutes, 16 secondsNewspapers around the world ran headlines about how a German scientist had overthrown Newton and revolutionized our understanding of the universe.
22:2622 minutes, 26 secondsEinstein went from being an obscure physics professor to being a household name. Since then, we've tested general
22:3422 minutes, 34 secondsrelativity in countless ways, and it's passed every single test.
22:4122 minutes, 41 secondsWe've measured the bending of light around massive galaxies, a phenomenon called gravitational lensing. We've
22:4922 minutes, 49 secondsdetected the slowing of time in strong gravitational fields.
22:5422 minutes, 54 secondsWe've observed the orbits of binary pulsars, pairs of neutron stars orbiting each other, and watched them slowly
23:0123 minutes, 1 secondspiral inward as they lose energy to gravitational waves. Exactly as Einstein's equations predict.
23:1023 minutes, 10 secondsLet me tell you about one of the most remarkable tests. In the 1970s, astronomers discovered a binary pulsar
23:1823 minutes, 18 secondssystem called PSRB 1913 + 16. A pulsar is a rapidly rotating
23:2723 minutes, 27 secondsneutron star that emits beams of radio waves like a lighthouse. This particular system consists of two neutron stars
23:3623 minutes, 36 secondsorbiting each other. One of them is a pulsar. So we can track its motion with incredible precision by timing the
23:4423 minutes, 44 secondspulses we receive. The pulses arrive with clockwork regularity like a cosmic metronome.
23:5223 minutes, 52 secondsBut the timing changes slightly as the pulsar orbits its companion. When it's moving toward us, the pulses arrive
24:0024 minutesslightly earlier. When it's moving away, they arrive slightly later. By carefully analyzing these timing variations,
24:0924 minutes, 9 secondsastronomers could determine the masses of both neutron stars, the size of their orbit, how elliptical it is, and how the
24:1724 minutes, 17 secondsorbit is changing over time. General relativity predicts that this system should be losing energy by emitting
24:2624 minutes, 26 secondsgravitational waves. As it loses energy, the orbit should shrink. The neutron
24:3324 minutes, 33 secondsstars should spiral closer together. The orbital period should decrease. The
24:4024 minutes, 40 secondseffect is tiny. The orbital period was about 8 hours.
24:4524 minutes, 45 secondsGeneral relativity predicted it should decrease by about 76 micros per year.
24:5224 minutes, 52 secondsThat's 76 millionth of a second per year. But pulsars are such precise clocks that this could be measured. And
25:0125 minutes, 1 secondover decades of observations, astronomers found that the orbital period was decreasing at exactly the rate Einstein's equations predicted.
25:1225 minutes, 12 secondsExactly.
25:1425 minutes, 14 secondsThis was the first indirect evidence for gravitational waves. It showed that gravitational waves must exist and must
25:2325 minutes, 23 secondscarry away energy from the system. It was powerful confirmation of general relativity.
25:3025 minutes, 30 secondsRussell Hulse and Joseph Taylor who discovered this system and made these measurements won the Nobel Prize in physics in 1993.
25:4125 minutes, 41 secondsBut that was just indirect [music] evidence.
25:4525 minutes, 45 secondsThe holy grail was to directly detect gravitational waves passing through Earth. This is incredibly difficult. A
25:5425 minutes, 54 secondsgravitational wave from even a catastrophic event like two black holes colliding stretches and squeezes space by an almost unimaginably tiny amount.
26:0626 minutes, 6 secondsWhen the waves from that first detection in 2015 passed through the LIGO detectors, they changed the length of
26:1426 minutes, 14 secondsthe 2 and 1/2 mile arms by about 110,000th the diameter of a proton. To
26:2126 minutes, 21 secondsput that in perspective, imagine trying to measure the distance to the nearest star about four light years away to an
26:2926 minutes, 29 secondsaccuracy of about the width of a human hair. That's the level of precision needed. The LIGO detectors use laser
26:3826 minutes, 38 secondsinterferometry. They split a laser beam and send it down two perpendicular arms, each 2 and 1/2 miles long. The beams
26:4726 minutes, 47 secondsbounce off mirrors at the ends of the arms and come back to the detector where they're recombined.
26:5426 minutes, 54 secondsIf both arms are exactly the same length, the two beams are in phase when they recombine and they interfere
27:0227 minutes, 2 secondsconstructively. But if one arm is even slightly longer than the other, the beams are out of phase and they interfere destructively.
27:1127 minutes, 11 secondsWhen a gravitational wave passes through, it stretches one arm while squeezing the other. Then it does the
27:1827 minutes, 18 secondsopposite, squeezing the first arm while stretching the second.
27:2327 minutes, 23 secondsThis oscillating change in length causes the interference pattern to oscillate.
27:2927 minutes, 29 secondsThe challenge is that everything else also causes the arms to change length.
27:3427 minutes, 34 secondsSeismic vibrations from earthquakes, traffic, ocean waves, thermal fluctuations causing the metal
27:4327 minutes, 43 secondsof the detector to expand and contract, quantum fluctuations in the laser light itself, even cosmic rays hitting the mirrors.
27:5427 minutes, 54 secondsAll of these noise sources had to be reduced or compensated for. The detectors are isolated from seismic
28:0228 minutes, 2 secondsvibrations by elaborate suspension systems. The laser systems are stabilized to incredible precision. The
28:1028 minutes, 10 secondsentire apparatus is kept in an ultra high vacuum to eliminate air currents.
28:1728 minutes, 17 secondsEven with all these precautions, detecting gravitational waves required decades of engineering development.
28:2428 minutes, 24 secondsThe first large-scale detectors were built in the 1990s and early 2000s. They ran for years without detecting
28:3328 minutes, 33 secondsanything. The signals were just too weak, buried in the noise. But the detectors were upgraded, made more sensitive.
28:4328 minutes, 43 secondsAnd on September 14th, 2015, at 5:51 a.m. Eastern time, both LIGO
28:5128 minutes, 51 secondsdetectors, one in Louisiana and one in Washington State, picked up a signal, a distinctive chirp, starting at low
29:0029 minutesfrequency and sweeping up to higher frequency over about a fifth of a second. This was the signature of two
29:0829 minutes, 8 secondsblack holes, each about 30 times the mass of the sun, spiraling into each other and merging. They were about 1.3
29:1729 minutes, 17 secondsbillion light years away. As they spiraled closer, they orbited faster.
29:2429 minutes, 24 secondsThe gravitational waves they emitted increased in frequency and amplitude.
29:3029 minutes, 30 secondsFinally, they merged, creating a single black hole of about 62 solar masses.
29:3729 minutes, 37 secondsAbout three solar masses worth of energy was converted directly into gravitational waves in the final moments before merger.
29:4729 minutes, 47 secondsFor that brief instant, this collision released more power than all the stars in the observable universe combined. The
29:5429 minutes, 54 secondssignal matched the predictions of general relativity perfectly. the frequency, the amplitude, the rate of
30:0230 minutes, 2 secondschange, everything. This was announced in February 2016. It was one of the most important scientific discoveries of the
30:1130 minutes, 11 secondscentury. It opened an entirely new way of observing the universe.
30:1630 minutes, 16 secondsSince then, we've detected dozens of these events.
30:2130 minutes, 21 secondsBlack hole merges with masses ranging from about eight solar masses to over 100 neutron star mergers.
30:3130 minutes, 31 secondsEven hints of exotic objects that might be neither black holes nor neutron stars.
30:3830 minutes, 38 secondsEach detection tests general relativity in extreme conditions. And each time the
30:4530 minutes, 45 secondstheory passes. We've also seen the first image of a black hole. In April 2019, the Event Horizon Telescope
Chapter 5: Imaging the invisible
