35:1935 minutes, 19 secondscan be curved by mass and energy. It can ripple. It can be measured and photographed.
35:2635 minutes, 26 secondsBut here's the thing. We still don't know what it actually is. Let me explain what I mean by that. Einstein's general
35:3535 minutes, 35 secondsrelativity describes how spacetime behaves. It tells us that mass curves spacetime.
35:4335 minutes, 43 secondsIt tells us that objects follow geodics through curved spacetime. It gives us equations we can use to calculate those
35:5135 minutes, 51 secondscurves and predict the motion of objects. But it doesn't tell us what spacetime is made of.
35:5835 minutes, 58 secondsIt treats spacetime as a smooth continuous fabric. You can zoom in on it as much as you want and it's still
36:0736 minutes, 7 secondssmooth. There's no smallest unit, no fundamental building block. It's infinitely divisible.
36:1536 minutes, 15 secondsBut we know from quantum mechanics that this can't be the whole story. Quantum mechanics says that everything in nature
36:2336 minutes, 23 secondscomes in discrete units at small enough scales. Energy comes in packets called quanta.
36:3136 minutes, 31 secondsThat's where the word quantum comes from. Light comes in packets called photons.
36:3736 minutes, 37 secondsMatter is made of atoms which are made of protons, neutrons, and electrons.
36:4436 minutes, 44 secondsThose protons and neutrons are made of quarks.
36:4836 minutes, 48 secondsEverything we've studied in detail turns out to be quantized at small enough scales. So it seems reasonable to think
36:5636 minutes, 56 secondsthat spacetime itself must also be quantized. There must be some smallest unit of space and time. Some fundamental
37:0637 minutes, 6 secondsbuilding block that spacetime is made of. This is where quantum gravity comes in. Quantum gravity is the attempt to
Chapter 6: Quantum gravity
37:1437 minutes, 14 secondscombine Einstein's general relativity with quantum mechanics to develop a theory that describes how
37:2237 minutes, 22 secondsspacetime behaves at the smallest scales where quantum effects become important.
37:2937 minutes, 29 secondsAnd this is where we run into serious problems.
37:3337 minutes, 33 secondsGeneral relativity and quantum mechanics are both incredibly successful theories.
37:3937 minutes, 39 secondsThey've been tested extensively and they work beautifully within their domains, but they don't fit together.
37:4837 minutes, 48 secondsThey're based on fundamentally different assumptions about how the universe works. General relativity treats spacetime as smooth and continuous.
37:5837 minutes, 58 secondsQuantum mechanics says things are discrete and uncertain.
38:0338 minutes, 3 secondsGeneral relativity is deterministic. If you know the state of the universe now, you can calculate its state at any other
38:1138 minutes, 11 secondstime. Quantum mechanics is probabilistic.
38:1538 minutes, 15 secondsYou can only calculate the probability of different outcomes.
38:1938 minutes, 19 secondsThese are fundamentally different worldviews and they clash when you try to apply both to the same situation.
38:2738 minutes, 27 secondslike the interior of a black hole or the big bang or any situation involving very strong gravity and very small distances.
38:3738 minutes, 37 secondsLet's talk about what happens when you try to combine them.
38:4238 minutes, 42 secondsOne of the first things physicists tried was to treat gravity like the other forces. The electromagnetic force is carried by particles called photons.
38:5338 minutes, 53 secondsThe weak nuclear force is carried by particles called W and Z bzons. The strong nuclear force is carried by particles called gluons.
39:0439 minutes, 4 secondsSo maybe gravity is also carried by particles. These hypothetical particles are called gravitons.
39:1339 minutes, 13 secondsIf you try to build a quantum theory of gravity using gravitons, you run into problems immediately. The calculations produce infinities.
39:2339 minutes, 23 secondsWhen you try to calculate the probability of certain events happening, the answer comes out as infinity, which
39:3039 minutes, 30 secondsis clearly wrong. Probabilities can't be infinite. They have to be numbers between zero and one.
39:4039 minutes, 40 secondsPhysicists have developed techniques for dealing with infinities in quantum field theory. These techniques called
39:4839 minutes, 48 secondsrenormalization work beautifully for the electromagnetic force and the weak and strong nuclear forces.
39:5639 minutes, 56 secondsBut they don't work for gravity. The infinities in quantum gravity are uncontrollable. They don't go away no
40:0440 minutes, 4 secondsmatter what tricks you try. This suggests that treating gravity as just another force carried by gravitons
40:1340 minutes, 13 secondsdoesn't work. Gravity is different. It's not just a force acting in spaceime. It is spacetime.
40:2240 minutes, 22 secondsSo, physicists have tried more radical approaches. Maybe spacetime itself is quantized.
40:3140 minutes, 31 secondsMaybe space comes in smallest possible units and time comes in smallest possible moments.
40:3840 minutes, 38 secondsMaybe if you zoom in far enough, spacetime isn't smooth at all. Maybe it's grainy, discreet, made of
40:4740 minutes, 47 secondsfundamental building blocks. The scale where this would happen is called the plank scale. The plank length is about 10 to the minus35 m.
40:5940 minutes, 59 secondsThat's 0.00000000000000.
41:0041 minutesZ00000000000000000000000000000000001
41:1441 minutes, 14 secondsm. It's incomprehensibly small. For comparison, a proton is about 10 to the
41:2141 minutes, 21 secondsminus15 m across. The plank length is about 20 orders of magnitude smaller
41:2741 minutes, 27 secondsthan a proton. The plank time is about 10 the minus 44 seconds.

Chapter 8: Emergent spacetime
56:4556 minutes, 45 secondsThere's also a more radical idea that's gained traction in recent years. Maybe spacetime isn't fundamental at all.

57:1557 minutes, 15 secondsQuantum mechanics allows for a strange phenomenon called entanglement.
57:2157 minutes, 21 secondsTwo particles can be entangled such that measuring one instantly affects the other, no matter how far apart they are.
57:2957 minutes, 29 secondsLet me explain what this means.
57:3257 minutes, 32 secondsSuppose you have two electrons that were created together in a specific quantum state. They might be entangled such that they have opposite spins.
57:4357 minutes, 43 secondsIf you measure one and find it spinning up, the other will instantly be spinning down. If you measure the first one and
57:5057 minutes, 50 secondsfind it spinning down, the other will be spinning up. But here's the weird part.
57:5657 minutes, 56 secondsBefore you measure, neither electron has a definite spin. Each electron is in a quantum superp position of spinning up and spinning down simultaneously.
58:0858 minutes, 8 secondsIt's not that one electron is secretly spinning up and the other is secretly spinning down and you just don't know

58:4658 minutes, 46 secondscorrelation that exists across space without anything traveling through the space between the particles.

1:15:241 hour, 15 minutes, 24 secondsspaceime is so warped that the direction toward the singularity becomes more like a direction in time than a direction in space.
1:15:331 hour, 15 minutes, 33 secondsFalling into the singularity becomes as inevitable as moving forward in time.
1:15:391 hour, 15 minutes, 39 secondsYou can't avoid it any more than you can avoid tomorrow. At the singularity itself, the curvature of spaceime
1:15:471 hour, 15 minutes, 47 secondsbecomes infinite. The equations of general relativity break down. They produce infinities, which means they're
1:15:561 hour, 15 minutes, 56 secondsno longer valid. General relativity is telling us that there's something it can't describe. that a deeper theory is
1:16:031 hour, 16 minutes, 3 secondsneeded. But the real mystery is what happens to information that falls into a black hole. In quantum mechanics,
1:16:121 hour, 16 minutes, 12 secondsinformation is never destroyed. If you know the exact quantum state of a system at one time, you can in principle
1:16:211 hour, 16 minutes, 21 secondscalculate its exact quantum state at any other time. The evolution is deterministic.
1:16:281 hour, 16 minutes, 28 secondsEven though individual measurements give probabilistic results, but black holes seem to violate this principle. If you
1:16:361 hour, 16 minutes, 36 secondsthrow a book into a black hole, all the information in that book, every arrangement of atoms, every pattern of
1:16:431 hour, 16 minutes, 43 secondsink on the pages falls past the event horizon and seems to disappear forever.
1:16:501 hour, 16 minutes, 50 secondsThe black hole doesn't care what you threw into it. It only has a few properties, mass, charge, and spin.
1:16:591 hour, 16 minutes, 59 secondsEverything else is lost. This bothered physicists for a long time. It seemed to violate a fundamental principle of quantum mechanics.
1:17:101 hour, 17 minutes, 10 secondsThen in 1974, Steven Hawking made a discovery that made the problem even worse.
1:17:191 hour, 17 minutes, 19 secondsHawking showed that black holes aren't completely black.
1:17:231 hour, 17 minutes, 23 secondsThey emit radiation. This became known as Hawking radiation. Here's how it
1:17:301 hour, 17 minutes, 30 secondsworks. Quantum mechanics says that empty space isn't really empty. It's filled with quantum fluctuations.
1:17:401 hour, 17 minutes, 40 secondsPairs of particles and antiparticles that pop into existence for incredibly brief moments before annihilating each
1:17:481 hour, 17 minutes, 48 secondsother. This happens everywhere all the time.
1:17:531 hour, 17 minutes, 53 secondsNear a black hole's event horizon, something special can happen. A particle, antiparticle pair can form
1:18:011 hour, 18 minutes, 1 secondjust outside the horizon. One particle falls into the black hole. The other escapes to infinity.
1:18:091 hour, 18 minutes, 9 secondsFrom far away, it looks like the black hole is emitting radiation.
1:18:141 hour, 18 minutes, 14 secondsThe energy for this radiation comes from the black hole's mass. So as a black hole emits Hawking radiation, it slowly
1:18:241 hour, 18 minutes, 24 secondsloses mass. It evaporates. For large black holes, this process is incredibly slow.
1:18:331 hour, 18 minutes, 33 secondsA black hole with the mass of the sun would take about 10 to the 67 years to evaporate. That's far longer than the
1:18:411 hour, 18 minutes, 41 secondscurrent age of the universe. But in principle, given enough time, any black hole will completely evaporate away.
1:18:501 hour, 18 minutes, 50 secondsHere's the problem. Hawking radiation appears to be completely random. It's thermal radiation. The same kind of
1:19:001 hour, 19 minutesradiation emitted by any hot object. It doesn't carry any information about what fell into the black hole. So if you
1:19:091 hour, 19 minutes, 9 secondsthrow a book into a black hole and wait for the black hole to evaporate, the Hawking radiation that comes out contains none of the information that
1:19:181 hour, 19 minutes, 18 secondswas in the book. The information seems to be destroyed. This violates quantum mechanics.
1:19:251 hour, 19 minutes, 25 secondsThis is the black hole information paradox. Does quantum mechanics break down at black holes or is general
1:19:331 hour, 19 minutes, 33 secondsrelativity wrong about what happens inside black holes? or is there some way information can escape that we haven't
1:19:411 hour, 19 minutes, 41 secondsfigured out yet? Physicists have been arguing about this for 50 years. It's one of the deepest problems in
1:19:481 hour, 19 minutes, 48 secondstheoretical physics, and solving it requires understanding what spacetime really is at the quantum
1:19:551 hour, 19 minutes, 55 secondslevel. There's been significant progress in recent years. Most physicists now think the information isn't actually destroyed.
1:20:061 hour, 20 minutes, 6 secondsInstead, it's encoded in the Hawking radiation in a very subtle, complex way.
1:20:121 hour, 20 minutes, 12 secondsThe radiation isn't truly random. It only looks random because the information is scrambled so thoroughly.
1:20:211 hour, 20 minutes, 21 secondsThis solution comes from ideas about holography and quantum entanglement.
1:20:261 hour, 20 minutes, 26 secondsThe idea is that all the information about what's inside a black hole is actually encoded on its event horizon.
1:20:341 hour, 20 minutes, 34 secondsThe event horizon acts like a hologram, a two-dimensional surface that contains all the information about the
1:20:411 hour, 20 minutes, 41 secondsthree-dimensional interior. As the black hole evaporates, this information is gradually transferred to the outgoing
1:20:481 hour, 20 minutes, 48 secondsHawking radiation, but it's encoded in correlations between different particles in the patterns of entanglement.
1:20:571 hour, 20 minutes, 57 secondsYou'd have to collect and analyze all the radiation from the entire evaporation process to recover the original information.
1:21:051 hour, 21 minutes, 5 secondsThis solution is called the island formula and it's been worked out in detail for simplified models of black
1:21:121 hour, 21 minutes, 12 secondsholes in the last few years. It suggests that general relativity and quantum mechanics can be reconciled.
1:21:211 hour, 21 minutes, 21 secondsinformation isn't destroyed.
1:21:241 hour, 21 minutes, 24 secondsBut the way it's preserved is far more subtle than anyone imagined. And crucially, this solution depends on the idea that spacetime is not fundamental.
1:21:361 hour, 21 minutes, 36 secondsThat it emerges from quantum entanglement.
1:21:401 hour, 21 minutes, 40 secondsThat the connections between different regions of space are actually quantum correlations between the particles in those regions. This brings us back to the fundamental question.
1:21:511 hour, 21 minutes, 51 secondsWhat is spacetime? The bottom line is this. We don't know what it's made of.
Chapter 10: Real-world applications
1:21:581 hour, 21 minutes, 58 secondsWe don't know if it's fundamental or emergent. We don't know what happened at the Big Bang when the equations predict a singularity where physics breaks down.
1:22:091 hour, 22 minutes, 9 secondsWe don't fully understand what happens inside black holes. We have equations that describe its behavior brilliantly.
1:22:181 hour, 22 minutes, 18 secondsEinstein's general relativity is one of the most successful theories in the history of science. It's been tested countless times and passed every test.
1:22:291 hour, 22 minutes, 29 secondsWe can measure spaceime.
1:22:321 hour, 22 minutes, 32 secondsWe can photograph it bending around massive objects. We can detect gravitational waves rippling through it.
1:22:411 hour, 22 minutes, 41 secondsWe can use it to build technology like GPS that works perfectly.
1:22:461 hour, 22 minutes, 46 secondsBut we don't have a complete theory that explains its true nature. We don't have a quantum theory of gravity. We don't
1:22:531 hour, 22 minutes, 53 secondsknow what happens at the plank scale where quantum effects should become important. This isn't a failure of science.
1:23:031 hour, 23 minutes, 3 secondsThis is where we are. This is the frontier of human knowledge. Every great theory in science eventually reaches a
1:23:121 hour, 23 minutes, 12 secondsboundary where it can't go further without something new. Newton's laws reach that boundary when scientists
1:23:201 hour, 23 minutes, 20 secondsstarted measuring the speed of light precisely and noticed the anomaly in Mercury's orbit. They needed Einstein's relativity to go further.
1:23:311 hour, 23 minutes, 31 secondsQuantum mechanics and relativity reached that boundary when scientists started thinking seriously about black holes and the big bang. Now we need something new.
1:23:431 hour, 23 minutes, 43 secondsA quantum theory of gravity. A description of what spaceime is at the plank scale. Somewhere beyond that
1:23:511 hour, 23 minutes, 51 secondsboundary is the answer. An understanding of what happened at the beginning of the universe. the resolution of the information paradox.
1:24:011 hour, 24 minutes, 1 secondMaybe a unified theory that describes all forces and particles. Maybe a multiverse.
1:24:081 hour, 24 minutes, 8 secondsMaybe something we haven't imagined yet.
1:24:121 hour, 24 minutes, 12 secondsBut before we go any further into these deep mysteries, let's pause and appreciate something remarkable. This
1:24:191 hour, 24 minutes, 19 secondsidea we don't fully understand. This theory that [music] breaks down at singularities and might have quantum
1:24:261 hour, 24 minutes, 26 secondscorrections we can't calculate yet is something we use every single day.
1:24:321 hour, 24 minutes, 32 secondsGeneral relativity isn't just an abstract theory for understanding black holes and the big bang. It affects your
1:24:401 hour, 24 minutes, 40 secondslife in concrete practical ways. Let's start with the most obvious example.
1:24:471 hour, 24 minutes, 47 secondsGPS, your phone, your car's navigation system, any device that tells you where
1:24:541 hour, 24 minutes, 54 secondsyou are on Earth, uses a network of satellites orbiting the planet.
1:25:001 hour, 25 minutesThere are currently about 30 GPS satellites circling Earth at an altitude of about 12,400
1:25:071 hour, 25 minutes, 7 secondsm. These satellites constantly broadcast signals containing two critical pieces of information.
1:25:151 hour, 25 minutes, 15 secondsthe exact time according to atomic clocks on board the satellites and the satellites precise position. Your GPS
1:25:241 hour, 25 minutes, 24 secondsdevice receives signals from at least four satellites. It measures how long each signal took to arrive and it uses
1:25:331 hour, 25 minutes, 33 secondsthat timing information to calculate your position. The speed of light is constant, about 186,000 m/s.
1:25:431 hour, 25 minutes, 43 secondsIf you know how long a signal took to arrive and you know it traveled at the speed of light, you can calculate how far away the satellite was when it sent
1:25:521 hour, 25 minutes, 52 secondsthe signal. With signals from four satellites, you can pinpoint your position in three-dimensional space.
1:26:001 hour, 26 minutesIt's elegant geometry. But there's a problem. The atomic clocks on the satellites don't tick at the same rate
1:26:081 hour, 26 minutes, 8 secondsas clocks on the ground. There are two effects from relativity at work here.
1:26:141 hour, 26 minutes, 14 secondsFirst, the satellites are moving fast relative to the ground, about 8,700 mph.
1:26:231 hour, 26 minutes, 23 secondsAccording to special relativity, time runs slower for moving objects. From the perspective of someone on the ground,
1:26:311 hour, 26 minutes, 31 secondsthe satellite clocks should be ticking slightly slower.
1:26:361 hour, 26 minutes, 36 secondsThis is called time dilation due to velocity. The effect is tiny but measurable. The satellite clocks lose
1:26:451 hour, 26 minutes, 45 secondsabout 7 microsconds per day compared to clocks on the ground. That's 7 millionths of a second per day. But
1:26:541 hour, 26 minutes, 54 secondsthere's a second effect that's even larger. The satellites are higher up in Earth's gravitational field. They're
1:27:021 hour, 27 minutes, 2 secondsfarther from Earth's center of mass, so they experience weaker gravity.
1:27:071 hour, 27 minutes, 7 secondsAccording to general relativity, time runs faster in weaker gravitational fields. From the perspective of someone
1:27:161 hour, 27 minutes, 16 secondson the ground, the satellite clocks should be ticking slightly faster.
1:27:211 hour, 27 minutes, 21 secondsThis is called gravitational time dilation. The satellite clocks gain about 45 microsconds per day compared to
1:27:301 hour, 27 minutes, 30 secondsclocks on the ground. When you add these two effects together, the velocity slowing down time and the weaker gravity
1:27:391 hour, 27 minutes, 39 secondsspeeding it up, the satellite clocks run about 38 microsconds per day, faster than clocks on the ground.
1:27:491 hour, 27 minutes, 49 seconds38 micros seconds doesn't sound like much, but light travels about 300 m, about 984 ft in one microcond.
1:28:011 hour, 28 minutes, 1 secondIf the GPS system didn't correct for these relativistic effects, your position would be off by about 7 miles.
1:28:081 hour, 28 minutes, 8 secondsWithin hours, GPS would be completely useless. Your navigation would tell you
1:28:151 hour, 28 minutes, 15 secondsyou're in the wrong city. Within days, it would tell you you're in a different state. But the GPS system does account
1:28:241 hour, 28 minutes, 24 secondsfor relativity. The satellite clocks are programmed to tick at a slightly different rate to compensate for both
1:28:321 hour, 28 minutes, 32 secondsspecial and general relativistic effects. When the satellites are on the ground being prepared for launch, their
1:28:401 hour, 28 minutes, 40 secondsclocks are set to run slow. Then when they're in orbit, the relativistic effects speed them up to exactly the right rate.
1:28:501 hour, 28 minutes, 50 secondsThis means every time you use GPS, every time you check your location on your phone or follow directions in your car,
1:28:591 hour, 28 minutes, 59 secondsyou're using Einstein's theory of relativity.
1:29:031 hour, 29 minutes, 3 secondsYou're using the fact that time runs at different rates depending on how fast you're moving and how deep you are in a
1:29:111 hour, 29 minutes, 11 secondsgravitational well. This is a direct practical application of our understanding of spaceime. It's not just theoretical.
1:29:201 hour, 29 minutes, 20 secondsIt's technology that billions of people use every day. But GPS is just the most obvious example. There are other ways general relativity affects modern life.
Chapter 11: Why fundamental physics matters
1:29:321 hour, 29 minutes, 32 secondsAstronomers use gravitational lensing to study the universe.
1:29:371 hour, 29 minutes, 37 secondsWhen light from a distant galaxy passes near a massive object, like another galaxy or a cluster of galaxies, the light's path bends.
1:29:471 hour, 29 minutes, 47 secondsThe massive object acts like a lens, focusing and magnifying the light from the distant galaxy. This allows us to
1:29:561 hour, 29 minutes, 56 secondssee galaxies that would otherwise be too faint to detect. We can study the early universe, galaxies that formed when the
1:30:041 hour, 30 minutes, 4 secondsuniverse was young, by using gravitational lensing to magnify them.
1:30:091 hour, 30 minutes, 9 secondsWe can also use lensing to map dark matter. Dark matter doesn't emit light, so we can't see it directly, but it has
1:30:181 hour, 30 minutes, 18 secondsmass, so it curves spacetime. When light passes through a region with dark matter, its path bends.
1:30:281 hour, 30 minutes, 28 secondsBy studying how light from background galaxies is distorted, we can map where the dark matter is and how much of it
1:30:361 hour, 30 minutes, 36 secondsthere is. This has revealed that dark matter makes up about 85% of all matter
1:30:431 hour, 30 minutes, 43 secondsin the universe. We don't know what dark matter is made of, but we know it's there because we can see its gravitational effects on spacetime.
1:30:531 hour, 30 minutes, 53 secondsGravitational waves are another tool.
1:30:561 hour, 30 minutes, 56 secondsSince the first detection in 2015, we've detected dozens of gravitational wave events, mostly from pairs of black holes
1:31:051 hour, 31 minutes, 5 secondsspiraling into each other and merging, but also from pairs of neutron stars colliding. These observations tell us things we couldn't learn any other way.
1:31:171 hour, 31 minutes, 17 secondsWe can measure the masses of black holes.
1:31:221 hour, 31 minutes, 22 secondsWe can test whether general relativity accurately describes what happens in extremely strong gravitational fields.
1:31:311 hour, 31 minutes, 31 secondsWe can study the equation of state of neutron star matter, the densest material in the universe.
1:31:381 hour, 31 minutes, 38 secondsIn 2017, gravitational wave detectors picked up a signal from two neutron stars colliding.
1:31:471 hour, 31 minutes, 47 secondsAt the same time, telescopes around the world observed a burst of gamma rays, x-rays, and visible light from the same event.
1:31:571 hour, 31 minutes, 57 secondsThis was the first time we'd observed the same astronomical event with both gravitational waves and electromagnetic
1:32:041 hour, 32 minutes, 4 secondsradiation. The collision created heavy elements like gold and platinum. By studying the light from the explosion,
1:32:131 hour, 32 minutes, 13 secondsastronomers could see the signatures of these elements being synthesized.
1:32:171 hour, 32 minutes, 17 secondsThis confirmed that neutron star collisions are where most of the heavy elements in the universe come from,
1:32:241 hour, 32 minutes, 24 secondsincluding the gold in your jewelry and the platinum in your electronics. More dramatically, gravitational waves allow
1:32:321 hour, 32 minutes, 32 secondsus to test general relativity in ways that weren't possible before.
1:32:381 hour, 32 minutes, 38 secondsWhen two black holes merge, they produce a specific pattern of gravitational waves. The frequency and amplitude of
1:32:461 hour, 32 minutes, 46 secondsthe waves depend on the masses of the black holes, how fast they're spinning, and how their spins are oriented.
1:32:541 hour, 32 minutes, 54 secondsGeneral relativity makes precise predictions about what this signal should look like. And every
1:33:011 hour, 33 minutes, 1 secondgravitational wave event we've detected [music] has matched those predictions perfectly.
1:33:071 hour, 33 minutes, 7 secondsThe theory works even in the most extreme conditions we can observe.
1:33:121 hour, 33 minutes, 12 secondsBlack holes, dozens of times the mass of the sun, moving at significant fractions of the speed of light, producing more
1:33:211 hour, 33 minutes, 21 secondspower in gravitational waves for a brief moment than all the stars in the observable universe combined. Einstein's
1:33:301 hour, 33 minutes, 30 secondsequations describe it all. There are also more exotic possible uses. Some physicists have speculated about using
1:33:391 hour, 33 minutes, 39 secondsunderstanding of space-time curvature to create advanced propulsion systems. If you could manipulate spacetime, curve it
1:33:471 hour, 33 minutes, 47 secondsin specific ways, you might be able to create something like a warp drive, contract space in front of a spacecraft
1:33:551 hour, 33 minutes, 55 secondsand expand it behind. This is purely theoretical. The energy requirements would be enormous, possibly requiring
1:34:041 hour, 34 minutes, 4 secondsmore energy than exists in the entire observable universe.
1:34:091 hour, 34 minutes, 9 secondsAnd there are serious questions about whether it's even possible in principle.
1:34:141 hour, 34 minutes, 14 secondsGeneral relativity allows such solutions mathematically. But that doesn't mean they can be realized in practice. But
1:34:221 hour, 34 minutes, 22 secondsthe point is understanding spacetime has practical implications not just for navigation and astronomy
1:34:301 hour, 34 minutes, 30 secondsbut potentially for future technologies we can barely imagine.
1:34:351 hour, 34 minutes, 35 secondsLet me give you some perspective on why solving the deeper mysteries matters.
1:34:411 hour, 34 minutes, 41 secondsWhy should you care about whether spaceime is made of loops or strings or entanglement?
1:34:471 hour, 34 minutes, 47 secondsWhy does it matter what happened at the Big Bang or whether black holes preserve information? These seem like abstract

1:35:021 hour, 35 minutes, 2 secondsFirst, understanding what spacetime is at the fundamental level would complete the picture of reality that physics has
1:35:101 hour, 35 minutes, 10 secondsbeen building for centuries. Right now we have two great theories that describe the universe. General relativity
1:35:191 hour, 35 minutes, 19 secondsdescribes gravity and the large scale structure of the cosmos. Quantum mechanics describes particles and forces
1:35:281 hour, 35 minutes, 28 secondsat small scales. Both theories are extraordinarily successful.
1:35:341 hour, 35 minutes, 34 secondsThey've been tested countless times and have never failed within their domains.
1:35:391 hour, 35 minutes, 39 secondsGeneral relativity has passed every test we've thrown at it. The bending of starlight during eclipses. The procession of Mercury's orbit.
1:35:511 hour, 35 minutes, 51 secondsGravitational time dilation verified by atomic clocks. The existence of black holes. The expansion of the universe.

1:36:161 hour, 36 minutes, 16 secondsIt explains the structure of atoms. The periodic table of elements, chemical bonds, the behavior of

1:36:461 hour, 36 minutes, 46 secondsbeen tested to extraordinary precision and it always works.
1:36:511 hour, 36 minutes, 51 secondsBut these two theories can't both be right in their current forms because they contradict each other. General
1:36:581 hour, 36 minutes, 58 secondsrelativity treats spacetime as smooth and continuous.
1:37:031 hour, 37 minutes, 3 secondsYou can zoom in as much as you want and it's still smooth. There's no smallest distance.
1:37:101 hour, 37 minutes, 10 secondsSpace is infinitely divisible. Quantum mechanics says everything comes in discrete units at small enough scales.
1:37:201 hour, 37 minutes, 20 secondsEnergy is quantized. Matter is made of discrete particles. There's a smallest unit of action.
1:37:291 hour, 37 minutes, 29 secondsPlanks constant.
1:37:321 hour, 37 minutes, 32 secondsGeneral relativity [music] is deterministic.
1:37:361 hour, 37 minutes, 36 secondsIf you know the curvature of spacetime and the positions and velocities of all objects at one moment, you can calculate
1:37:431 hour, 37 minutes, 43 secondsexactly where everything will be at any other moment. The future is completely determined by the present.
1:37:521 hour, 37 minutes, 52 secondsQuantum mechanics is probabilistic.
1:37:561 hour, 37 minutes, 56 secondsYou can only calculate the probability of different outcomes. Individual measurements give random results. Even
1:38:041 hour, 38 minutes, 4 secondsthough the underlying wave function evolves deterministically, the future is not completely determined by the present.
1:38:141 hour, 38 minutes, 14 secondsGeneral relativity says information is preserved in the smooth evolution of spacetime.
1:38:201 hour, 38 minutes, 20 secondsQuantum mechanics says information is preserved in the unitary evolution of quantum states. But black holes seem to
1:38:281 hour, 38 minutes, 28 secondsdestroy information according to general relativity, violating quantum mechanics.
1:38:331 hour, 38 minutes, 33 secondsThese are fundamentally different worldviews and they clash when you try to apply both to the same situation.

2:372 minutes, 37 secondsin the other direction. This pattern repeats again and again as the wave passes through. If we rotate the camera so that we're looking at the ring of
2:462 minutes, 46 secondsmasses face on, the distortion becomes clearer. It is the changing separation between the masses that reveals the stretching and squeezing of space itself.
2:562 minutes, 56 secondsAnd we can extend this idea further.
2:582 minutes, 58 secondsImagine several slices of space, each containing its own ring of masses. As the gravitational wave fronts pass
3:053 minutes, 5 secondsthrough the slices, one after another, the rings begin stretching and squeezing in succession with a slight phase delay between them. And the remarkable thing
3:143 minutes, 14 secondsis that this distortion can be quantified, allowing us to define a measure of gravitational wave strain.
3:233 minutes, 23 secondsFor example, imagine that we measure the vertical displacement of the top mass away from its equilibrium position as a
3:303 minutes, 30 secondsfunction of time. If we plot this displacement, we see a smooth periodic oscillation. And we can now use this
3:383 minutes, 38 secondsoscillation to define the strain produced by the gravitational wave.
3:433 minutes, 43 secondsTo do that, let's look again at our displaced ring of masses. And then we can label deltar as the amplitude of the
3:503 minutes, 50 secondsdisplacement. Then if the radius of the undeformed ring of masses is r, then the strain produced by the gravitational
3:583 minutes, 58 secondswave is roughly the fractional change in radius deltar divided by r. This is the key quantity. The strain tells us the
4:064 minutes, 6 secondsdistortion as a fraction of the original length. If h is large, the stretching and squeezing is large. If h is tiny,
4:134 minutes, 13 secondsthe distortion is incredibly weak. And this is the same basic quantity that LIGO measures. In the ring of masses
4:214 minutes, 21 secondspicture, we write that h is roughly equal to deltar / r. And for ligo, the same idea becomes that h is roughly
4:294 minutes, 29 secondsequal to deltal over l. Where here l is the length of one detector arm and deltal is the tiny change in that length
4:374 minutes, 37 secondscaused by the passing gravitational wave. Now strictly speaking, LIGO measures the difference between the changes in the two arm lengths and the
4:454 minutes, 45 secondsexact relationship depends on the wave's direction and polarization. But for our purposes, the essential idea is simple.
4:524 minutes, 52 secondsA gravitational wave produces a tiny fractional change in length and that fractional change is what we call the strain.

Chapter 4: Stiffness of space-time
7:507 minutes, 50 secondsIf spacetime itself can ripple, do those ripples tell us something about the properties of spacetime itself? And could we assign spacetime something like
7:587 minutes, 58 secondsa stiffness? After all, when we look at mechanical waves here on Earth, the velocity of the wave is determined by the properties of the underlying medium.
8:088 minutes, 8 secondsFor example, for sound waves traveling through a given gas such as air, the velocity depends on the pressure and density of the gas. And if these are
8:168 minutes, 16 secondsknown, the velocity can be in principle predicted. And for a longitudinal wave traveling along a solid rod, the velocity depends on the Young's modulus
8:258 minutes, 25 secondsof the material and its density. Where you can think of the Young's modulus as a measure of how stiff the material is.
8:348 minutes, 34 secondsSo the question we're now going to ask is how stiff is spacetime and can we assign spacetime something like a Young's modulus? Now this might sound
8:448 minutes, 44 secondslike a ridiculous question but it's actually been asked seriously by physicists.
8:498 minutes, 49 secondsAt the end of Kipth Thorne's 2018 reigns lecture exploring the universe with gravitational waves a member of the audience asked Thorne what the Young's
8:588 minutes, 58 secondsmodulus of spaceime was. Thorne referred the question to fellow Nobel laurate Raina Vice who was sitting in the audience.
9:079 minutes, 7 secondsThorne and Vice along with Barry Barish shared the 2017 Nobel Prize in physics for their decisive contributions to the
9:149 minutes, 14 secondsLIGO detector and the observation of gravitational waves. When asked the question, Vice explained that there
9:219 minutes, 21 secondsisn't one single effective stiffness for spacetime. The answer depends on the frequency of the gravitational wave. But at a frequency of around 100 hertz,
9:309 minutes, 30 secondswhich is similar to the frequencies first detected by LIGO, spacetime behaves as though it has a stiffness of roughly 10 ^ of 20 times greater than
9:399 minutes, 39 secondsthat of steel. Now, that's an almost incomprehensibly large number. So, where does it come from? How can we take something like a gravitational wave and
9:489 minutes, 48 secondsuse it to calculate an effective stiffness for spacetime?
9:529 minutes, 52 secondsWell, to answer that question, we first need to understand how physicists actually measure the stiffness of a material.
10:0010 minutesImagine that we clamp one end of a steel rod and pull on the other end with a force F. The rod stretches slightly. If we then double the force, it stretches
10:0810 minutes, 8 secondsby roughly twice as much. If we treble the force, it stretches by roughly three times as much and so on. For small defamations, this relationship is
10:1710 minutes, 17 secondsbeautifully simple. The extension is proportional to the applied force. This is the well-known Hook's law. Okay. So
10:2510 minutes, 25 secondsnow suppose I tell you that I stretched a rod using a force of 1,000 Ntons. Is that a lot or a little? Well, it
10:3310 minutes, 33 secondsdepends. You see, if a rod has a cross-sectional area of 1 square meter, that force is spread over a huge area.
10:4010 minutes, 40 secondsBut if the rod has a cross-sectional area of just one square millimeter, exactly the same force is concentrated into a tiny region. Clearly, what matters is not simply the force itself.
10:5110 minutes, 51 secondsIt's the force per unit area. So, let's now consider our metal rod with cross-sectional area A. If we imagine
10:5910 minutes, 59 secondsthat a force F is applied, then we can define the stress sigma as being equal to the force per unit area. And because
11:0711 minutes, 7 secondsthe units of force are newtons and the units of area are me squared, it follows that stress has units of newtons per
11:1411 minutes, 14 secondsmeter squared. and 1 Newton per meter squared is often called 1 Pascal.
11:2111 minutes, 21 secondsOkay. So now suppose that we have two rods of different lengths made from different materials. If both stretch by 1 mm when a force is applied then
11:2911 minutes, 29 secondsclearly the relative defamation is different for the two rods and so the absolute change in length isn't the most
11:3611 minutes, 36 secondsuseful quantity. Instead we define the strain which we've already encountered.
11:4311 minutes, 43 secondsIf the original length of the rod is L and when the force F is applied the rod extends by an amount delta L, then we
11:4911 minutes, 49 secondscan define the strain as the fractional change in its length. epsilon equals delta L / L. Notice that the strain has
11:5711 minutes, 57 secondsno units. It's simply a ratio. Now we can compare completely different materials on equal footing. Stress tells
12:0412 minutes, 4 secondsus how hard we're pulling per unit area and strain tells us how much the material deforms.
12:1112 minutes, 11 secondsAnd the remarkable thing is that for many materials undergoing small defamations, these quantities are proportional. Doubling the stress
12:1812 minutes, 18 secondsdoubles the strain. Having the stress halves the strain. And if we plot stress against strain, the gradient of the graph gives what is known as the Young's
12:2612 minutes, 26 secondsmodulus of the material. We can therefore define the Young's modulus as Y= sigma / epsilon. In other words, the
12:3512 minutes, 35 secondsYoung's modulus tells us how much stress is required to produce a given strain.
12:4012 minutes, 40 secondsAnd since strain has no units, it follows that the units of Young's modulus are the same as the units of stress, namely Pascals.
12:5012 minutes, 50 secondsAnd to get a sense of the kind of numbers involved, soft rubber can have a Young's modulus of around 10 6 pascals.
12:5712 minutes, 57 secondsSteel is roughly 10 11 pascals and diamond is close to 10 12 pascals. So now we have a quantitative measure of
13:0613 minutes, 6 secondsstiffness. But how can we apply this idea to spacetime itself? When LIGO detected a passing gravitational wave,
Chapter 5: Elastic energy models

Chapter 7: Fracture mechanics analogy
24:5724 minutes, 57 secondsIf we are already entertaining the idea that spacetime has something like stiffness, could it also have something like a breaking point? Well, to answer
25:0625 minutes, 6 secondsthat, we need to borrow a tool from a completely different corner of physics.
25:1025 minutes, 10 secondsFracture mechanics. This is the physics of cracks growing in materials. And that's what makes this so fun. We're
25:1825 minutes, 18 secondsgoing to take an idea normally used to understand why glass shatters and ask what happens if we apply it to spaceime itself.
25:2725 minutes, 27 secondsAnd this idea is not plucked out of thin air. A recent paper called breaking spacetime does exactly this as a fun speculative pedagogical thought
25:3525 minutes, 35 secondsexperiment. It borrows the physics of brittle fracture and applies it to spacetime as if spacetime were a brittle material. Not because spacetime
25:4425 minutes, 44 secondsliterally is one, but because the analogy leads somewhere very interesting. To understand that analogy, we first need to understand how brittle
25:5325 minutes, 53 secondsmaterials actually break. And the key idea was first worked out by the British engineer Alan Griffith in 1921.
26:0226 minutes, 2 secondsHe realized that tiny cracks were the key to understanding why materials break. So imagine pulling on a piece of
26:0926 minutes, 9 secondsglass containing a microscopic crack. As the crack grows, two things happen. On the one hand, creating the new crack
26:1826 minutes, 18 secondssurface costs energy. Atomic bonds have to be broken and new surfaces have to be created. The energy required to create
26:2626 minutes, 26 secondsone square meter of a new surface is called the surface energy and is usually denoted gamma subscript S. The larger
26:3526 minutes, 35 secondsthe new surface you create, the more energy is required. So crack growth costs energy. But something else happens
26:4426 minutes, 44 secondsat exactly the same time. You see the material surrounding the crack was storing elastic energy. As the crack
26:5026 minutes, 50 secondsgrows, some of that stored energy is released. And so crack growth also releases energy. And these two effects
26:5926 minutes, 59 secondscompete with one another. If creating the new crack surface costs more energy than is released, then the crack stops growing.
27:0827 minutes, 8 secondsBut if extending the crack releases more energy than it costs to create the new surfaces, then the crack becomes unstable. It grows by itself and the material fractures.
27:1827 minutes, 18 secondsGriffith showed that for a simple brittle material, this balance leads to an expression for the critical stress at which fracture occurs. Ignoring
27:2627 minutes, 26 secondsnumerical constants that depend on the exact geometry of the crack, the result is that sigma c ^ 2 scales as y * gamma
27:3527 minutes, 35 secondssubscript s divided by d, where sigma subscript c is the critical stress at which the fracture occurs, y is the
27:4327 minutes, 43 secondsyoung's modulus, gamma is the surface energy, and d is the length of the crack. Okay, so let's now pause for a
27:5027 minutes, 50 secondsmoment and interpret this equation. It's saying that a material is harder to break if its surface energy is large.
27:5827 minutes, 58 secondsThat makes sense. Breaking stronger atomic bonds requires more energy. The critical stress is also larger if the
28:0528 minutes, 5 secondsmaterial is stiffer, aka has a higher Young's modulus. Again, that makes sense. A stiffer material stores more elastic energy for a given defamation.
28:1528 minutes, 15 secondsFinally, notice the dependence on the crack length. Long cracks are dangerous.
28:2228 minutes, 22 secondsThe larger the crack already is, the smaller the stress required for it to continue growing. This is why engineers
28:2928 minutes, 29 secondsworry so much about tiny defects inside aircraft wings or bridge supports.
28:3428 minutes, 34 secondsMaterials fail because no material is perfectly free of defects. But now comes
28:4128 minutes, 41 secondsthe conceptual leap. Suppose we imagine applying this equation to spacetime itself and treating it as a brittle
28:4928 minutes, 49 secondsfabric. In that case, what would Griffith's equation become?
28:5428 minutes, 54 secondsWell, we immediately face three questions. First, what should we use for the Young's modulus? Second, what should
29:0229 minutes, 2 secondswe use for the crack length? And finally, what should we use for the surface energy?
29:0829 minutes, 8 secondsWell, in answer to the first question, we've already introduced an effective Young's modulus for spaceime using gravitational wave data. So, we can use
29:1729 minutes, 17 secondsthis quantity for the crack length. we use the smallest meaningful distance scale. Now in an ordinary material, this
29:2529 minutes, 25 secondswould be limited by its microscopic structure below which the idea of a continuous solid breaks down. By analogy
29:3229 minutes, 32 secondsfor spacetime, we're going to use the plank length, the natural scale at which its description as a smooth continuum is
29:4129 minutes, 41 secondsexpected to fail. By choosing the smallest physically meaningful length scale, we will arrive at an estimate for
29:4929 minutes, 49 secondsthe maximum possible fracture stress within this model. The plank length itself can be estimated by combining the
29:5729 minutes, 57 secondsreduced plank constant, Newton's gravitational constant and the speed of light in such a way as to have an overall dimension of length. And if you
30:0630 minutes, 6 secondsplug in the values of the constants, you find a length of order 10us 35 m. And finally, the surface energy of spacetime
30:1530 minutes, 15 secondswithin this framework is an unknown property. So instead of trying to calculate the critical stress directly,
30:2230 minutes, 22 secondswe're going to ask a slightly different question. How much elastic energy would be stored inside a single planksiz cube
30:3030 minutes, 30 secondsof space just as it reaches the point of fracture? To answer that, we begin with the elastic energy density. Earlier we
30:3830 minutes, 38 secondsshowed that the energy stored per unit volume of an elastic material is equal to half sigma x* epsilon. And then if we
30:4630 minutes, 46 secondsuse the definition of young's modulus, we can rewrite the energy density as sigma^ 2 / 2 y. The volume of our tiny
30:5630 minutes, 56 secondsplanksiz cube is equal to the plank length cubed.
31:0131 minutes, 1 secondAnd so we now have an expression for the energy density and an expression for the volume. And therefore the total elastic
31:0831 minutes, 8 secondsenergy stored in the cube will be equal to the energy density multiplied by the volume. And if we sub in our two
31:1631 minutes, 16 secondsprevious expressions, we find the following result. Now we're interested in the instant just before fracture
31:2331 minutes, 23 secondsbegins. And that means the stress has reached the critical stress value. And so we can write that sigma is equal to
31:3131 minutes, 31 secondssigma subscript c. And so our expression then takes the following form.
31:3731 minutes, 37 secondsAnd if you recall the Griffith criteria stated that sigma c^ 2 scaled like y * gamma / d. And since we're assuming the
31:4631 minutes, 46 secondssmallest possible crack length equal to the plank length, we can write this as y * gamma / l subscript p. And then if we
31:5631 minutes, 56 secondssubstitute this into the elastic energy expression, we find the following result.
32:0232 minutes, 2 secondsWe now notice that something rather elegant happens. The Young's modulus completely cancels and we end up with half gamma * the plank length squared.
32:1132 minutes, 11 secondsAnd then since we're only interested in order of magnitude, we can write the elastic energy as roughly equal to gamma
32:1832 minutes, 18 seconds* the plank length squared. Now this is a remarkably simple result. It says that the elastic energy stored in a planksiz
32:2632 minutes, 26 secondscube of space at the onset of fracture is simply the hypothetical surface energy per unit area multiplied by the
32:3532 minutes, 35 secondsarea of one face of the cube. Notice something else. The Young's modulus has disappeared entirely. At first, that
32:4232 minutes, 42 secondsmight seem surprising. After all, we've spent most of the video calculating the effective stiffness of spaceime. But it
32:5032 minutes, 50 secondsactually makes physical sense. You see, for the same strain, a stiffer material stores more elastic energy. However,
32:5832 minutes, 58 secondsthat same stiffness also raises the stress required to make a crack grow.
33:0333 minutes, 3 secondsAnd those two effects exactly compensate one another. So, the elastic energy at the onset of fracture no longer depends
33:1133 minutes, 11 secondsexplicitly on the stiffness. Everything has now been reduced to a single unknown quantity, gamma subscript s. But our equation alone cannot tell us its value.
33:2533 minutes, 25 secondsHowever, we can turn the problem around and ask what is the greatest amount of energy that could plausibly be
33:3233 minutes, 32 secondsconcentrated inside a plank-sized region of spaceime.
Chapter 8: Planck scale limits
33:3733 minutes, 37 secondsWell, the natural scale is the plank energy. If too much energy is squeezed into too small a region, its gravitational field becomes so strong
33:4633 minutes, 46 secondsthat a horizon can form. And when the size of that region approaches the plank length, the corresponding gravitational collapse energy is of order the plank
33:5533 minutes, 55 secondsenergy. Now, one way of reaching this scale is by considering the concept of power.
34:0234 minutes, 2 secondsPower as you probably know is simply energy transferred per unit time which we can write as P= E over T. Now suppose
34:1134 minutes, 11 secondsthere exists some fundamental maximum power allowed by nature. So if we can estimate the greatest physically
34:2034 minutes, 20 secondsmeaningful power and determine how much energy it could deliver over the natural time scale of a planksiz region, we
34:2634 minutes, 26 secondsobtain an estimate of the maximum energy available on that scale. So can we estimate this maximum power? Well, the
34:3534 minutes, 35 secondsanswer is yes. And for those interested, I've made an entire video explaining how to do this. The link should be at the top of the screen. But for the purposes
34:4334 minutes, 43 secondsof this video, we will simply motivate the result by using dimensional analysis along with the speed of light and
34:5134 minutes, 51 secondsNewton's gravitational constant. The maximum power scale can be expressed as C ^ 5 / G. This has the units of power
35:0135 minutes, 1 secondand this is known as the plank power and is often discussed as a possible maximum luminosity with a value of order 10 ^ 52
35:1135 minutes, 11 secondswatt which is equivalent to 10 ^ 52 jew/s a truly enormous incomprehensible power.
35:2135 minutes, 21 secondsBut for how long could this power act within a planksiz region? Well, the natural time scale is the time taken for light to travel across one plank length.
35:3235 minutes, 32 secondsSince time equals distance divided by speed, we would write this time as the plank length divided by the speed of light. And this is known as the plank time.
35:4235 minutes, 42 secondsIt then follows that the maximum energy that could be delivered on this scale will be equal to the plank power multiplied by the plank time.
35:5235 minutes, 52 secondsAnd if we then sub in the explicit expressions for the plank power and the plank time and then simplify, we end up with the following expression. And this is the plank energy.
36:0336 minutes, 3 secondsSo let's pause here a moment and take stock within this argument. This expression gives us the natural upper energy scale that could be deposited
36:1036 minutes, 10 secondsinto a plank size region during a plank timel long burst. Now compare that with the elastic energy stored in that same
36:1836 minutes, 18 secondsplanksiz region at the onset of fracture which we derived earlier. And so now if spaceime is going to break the energy
36:2636 minutes, 26 secondsdelivered must be at least as large as the elastic energy at the onset of fracture. So the fracture condition is
36:3336 minutes, 33 secondssimply that the maximum energy is greater than or equal to e subscript e.
36:3836 minutes, 38 secondsAnd then if we substitute both expressions into the inequality and then simplify, we end up with the following condition.
36:4736 minutes, 47 secondsAnd this is the central inequality of the argument. Now, at first it might not look like much, but we can gain some insight by looking at the units on the
36:5636 minutes, 56 secondsleft hand side. The units of surface energy are jewels per square meter and the units of plank length are clearly meters. And so the units of the entire
37:0437 minutes, 4 secondsleft hand side are jewels per meter which we recognize as being equivalent to newtons which are the units of force.
37:1237 minutes, 12 secondsSo the left hand side is a force. More specifically it is the characteristic force associated with breaking a
37:2037 minutes, 20 secondsplanksiz patch of spaceime if spacetime had something like a surface energy. And the inequality says that this tearing force cannot be larger than C ^ 4 / G.
37:3337 minutes, 33 secondsBut this combination of constants already has a name. It is the plank force. Numerically it has a value of
37:4137 minutes, 41 secondsaround 10 44 newtons. The plank force is closely related to an idea in general relativity called the maximum force
37:4937 minutes, 49 secondsconjecture or the maximum tension principle. The idea is that classical general relativity may not allow arbitrarily large forces or tensions.
38:0038 minutesOne motivation is that attempts to concentrate too much energy into too small a region can instead lead to horizon formation and black hole
38:0938 minutes, 9 secondscreation. Essentially, the geometry of spaceime itself intervenes. So the plank force is sometimes discussed as a
38:1738 minutes, 17 secondsfundamental limiting scale in general relativity. A bit like the speed of light is a fundamental limiting speed in special relativity.
38:2838 minutes, 28 secondsAnd so our inequality can be read as follows. If we define gamma times the plank length as the characteristic force
38:3638 minutes, 36 secondsscale associated with tearing a planksiz region of spaceime, then that force must not exceed the plank force scale if
38:4538 minutes, 45 secondsfracture is to be energetically possible. But notice what this does and does not tell us. It gives us an upper
38:5338 minutes, 53 secondsbound, but it does not tell us the actual tearing force of spacetime or how far below the plank force it might be.
39:0139 minutes, 1 secondIt could be close to this limit or it could be many orders of magnitude smaller.
39:0739 minutes, 7 secondsWe can nevertheless consider the most extreme case in which the bound is saturated. In other words, we can
39:1439 minutes, 14 secondsconsider when gamma time plank length is roughly equal to c ^ 4 / g. If we then
39:2139 minutes, 21 secondsrearrange for the surface energy, we find the following result. And if we then sub in the values, we obtain a
39:2739 minutes, 27 secondsvalue of approximately 10 79 per meter squared. This number is almost impossible to comprehend. For reference,
39:3739 minutes, 37 secondsthe surface energy of glass is only a few jewels per square meter. And for most metals, it is also roughly of order
39:4439 minutes, 44 seconds1 to 10 jewels per meter squared. So what we're seeing here is an extreme upper limit scale for the surface energy
39:5139 minutes, 51 secondsof spacetime that is almost 80 orders of magnitude larger than the corresponding values for ordinary materials. But it's important to remember what this means.
40:0240 minutes, 2 secondsThe argument does not tell us how resistant spacetime actually is to fracture. What it tells us is that the
40:0940 minutes, 9 secondsnatural upper scale for any such tearing force is essentially the plank force. If the fracture analogy has any meaning at
40:1740 minutes, 17 secondsall, then the extreme limiting case corresponds to spaceime being almost unimaginably resistant to being torn
40:2440 minutes, 24 secondsapart. But now there is one final loose end to tie up. You see earlier gravitational waves gave us an effective
40:3240 minutes, 32 secondsYoung's modulus for spacetime of order C ^ 2 F^² / G and if you recall this came from the elastic side of the analogy.
40:4240 minutes, 42 secondsThen the fracture argument gave us a hypothetical maximum surface energy scale of order 10 79 JW per meter
40:5140 minutes, 51 secondssquared and this came from the breaking side of the analogy.
40:5540 minutes, 55 secondsSo our next question is simple. Can we somehow connect the surface energy we just estimated with the Young's modulus
41:0341 minutes, 3 secondsvalue we calculated earlier? At first, it looks like we can't because they're not the same kind of quantity. The
41:1041 minutes, 10 secondsYoung's modulus has units of Pascals which are equal to Newtons per meter squared. And since 1 Newton is equal to
41:1841 minutes, 18 seconds1 JW per meter, we can also write the units of Young's modulus as Jew per meter cubed. So we see Young's modulus has the same units as an energy density.
41:2941 minutes, 29 secondsOn the other hand, surface energy has units of jewels per meter squared. It's an energy per area scale. So we cannot
41:3841 minutes, 38 secondscompare the Young's modulus directly with the surface energy. So is it possible to somehow equate these two scales?
41:4741 minutes, 47 secondsWell, if you think about it, to turn an energy per volume into an energy per area, we need to multiply by a length.
41:5541 minutes, 55 secondsAnd because we're asking about plank scale fracture, the natural length to use is the plank length. In other words,
42:0242 minutes, 2 secondsif we take the effective Young's modulus and multiply by the plank length, we obtain a comparable energy per area
42:1042 minutes, 10 secondsscale. And we can then ask at what frequency this becomes comparable with the surface energy scale.
42:1842 minutes, 18 secondsNow if you recall we found earlier that the effective Young's modulus takes the following form. So let's now sub this
42:2542 minutes, 25 secondsinto our new relation. And then when we do this we find the following expression. So now we can finally ask
42:3142 minutes, 31 secondsour question. What is the frequency at which the two scales become comparable?
42:3742 minutes, 37 secondsTo answer this question, we simply rearrange for frequency and we find that it scales as the square root of gamma * g / c ^ 2 l.
42:4842 minutes, 48 secondsAnd then if we take the expression for the surface energy that we derived earlier and sub this in we get the following expression. And then if we
42:5642 minutes, 56 secondssimplify we end up with c / lp. Now if you recall the plank time was equal to
43:0243 minutes, 2 secondslp / c. And so we see that the frequency that naturally emerges is equal to 1 over the plank time which is none other
43:1143 minutes, 11 secondsthan the plank frequency which is of order 10 ^ 43 hertz. So if we ask when the young's modulus scale from
43:2043 minutes, 20 secondsgravitational waves becomes comparable with the surface energy scale from fracture mechanics the answer is only
43:2743 minutes, 27 secondswhen the disturbance frequency is comparable to the plank frequency.
43:3143 minutes, 31 secondsOrdinary gravitational waves such as those detected by LIGO are nowhere close to this scale.
Chapter 9: Conclusion
43:3943 minutes, 39 secondsSo can spacetime actually break? Well, the honest answer is that we don't know.
43:4443 minutes, 44 secondsGeneral relativity does not describe spacetime as a brittle material or as a true fabric. It contains no atoms, no
43:5243 minutes, 52 secondschemical bonds, no crystal latises, and no literal Young's modulus. The entire fracture model is an analogy, but it's a
44:0144 minutes, 1 secondrevealing analogy because when we push it as far as it can reasonably go, the different parts of the analogy meet
44:0844 minutes, 8 secondsconsistently at the plank scale. And the plank scale is the very place where physicists expect Einstein's smooth
44:1644 minutes, 16 secondsdescription of spacetime to require something deeper. So if spacetime can break, we do not yet know what that
44:2544 minutes, 25 secondswould mean. But this argument suggests where we might need to look. And that is where we will end today. So, thank you
44:3344 minutes, 33 secondsfor listening and until next time, goodbye.
